# Insights of Tech Report

A running index of system cards and technical reports that are out in the world.
For each one: **the report's own load-bearing findings and numbers** (fetched and
independently fact-checked before they went up), then **my read of it** in two
dimensions — what I'd look at from where I sit, and where it meets the questions in
my research notes above. Titles are toggles.


<details>
<summary><strong>Denser ≠ Better: Limits of On-Policy Self-Distillation for Continual Post-Training</strong> · CAS (HKISI / Institute of Automation) + UCAS + NJUST, July 2026</summary>

*A 33-page arXiv preprint (2607.01763v1, submitted 2 Jul 2026; cs.LG, cross-listed cs.CL) asking whether
on-policy self-distillation is a safe objective for continual post-training. Its test case is SDPO —
where one model is both student and privileged-context teacher and **every generated token becomes a
distillation target** — compared against sequence-level GRPO. It varies the density and stability of the
token-level signal (teacher EMA rate, chain-of-thought distillation on/off, random token masking) on
Qwen3-4B across a four-domain sequence of Math, Science, ToolUse and Coding, with Olmo-3-7B used only for
single-domain Math comparisons. The headline claim is negative: dense self-distillation is a fast
specializer but a fragile continual-learning signal that drifts further in parameter and response space,
forgets more than GRPO, and can collapse outright.*

**From the report**

> Core method: SDPO uses the same model as teacher and student — the student sees only the prompt while the teacher is conditioned on privileged context (the regeneration prompt supplies a "Correct solution: {previously correct solution}") — and training minimizes the generalized Jensen–Shannon divergence between the student and stopgrad(teacher) distributions **at every generated token**, rather than one reward per sequence. — §2.3, App. C.4
>
> Training recipe: base models Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507, Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507, Olmo-3-7B-Instruct-DPO and Olmo-3-7B-Think-DPO; training data DAPO-Math-17k (Math), SciKnowEval (Science), ToolAlpaca (ToolUse) and Dolci-Think-RL-7B (Coding); 50 training steps (20 for Coding), 8 rollouts at temperature 1.0, max response length 14,336, distillation Top-K 100, LR 1e-5 for SDPO versus 1e-6 for GRPO, and GRPO's KL coefficient set to 0.0. — App. C.1, Table C.2
>
> Eval setup: ten columns scored as Acc@8 — the in-distribution held-out splits of SciKnowEval, ToolAlpaca and Dolci-Think-RL-7B, plus AIME (in-distribution for Math) and the OOD set Math500, GPQA, BFCLv4, LCBv6, ZebraLogicBench and MMLU-Redux 2.0. Forgetting is measured against the base model as a relative change split into gain and forgetting terms. — Table C.1, App. A
>
> Headline (specialization): under single-domain Math training SDPO with a frozen teacher is the strongest specialist, raising AIME from 32.71% to 56.42% and Math500 from 73.85% to 84.29% against GRPO's 44.67% on AIME; Coding-SDPO lifts LCBv6 from 46.73% to 60.12% (the in-text "13.39%" is an absolute point difference, not a relative gain). — §4.1, Table 2
>
> Headline (continual reversal): over the sequence Math → Science → ToolUse → Coding, the 10-benchmark average moves from a 61.60 base to **69.90 for GRPO**, but falls to 52.55 for SDPO with a frozen teacher, 42.84 for SDPO with CoT distilled, and 0.02 for SDPO with a 5% EMA teacher. — Table D.6
>
> Headline (collapse): SDPO-frozen's ToolUse score is 37.35% right after that stage but 9.93% after the full sequence (↓80% versus base); the 5% EMA variant collapses at the third stage to a 0.05 ten-benchmark average and stays there (0.02 after the final stage), scored ↓100% against base on every category — the visible artifact being endlessly repeated "\boxed" tokens and JS divergence dropping to zero within the first few steps of ToolUse training. — §4.2, §5.2, Table 3
>
> Mechanism: the drift ordering SDPO-CoT > SDPO-5% > SDPO-frozen > GRPO holds across four SVD-based weight metrics (normalized spectral shift, principal-angle rotation, principal-mask overlap, principal-mask update sparsity), with GRPO's principal-mask overlap staying near 99.9%, and is mirrored in response space by lower, heavier-lower-tailed cosine similarities of rollout embeddings. Theorem 1 formalizes it as excess KL drift, since SDPO's per-token target is the teacher projection rather than the KL-minimal sequence-reward kernel. — §5.1, §5.3
>
> Caveats the paper states: reordering data or masking tokens "only partially mitigates this collapse" — random masking of 25/50/75% of tokens still leaves stage-3 averages of 1.85 / 15.18 / 23.51 — and a footnote concedes its in-distribution versus out-of-distribution labels "are pragmatic labels, not strict measures of inter-dataset distance". The paper reports no random seeds and no multi-run variance for any accuracy table; the only 95% confidence intervals are across model layers in the drift figures. — §5.2, Tables D.5–D.6, App. C.1

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3.2 with Table D.2 first, because the split it finds is exactly the split in my own trajectory types — CoT distillation helps short schema-constrained ToolUse traces but drives AIME to 8.13% on long Math rationales, which says token-level distillation is safe for tool-call structure and dangerous for long reasoning traces, not uniformly one or the other. The paper's own account is token reliability: long rationales are "underdetermined and often contain redundant self-checks, uncertain interpretations, formatting habits, or incorrect intermediate claims" weakly tied to final correctness, while tool traces are short and schema-constrained. §5.3's Theorem 1 is the cleanest statement I have seen of why on-policy sampling is not the same as a conservative update: sampling controls *where* supervision lands, but the teacher projection controls *which* distribution is targeted at each history, and the gap between that projection and the KL-minimal sequence-reward kernel is a non-negative excess-KL term that predicts the extra forgetting. The four SVD weight metrics in §5.1 are worth lifting on their own — they run on checkpoints alone, so I could get a forgetting early-warning between stages without spending any eval budget. And I would go straight to the masking rows of Table D.6, since "just gate the token-level signal" is the obvious first repair and the paper shows it only partially works, so gating has to be selective rather than random.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Post-cutoff distillation** — the most direct hit, and it is a controlled negative result on exactly that note's shape. My note proposes on-policy distillation from a privileged-context teacher with a per-token reward; Theorem 1 says a teacher conditioned on augmented context targets a distribution that is *not* the KL-minimal one and therefore carries excess KL drift, and the finding that random token masking only partially mitigates collapse speaks straight to my open question about gating the reward to genuinely new content. This is the third independent result this month pointing the same way — a dense self-computed signal helps only inside a narrow band and hurts outside it. **Over-reflection in search agents** — a mechanism for why confirm-then-keep-searching would *survive* distillation: §3.2 finds long rationales carry redundant self-checks and formatting habits weakly tied to correctness, and §5.2 names the confirmation-bias loop in which the student emits a pattern, the student-derived teacher assigns it token-level support, and the next update makes it more likely. That is a concrete amplification mechanism for a behaviour that changes nothing. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a caution on the shared premise of extracting more supervision per expensive rollout: SDPO harvests one target per generated token instead of one scalar per sequence, and this paper prices that extra signal, buying faster specialization (AIME 32.71% → 56.42%) while pushing the four-stage average from 69.90 under GRPO down to 52.55, or to 0.02 with a fast teacher. **AgentPlanet** — connects only on the reward-channel-integrity half: the failure here is a supervision signal that is not independent of the policy it grades, since the teacher *is* the student under privileged context. The world-authoring role is a stretch — no environment synthesis appears.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the restart-and-freeze schedule with its matching rule γ ≈ 1 − (1−α)^k is a near-free change to any EMA-teacher setup — periodically refresh the teacher from the student then hold it fixed instead of updating every step — and it recovered +6.50 average points at α=5% (65.34 → 71.84) while a hard restart also beat the frozen teacher outright (71.88 → 72.94). That separates "teacher is stale" from "teacher is a moving target" as two distinct failure modes I would otherwise have conflated. The open question the paper leaves is whether its drift metrics are predictive enough to become an *online control signal* rather than a post-hoc diagnostic: the ordering matches the eventual forgetting, and JS divergence collapsing to zero within a few steps of a stage change is an unmistakable early tell, but nobody tests whether thresholding either one would stop a run before it collapses.

[Source (arXiv 2607.01763)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01763) · [code](https://github.com/Moenupa/SDPO-CL)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Escaping the Self-Confirmation Trap: An Execute-Distill-Verify Paradigm for Agentic Experience Learning</strong> · Zhejiang U. + Tsinghua SIGS (+ NPU / USTC / SJTU), June 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2606.24428v1, submitted 23 Jun 2026; cs.CL, 28 pages) naming a failure mode in agent
memory systems — the "Self-Confirmation Trap", where an agent that both executes a task and judges its own
trajectory banks wrong-but-internally-coherent runs as successful experience, which then compounds through
retrieval and reuse — and proposing EDV, an Execute-Distill-Verify scaffolding framework that splits those
roles across different models. Two sampled models execute in parallel, a third distills candidate
experience by cross-trajectory comparison, and the executors then vote under a default-reject policy. No
model weights are updated anywhere; the reported headline is an average Pass@1 of 86.6 on τ²-bench against
83.5 for a routing baseline and 76.4–79.6 for single models without memory.*

**From the report**

> The trap is formalized, not just named: with correctness c(τ) and a binary self-approval v(τ), a single-agent loop uses the same policy for execution and evaluation, so "execution errors and evaluation failures [are] statistically dependent" and P(v=1 | c=0) "is substantially elevated" — "this erroneous endorsement of flawed trajectories constitutes the core of the Self-Confirmation Trap". — §3.1
>
> The verifier is an LLM auditor, not a deterministic checker or a trained reward model: verification is done by the original execution agents, each returning a binary vote — unanimous approval writes to a shared memory bank, subset approval to that agent's private bank, everything else is discarded. The Verify prompt frames the model as "an exceptionally strict memory audit expert" with a "Default stance: reject" and four simultaneous gates (Groundedness, Specificity, High Utility, Zero Redundancy), with one near-deterministic sub-check: "Ensure all function names/arguments strictly match <Tools> schema". — §4.4, §B.2
>
> There is no gradient training anywhere; EDV is pure inference-time scaffolding over frozen off-the-shelf models. Config: 2 reasoning models with 4 candidate trajectories per step, 8,192 max output tokens, stage-wise temperatures (Execute, Distill, Verify) = (1.0, 1.0, 0.0), max 5 memory items per step stored as JSON, retrieval by embedding cosine similarity with asymmetric thresholds 0.80 (public) / 0.85 (private), and "a minimalist incremental update strategy: new memory are strictly appended to the repository without clustering, deduplication, or pruning". — §C.1–C.3
>
> Headline results: on τ²-bench, EDV reaches average Pass@1 86.6 (AIRLINE 72.0 / RETAIL 88.6 / TELECOM 99.1) against Router 83.5, LLM-as-Judge 81.5, best single-model memory baseline 81.9, and no-memory single models at 76.4–79.6; on a second multi-turn suite, overall 58.10 against Router 55.96 and best no-memory 52.34. — §5.2, Tables 1 and 3
>
> The cost of contaminated memory is measured by injection: taking the single-agent memory baseline and injecting "erroneous yet internally coherent experiences (e.g., incorrect payment rules) accounting for 10% of the total memory volume" into τ²-bench RETAIL drops Pass@1 "sharply from 82.5 to 77.2" — a 5.3-point loss for a 10% dose. — §5.4
>
> The progressive ablation isolates which decoupling matters, on τ²-bench RETAIL Pass@1: basic single-agent 83.3 → **single-agent + Self-Verify 83.2** (self-verification is slightly *worse* than no verification at all) → single-agent + independent external verifier 84.5 → multi-agent with executor self-distill 85.9 → multi-agent + third-party distill 87.1 → full EDV 88.6. The paper's reading: "a single model tends to endorse its own erroneous yet self-consistent reasoning", and "trajectory diversity is a necessary prerequisite for higher-quality memory". — §5.5.1, Table 4
>
> Memory quality and its downstream effect: a human audit of items actually written to the bank moves Groundedness/Correctness 3.72 → 4.41, Noise/Hallucination 1.21 → 0.63 and Potential Harm if Reused 1.08 → 0.51 on a 5-point scale; the memory-scaling curve then diverges — both methods start at 0.781 Pass@1 with zero retrieved memories, EDV rises monotonically to 0.886 at 3 memories while the single-agent baseline peaks at 1 memory (0.825) and *degrades* to 0.793 at 3. Better memory also cuts cost: EDV "reduces average inference token consumption by 24.5%" versus that baseline while scoring higher. — §5.3, §5.6, §A.2
>
> The paper states three limitations of its own consensus design: "Risk of Consensus Bias; if heterogeneous agents share a specific failure mode, the consensus mechanism might inadvertently validate noise"; "Interference from Low-Quality Agents, where a significantly underperforming agent can obstruct consensus"; and "Attribution Difficulty, as the multi-turn interplay between critics and executors makes pinpointing the root cause of failures significantly harder than in linear systems". Note also that on the web-agent benchmark's task-level success rate EDV is never the sole best — beaten on two splits and only tying on the third — while step-level metrics move a lot. — §G, Table 2

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the row I actually care about is Table 4 line 2 — single-agent + Self-Verify scores 83.2 against 83.3 for no verification at all. That is the cleanest published evidence I have seen that a model auditing its own trajectory contributes literally nothing, and the follow-on row (independent verifier on a single trajectory, +1.2 only) says the fix is not an independent seat but a **comparison set**: the verifier needs sibling trajectories to have anything to disagree with. That reframes verifier design from "who judges" to "what does the judge get to compare against". §5.4's contamination protocol is the experiment shape I want as a standing instrument — inject k% wrong-but-internally-coherent items into a store and read the task-score drop — because it converts "bad data is bad" into a dose-response number. §B.2 read against §4.4 is where I check whether the verifier is real: it is a prompted judgement, not a reward model or an environment check, with one exception worth lifting — the schema-alignment gate requiring every referenced function name, argument and return field to exist in the provided tool spec, which is deterministic, cheap, and catches the protocol-hallucination class. And the web-agent table is my sanity check on the headline: the harness caps task success by top-10 retrieval truncation, and inside that ceiling the step-level metrics move while the task-level metric barely does — the standard signature of gains that live in per-step grounding rather than in completing the task.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the tightest mechanism link. Their first memory class is literally "Breaking Inertia & Dynamic Adaptation", and its worked example is a trajectory where the top-rated result was already found at step 6 and the agent kept adjusting filters; the distilled memory is "Stop filtering and select the result when criteria are met." That is my confirm-then-keep-searching pattern, and their fix is to encode the stop rule as a *retrievable text memory* rather than as a state-conditioned stop/pivot policy — with the 24.5% token reduction as the efficiency payoff of exactly that stopping behaviour. It is a clean point of comparison for whether retrieval-time stop rules can substitute for stop/pivot RL. **AgentPlanet** — the direct counter-thesis to my factorization, which is why it is useful. My note has one model playing all three roles; §3.1 argues that coupling roles inside one policy is precisely what makes P(v=1 | c=0) elevated, because execution errors and evaluation failures become statistically dependent. Their own §G limitation is my anti-Goodhart question restated — if heterogeneous agents share a failure mode, consensus validates noise — i.e. role separation buys independence only to the extent the models' error modes are actually decorrelated, which they never measure. That is the sharpest available argument that the reward-channel-integrity leg needs a *decorrelation test* rather than a role count. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — they generate 4 candidate trajectories per step across two models, then discard all but one plus 1–3 distilled sentences, with the bank strictly append-only. So the entire rollout budget compresses into retrievable text and zero gradient steps, and their scaling curve — reuse value monotone for EDV up to 3 retrieved memories but peaking at 1 and then degrading for the single-agent baseline — is the text-memory analogue of a replay buffer going stale, arguing the reuse channel's value is gated by admission quality, not buffer size. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a stretch, since nothing distills into weights, but the gating logic maps: their Verify gate rejects any candidate that is common sense, that merely summarizes the process, or that is "already covered by the <AgentPolicy> rules" — a rule-level version of gating a reward to knowledge the student could not already have had, implemented as a prompted rejection over text before storage rather than a per-token reward.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the §5.4 injection protocol as a reusable audit instrument rather than a one-off result — plant k% wrong-but-internally-coherent items in a memory or trajectory store and read the task-score drop. Their single 10% dose cost 5.3 Pass@1 points; I would run it as a curve (2/5/10/20%) to get an actual contamination-tolerance threshold, which is the number the paper never produces. The open question their design leaves: the verification group *is* the execution group, so approval remains executor-sourced, just aggregated. They do test single independent verifiers (84.5 and 87.1, both below EDV's 88.6 executor-consensus), but never a multi-agent consensus group disjoint from the executors, and never measure error correlation across the three pool models — so §G's conceded shared-failure-mode risk stays unquantified. That decorrelation measurement is the experiment I would want before believing the mechanism generalizes to a pool of *similar* models.

[Source (arXiv 2606.24428)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24428) · [code](https://github.com/shidingz/EDV)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Self-Play Only Evolves When Self-Synthetic Pipeline Ensures Learnable Information Gain</strong> · King's College London + The Alan Turing Institute, February 2026</summary>

*An ICML 2026 position-track paper (arXiv 2603.02218; v1 10 Feb 2026, v2 15 May 2026; 22-page PDF)
arguing that self-evolving LLM loops plateau or collapse not because reward design is bad but because the
self-synthesised data stream stops carrying new **learnable information** for a bounded observer. It
formalises learnable information as "epiplexity" — the model-description term of a minimum-description-length
objective optimised within an explicit parameter budget and inference-time budget — and proposes three
design principles around a triadic Proposer/Solver/Verifier decomposition in which one base model plays
all three roles. Two diagnostic experiments on a code self-play setup support the claim, including a
nine-iteration run where solver reward climbs while the epiplexity of the generated data oscillates with
no trend. Cite v2 specifically: the reward-versus-epiplexity table is v2-only.*

**From the report**

> Core framing: one self-evolving model plays three roles — PROPOSER (generates tasks), SOLVER (attempts solutions), VERIFIER (provides training signals); Proposer + Verifier constitute the "internal environment" that co-evolves with the Solver, and "there is no external labelled dataset, teacher model, or reward model, so the loop is self-evolving." Success is defined as "a monotonic increase in learnable information" across iterations rather than task accuracy inside a fixed self-play game. — §1, §2.1
>
> The criterion, stated formally: a bounded-MDL optimiser over observers within a parameter budget C and inference budget T, giving epiplexity (learnable structure) and bounded entropy (what still looks like noise to that observer), with MDL their sum. Epiplexity "identifies a Goldilocks Zone for self-evolution: data must be neither too simple (low S, low H) nor too hard (low S, high H) for the current observer". — §2.2, Eqs. 1–4
>
> Estimation is a training run, not a formula you can call inline: epiplexity is measured by prequential coding, and Algorithm 1 trains an observer over the dataset (LoRA rank 16, up to 20 epochs with early stopping), computes per-token MDL at each epoch, and returns the prequential code length at the MDL-minimising epoch. — §4, Eq. 7, Algorithm 1
>
> Experimental setup: the public Absolute Zero repository with LoRA fine-tuning on the Qwen2.5 family; seed datasets generated by Qwen2.5 7B, Qwen2.5 14B and Qwen3 4B; 10% held out as validation; max sequence length 2048, LR 1e-4, up to 20 epochs with early stopping after five consecutive non-improving MDL epochs. The self-play run follows the Absolute Zero RL algorithm on a Qwen2.5 3B model over nine iterations on three code tasks (abduction / deduction / induction). — App. A, §4
>
> Headline 1: epiplexity is an inverted U in solver scale — across solver sizes 0.5B through 72B it rises then falls, peaking at 7B for all three tasks and all three proposers. The paper attributes the fall to the observer switching regimes: "Once a certain budget threshold is exceeded, the model instead opts for direct memorisation and abandons learning effective structure." — §4, Figure 5
>
> Headline 2 (v2, Appendix C, Table A1): on abduction the solver reward climbs 0.147 → 0.941 while epiplexity ends at 49.2 ×10³ bits against 48.8 ×10³ at iteration 1 — reward improves steadily while the measured learnable information of the generated data is where it started. "A steadily improving reward signal can coexist with highly unstable or degenerate generative complexity." On induction, epiplexity oscillates roughly six-fold with no trend across the nine iterations (75.7, 76.7, 458.9, 459.6, 76.5, 76.7, 462.1, 77.2, 474.3 ×10³ bits). Conclusion: "monitoring reward alone is insufficient to detect the collapse phenomena." Note the paper's own summary sentence ("rewards gradually increase over iterations across all three reasoning types") is contradicted by its abduction *proposer* column, which falls essentially monotonically from −0.152 to −0.458.
>
> The explicit reward-gaming test: "Absolute Zero reports a reward seesaw phenomenon in self-play reinforcement learning, which we also reproduce in our experiments. Epiplexity provides complementary insight by indicating whether this seesaw reflects meaningful, learnable structure or merely zero-sum reward redistribution between the proposer and solver. When rewards shift between roles without a corresponding change in Epiplexity, this suggests the system is engaged in reward gaming rather than genuine learning progress." — Appendix C (v2)
>
> Caveats the paper states: the experiments are "diagnostic rather than exhaustive" and a "proof of concept"; "closing the asymmetry gap is currently applicable only in domains that are easy to verify"; "learnable information cannot replace metrics related to final task accuracy, as it is a macroscopic measure; not all learnable information is necessarily useful for task completion"; and "the epiplexity metric comes from recent work (Finzi et al., 2026) and isn't yet widely validated". Its §4 claim that collapse "manifests as a decline in SOLVER capability and a collapse of the problem patterns generated by the proposer" is an authors' characterisation, not a measurement — no accuracy or pass-rate number appears anywhere in the paper. — §4, App. B, §7

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* Table A1 in Appendix C is the first thing I would read and possibly the only thing I would cite: for nine iterations the solver reward on abduction climbs 0.147 → 0.941 while the epiplexity of the data it is training on returns to where it started. That is a measured instance of the reward channel and the data channel disagreeing for an entire run — exactly the case where my own iteration logs would have told me things were going fine. I want §2.2 with Algorithm 1 together, because they decide where this can sit in a pipeline: estimating epiplexity means training an observer over the candidate dataset and taking the prequential code length at the MDL-minimising epoch, so this is a *between-iteration audit on a data batch*, not an inline accept/reject on a generated task, and I should not plan it as a per-sample filter without redesigning the estimator. Figure 5's inverted U is the caveat that matters for reuse — epiplexity peaks at a 7B solver and falls by 72B because the larger observer switches to memorisation — which makes any "this data is information-rich" number observer-relative, so reporting one means pinning the observer size and inference budget alongside it. And §3.1's asymmetry ladder gives a difficulty-targeting rule I can state precisely: match the propose-versus-solve gap to the current solver, because "when the gap exceeds the SOLVER capacity, it appears as time-bounded randomness, yielding noise-like tasks and stalled learning" — a cleaner failure account than "too hard", naming the specific symptom to look for.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the closest match, structurally. This factorises a self-evolving loop into exactly three roles played by one shared base model, which is the same one-model-many-roles move on a different cut than my world-authoring / policy / world-model split. It supplies two things my open questions ask for: task authoring treated as a role that must be explicitly synchronised to the solver frontier rather than left fixed, and an anti-Goodhart gate stated as a *measurable quantity* — monotonic epiplexity gain under a bounded observer — with Table A1 demonstrating a nine-iteration case where reward integrity fails precisely because reward alone was the gate, and the Appendix C passage above giving the explicit reward-gaming test. **Over-reflection in search agents** — a mechanism-level parallel on the generation side rather than the trajectory side: the paper's rejection of curriculum learning is that "a curriculum can increase apparent difficulty while repeatedly sampling structurally similar synthetic directions, leading to optimisation without information gain", which is the data-pipeline version of my finding — more activity, no new information. Epiplexity is a candidate way to make "these extra rounds changed nothing" quantitative rather than pattern-matched. A partial stretch: the paper measures whole data streams with a trained observer, never individual trajectories. **Post-cutoff distillation** — §3.3's Proactive Information Seeking is the same gating problem: a closed loop is bounded by information already in the weights, and bolting on a fixed corpus "reduces to fine-tuning on the corpus"; external context is only useful when selected against the learner's current frontier. Its bounded-observer definition — what counts as learnable is relative to a stated capacity and inference budget — is a more formal handle on my open question, since "could not already have had" is exactly an observer-relative statement. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a stretch, flagged as one: no replay, no off-policy reuse, no update-to-data ratios. The shared idea is only that the binding constraint on a self-generated data stream is learnable information for the *current* observer rather than sample count, and Figure 5 says that answer changes as the policy trains — a caveat for any fixed replay-buffer policy.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* steal the Table A1 protocol, not the theory — log proposer reward, solver reward, and a data-information measure side by side for every iteration of any self-play or trajectory-synthesis loop. The paper's own numbers show why, and the third column is the one that would have flagged a run the two reward columns signed off on. The sharp open question is the one its own title asserts: it never closes its loop. It defines the learnable-information-gain criterion and shows unfiltered self-play violating it, but no experiment enforces it — there is no accept/reject gate, no filtered-versus-unfiltered comparison, and no stated cost for running the estimator inside a generation loop. Since Algorithm 1 needs a full observer training run per measurement, whether a usable online proxy exists is undetermined, and that is the first thing I would try to answer.

[Source (arXiv 2603.02218)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02218)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>AutoWebWorld: Synthesizing Infinite Verifiable Web Environments via Finite State Machines</strong> · HKUST(GZ) + DeepWisdom + PKU + Mila/UdeM, February 2026</summary>

*A 27-page arXiv preprint (2602.14296v1, submitted 15 Feb 2026; cs.AI/cs.SE) proposing a pipeline that
writes a website down as an explicit finite state machine — page set, per-page "signature" variables,
action preconditions, deterministic effects, goal states — and then has coding agents compile that FSM
into a runnable Vue front-end whose DOM selectors are the ones the FSM declared. Breadth-first search over
the state graph enumerates shortest goal-reaching action sequences, replayed in the generated site with
Playwright; step validity comes from the FSM's precondition/effect rules and task success from a goal
predicate over state, so **no human annotator or LLM judge scores the trajectory**. The released scale is
29 environments / 875 pages / 11,663 verified trajectories at $0.04 each ($447.37 total build cost), and
the headline is that GRPO on roughly 16K synthesized steps lifts a 7B agent to 27.42% on WebVoyager.*

**From the report**

> Internal state is a page id plus a structured signature (query text, filter/sort options, pagination index, form fields, selected item/cart contents), and every precondition "must reference a signature path (starting with $.) and must not depend on screenshot-level element existence", so action availability and the goal predicate are "deterministic and evaluation does not depend on screenshot-level heuristics". Correctness and execution are kept as two separate levels — "the semantic transition is the source of truth for correctness; the GUI procedure provides an execution realization" — with BFS deduplicating states and returning shortest paths. — §3, §4.3, §A.1.2–A.1.3, §A.2.3
>
> The FSM is compiled into a site by a coding agent in four stages — guidelines/scaffolding, batched page synthesis with a generate-review-revise loop, build, and a self-repair loop fed the build logs — and "during FSM specification, we assign a unique selector to every interactable UI component on each page… This shared selector namespace provides a deterministic bridge from an FSM path to concrete UI operations." Execution-based filtering then replays every candidate with Playwright, where "a trajectory is accepted only if all steps can be carried out successfully… This filtering rule is intentionally strict". — §4.2–§4.4, §A.2.5
>
> The FSM validator runs five deterministic checks before an FSM is accepted: all terminal pages reachable from the initial page; preconditions reference only `$.` signature paths with no placeholders; effects deterministic and local-update-only (an action may change only fields it declares); navigation transitions well-defined with deterministic target-page init and same-name merge; and search/filter/sort actions must reset pagination fields. Failing checks go to an Improver that revises only the implicated pages/actions. — §A.1.6
>
> Dataset comparison: 875 website pages, 11,663 trajectories, "Synthesized" env type, "Inherent" verifier, 21.9 average steps and $0.04 per trajectory, against Explorer (94K traj, external verifier, 7.7 steps, $0.15), AgentTerk (10,398, 12.1, $0.55), Fara (145,603, 6.9, $1.00) and Mind2Web (2,350, 7.3, $0.80). — Table 1, §4.5
>
> Training recipe: the 11,663 trajectories are reduced to 1,215 distinct trajectories (one sampled per set of parallel trajectories for the same task) totalling 12,585 interaction steps, combined with grounding examples into ~16K training steps used for GRPO from a Qwen2.5-VL-7B checkpoint, 8 rollouts per prompt, effective batch 256, 1 epoch, BF16 + ZeRO-3 on 8×A800. The reward is a sum of three verifier-side terms with no learned judge: binary exact match on parsed action type, a coordinate term scoring 1 only if the predicted click falls inside the ground-truth bounding box, and a full-string regex format match. — §5.1, §D.1, §D.3–D.4
>
> WebVoyager under a 15-action cap: Ours-7B 27.42% overall versus UI-TARS-1.5-7B 26.51%, Claude-4.0-Sonnet 26.11%, GPT-5.1 18.96% and Qwen2.5-VL-7B 5.62%; Ours-3B reaches 15.09% against Qwen2.5-VL-3B's 3.96%. The paper contrasts the data cost: "UI-Venus reports training with roughly 1M SFT samples and TongUI uses about 350K RL samples, whereas our results are obtained with only 16K synthesized steps for GRPO training". — §5.2.1, Table 3
>
> Scaling with the mixture held fixed at a 2:8 grounding-to-navigation ratio, four training-set sizes of 8 / 256 / 1,024 / 16,253 samples give WebVoyager 3.92% → 17.59% → 19.09% → 27.42% and Online-Mind2Web 1.22% → 7.32% → 7.93% → 14.02%. — §5.3, Figure 4
>
> Eval-setup caveat the paper states: WebVoyager and Online-Mind2Web success is scored by an *external LLM judge* "from the instruction and interaction trace", each task is capped at 15 actions, and "since some websites frequently trigger access-denied errors or redirect to CAPTCHA mid-episode, we evaluate the 9 most stable websites and filter out the rest". Its other stated caveats are that "a small fraction of candidates may still fail when executed in the generated front-end due to website implementation mismatches" and that "although our total trajectory count is smaller than the largest real-world corpora"; there is no Limitations section, and §5.5 argues the reverse direction on realism — agents score *lower* on its 25-task synthetic suite (20.00 and 16.00) than on WebVoyager (26.51 / 26.11), which it reads as evidence the synthesized sites are "not trivially easier". — §4.4, §4.5, §5.1, §5.5

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the rule I actually want is in §A.1.2–A.1.3 and §A.2.3 — preconditions and goal predicates may only reference declared state paths (must start with `$.`) and are forbidden from reading rendered output. That is a lint I can impose on my own task verifiers, where success checks too often read text the policy itself emitted: it turns "don't let the model grade itself" from a principle into a schema constraint a validator can reject. §4.3–§4.4 with §A.2.5 is worth copying wholesale — keep a semantic trajectory (source of truth for correctness) and a GUI-execution trajectory (realization) as two levels, then accept only candidates that replay end to end. Table 2's cost breakdown reframes my own synthesis budget: environment construction and deterministic verification are the cheap, fixed part ($52.26 + $57.10), while step-level reasoning-trace generation is $272.17 of $447.37 — so if the same split holds for tool-agent synthesis, optimizing the environment builder is optimizing the wrong quarter. And I would read §5.1's evaluation details before believing Table 3: the intrinsic verifier stops at the environment boundary, so the headline 27.42% is still an external-judge number on 9 hand-picked stable sites under a 15-action cap, and a 0.91 pp gap over the nearest baseline measured that way is inside the band I would demand repeats for. Two source-side notes worth carrying: Table 7's four cost columns reconcile exactly across its 27 named rows while the website count (27 vs 29) and trajectory sum (11,638 vs 11,663) do not, and Tumblr appears as a cloned environment in the appendix figures but not in that table — direct evidence for at least one of the two missing environments.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — this is the world-authoring term made explicit and non-learned. The authoring role emits an FSM fixing the initial state, page set and transition rules; the world-model term collapses to a hand-specified deterministic transition function instead of a learned predictor; and the anti-Goodhart gate my note asks for shows up literally as the validator's five deterministic checks that an authored world must pass before any trajectory is drawn from it. It also prices the shortcut: the world model is free and unhackable, but it cannot generalize one step beyond what the author wrote down. **Over-reflection in search agents** — every kept trajectory is a BFS-shortest path to a goal predicate, so the supervision contains no confirm-then-keep-searching tail by construction, while the horizon is long (21.94 average steps versus 6.9–12.1 elsewhere) — a clean minimal-path control against my 63% keep-searching corpus. The pipeline *describes* the capability to emit negative trajectories by truncating before goal satisfaction or forcing precondition-violating actions, but never reports generating them, releasing them, or training a stopping decision on them, and its 15-action eval cap means a shortest-path-trained agent's stopping behaviour is never separately measured. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — the opposite lever on the same economics: instead of reusing expensive policy rollouts off-policy, make the rollout deterministic and enumerable so it costs $0.04, then throw most of them away anyway (11,663 verified reduced to 1,215 distinct, to avoid overfitting homogeneous patterns). The cost table then says the expense migrated to per-step reasoning generation — exactly the artefact a replay buffer would amortize and BFS enumeration does not. **Wearable world model** — a stretch on domain, but a real one on my latent-encoder-versus-generative fork: this is a third answer, a *symbolic* world model whose transition function is a JSON rule table, so it has zero inference cost and needs no compression at all. The price is that its fidelity is bounded by what a proposer agent wrote down, which is the trade my note is really about.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the "`$.`-path only" contract as a hard lint on task verifiers — a success predicate may reference only declared environment state variables and is rejected if it reads rendered output or model-produced text, with the five deterministic authoring checks as the gate an authored task must pass before it can produce training data. The open number the paper leaves: it never reports the accept rate of the strict replay filter, i.e. how many FSM-valid BFS trajectories die on front-end implementation mismatch. That single ratio is the direct measurement of FSM-to-environment fidelity, and its absence is why the realism argument has to lean on the indirect evidence that agents score lower on its synthetic suite than on WebVoyager.

[Source (arXiv 2602.14296)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14296) · [project page](https://evanwu1125.github.io/AWW_homepage/)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Scalable Environments Drive Generalizable Agents</strong> · HKUST(GZ) + DeepWisdom + PKU + NUS + SUTD + Mila/UdeM, May 2026</summary>

*A 13-page position paper (arXiv 2605.18181v1, submitted 18 May 2026) arguing that generalizable agents
require **environment scaling** — expanding the distribution over executable rule-sets — rather than only
scaling trajectories or tasks inside a fixed rule-set. It formalizes an environment as an executable tuple
inducing an agent-facing interaction kernel, proposes a three-regime taxonomy (trajectory / task /
environment scaling) with a decision rule for boundary cases, contrasts programmatic generators against
generative world models, and proposes a five-axis suite-level reporting checklist. It contains **no
experiments, no training runs and no empirical results of the authors' own**; its only quantitative content
is a survey-style table of statistics attributed to five existing environment suites.*

**From the report**

> An environment is an executable rule set — state space, action interface, transition dynamics, observation space and environment signal space (rewards, verifier outputs such as unit tests, termination flags) — and the relevant distinction is behavioral, not implementational: it induces an agent-facing interaction kernel, so "two implementations may use different hidden state spaces or internal transition code, yet induce the same observable interaction process for the agent". — §2, Eq. 1
>
> Three regimes separated by primary deliverable: trajectory scaling expands the trajectory distribution with the rule-set fixed; task scaling expands the task distribution, where a task is (goal, constraints, initial conditions, success verifier), and tasks "do not alter the interaction kernel"; environment scaling expands the distribution over rule-sets. The one-line separator: "task scaling changes the objective, while environment scaling changes the interaction". — §2–§3
>
> A formal test for whether a generated environment is genuinely new: a behavioral distance taken as a supremum over a probing policy class of the divergence between the two environments' distributions over observable records (observation, action, signal). "If [it] = 0, then the two implementations are indistinguishable to the agent under the chosen probes, even if their hidden states or internal code differ." — §3.3
>
> A two-stage boundary rule for classifying a pipeline — classify by primary deliverable first, then use rule-set change as secondary evidence: "We treat a method as environment scaling only when it delivers a reusable environment instance with a modified interface or dynamics contract" and "If validation only ensures that the interface elements and execution paths needed for particular tasks work, it is task scaling." One worked example is demoted to task scaling on exactly that basis. — §3.4
>
> Programmatic generators are factored into a verifiable rule scaffold and a content variable: the scaffold "specifies the executable interaction contract (e.g., action interface and transition logic)" while the content variable "controls high-entropy semantics such as page contents, entities, and narratives", sampled by embedding language-model calls inside the programmatic scaffold — which "increases semantic diversity without changing the core executable interaction, preserving reproducibility and verifiability". — §4.1, Eqs. 4–5
>
> The five-axis suite-level reporting checklist: **Executability** (compilation/run success rate, crash-free interaction steps, deterministic replay under fixed seeds); **Signals** (oracle coverage — fraction with checkable ground truth; reward reliability across repeated trials; invariant violations indicating inconsistencies in the transition or signal functions); **Coverage** (controlled perturbations that "vary one component while holding others fixed"); **Complexity** (minimal solution horizon, effective action branching, feedback sparsity or delay as credit-assignment length); **Efficiency** (generation throughput, cost per valid environment, simulation cost per step). — §4.3
>
> The training objective is stated but never run: maximize expected environment signal over environments drawn from the scaled distribution, plus a localized variant over a neighborhood of executable variants around a deployment target, and a rule-shift mismatch term — with the caveat that "the signed gap can cancel across environments". Kolmogorov complexity is explicitly rejected as an operational complexity measure because "it is not operational and is highly sensitive to language and library abstractions in real implementations". — §4.1, §4.4, Eqs. 7–8
>
> What the paper does not contain, stated plainly: no scaling curve relates environment count or diversity to held-out generalization; no saturation or diminishing-returns threshold appears anywhere ("saturat", "diminish", "plateau", "log-linear", "power law" and "scaling law" each occur zero times); and no base models, RL/SFT method, step counts or rollout counts appear, since the objectives are proposed rather than optimized. §5 concedes three counterpositions, including that curation quality beats scale ("We agree that expert curation is often the fastest route to high-quality testbeds") and that synthetic environments "risk overfitting to suite-specific artifacts and may not transfer". — §4.4, §5

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §4.3 first, treated as a reporting template for an environment-synthesis run — the two indicators I do not currently emit are "reward reliability across repeated trials" and "invariant violations that indicate inconsistencies in [the transition or signal functions]". Those are verifier-integrity checks on the *environment itself* rather than on the trajectory, and they catch a broken signal channel before it silently trains a policy on noise. §3.3's behavioral distance is the piece I want to actually implement: it converts "is this synthesized environment genuinely new or a re-skin of its parent?" from a judgment call into a runnable acceptance gate — probe both instances with the same policy class over a fixed horizon and require a nonzero distance on observable records before the instance counts toward diversity. §3.4's two-stage rule is the sharpest available audit of my own pipelines: a generator that only guarantees the interface elements needed for its specific tasks is task scaling wearing environment-scaling clothes, and the honest test is whether the output is a standalone instance reusable for repeated rollouts under a new contract. Read §4.4's localized-neighborhood objective as the cheap version of the argument — perturb layouts, entities and observations while preserving the interaction contract — but note there are no numbers of the authors' own to compare against; the only quantities in the paper are third-party suite statistics whose own caption disclaims them ("LoC is reported as a coarse engineering footprint and should not be interpreted as a scientific complexity measure").
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — closest match, and useful as an external formalization to check mine against. The paper factors the same pieces: a world-authoring term over executable rule-sets (initial conditions sit in the *task* tuple, not in the environment), a policy, and a transition/world-model term, and its objective optimizes policy and update rule *in expectation over authored worlds*. The divergence is exactly my thesis: it keeps the generator architecturally separate from the agent and never treats authoring as a learnable role of the same model. Its five reporting criteria plus the behavioral-distance test are directly usable as the anti-Goodhart gates a world builder must pass — in particular "invariant violations that indicate inconsistencies in [the transition or signal functions]" is a reward-channel-integrity check, one of my stated open questions. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — complementary rather than overlapping: §4.4 attacks the same rollout-scarcity problem from the *supply* side, generating a neighborhood of executable variants so cheap fresh on-policy experience is abundant while task semantics stay anchored to the deployment domain. Its Efficiency axis (generation throughput, cost per valid environment, simulation cost per step) is precisely the accounting that decides which lever is cheaper for search agents — and the paper offers no measurement of either, so the trade is posed and not settled. **Over-reflection in search agents** — a narrower link, through one criterion rather than the thesis: the Complexity axis recommends reporting *minimal solution horizon*, which is the denominator my confirm-then-keep-searching measurement currently lacks, letting excess rounds be expressed as overshoot against a known optimum instead of a raw count. The paper says nothing about stopping or pivoting; it supplies the environment-side statistic, not the behavior analysis. **Wearable world model** — partial, and the domain link is a stretch, since the paper is silent on quantization and on-device deployment. Where it does bear on my open fork is reliability rather than cost: it formalizes the generative world model as a learned transition distribution, and its appendix table marks the one generative entry as executable but *not* consistent — i.e. the generative branch buys coverage at the price of deterministic replay, which argues for the latent-encoder side whenever repeatability matters more than open-endedness.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the behavioral-distance acceptance gate into my environment-synthesis pipeline as a diversity check with teeth — run a fixed probing policy class over a horizon against a candidate instance and its parent, and only count the candidate toward the environment distribution if the distributions over observable records separate. Combined with the Coverage rule (perturb one component while holding the others fixed), that gives per-axis diversity accounting instead of an instance count, which is the number I currently over-trust. The sharp open question is the one its title asserts: it defines the environment distribution and writes the objective over it, but supplies no dose-response evidence at all — no curve relating environment count or behavioral spread to held-out transfer, and explicitly no saturation threshold anywhere. "How many behaviorally-distinct environments buy how much cross-environment transfer, and where does it flatten" is exactly the experiment this position paper motivates and does not run.

[Source (arXiv 2605.18181)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18181)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>RRC: Unlocking Generative Reward Models in LLM Reinforcement Learning via Ranking-Based Reward Construction</strong> · Northeastern University (Shenyang) + NiuTrans Research, August 2026</summary>

*A 26-page arXiv preprint (2608.06310v1, submitted 6 Aug 2026; cs.LG/cs.CL) proposing Ranking-based
Reward Construction, a method for using generative reward models in RL by deriving each response's scalar
reward from its **win count in pairwise comparisons** rather than from a preference-token probability. The
paper argues that generative reward models are natively comparative while GRPO-style RL consumes scalar
scores, and offers two ranking strategies: self-competitive ranking (all sampled responses compete against
each other) and anchor-guided ranking (each sample is compared against a small fixed set of reference
responses). Experiments train LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct policies with 3B- and 8B-scale
reward models, reporting gains over probability-based construction (e.g. AlpacaEval2 35.8% → 41.3%).*

**From the report**

> The mismatch it names: "discriminative reward models are inherently designed to produce scalar reward scores, whereas generative reward models are naturally formulated to perform comparative ranking", and forcing the latter into a scoring role "can lead to suboptimal preference prediction and policy optimization". The motivating measurement: generative RMs beat discriminative ones by +7.5% on RM-Bench and +4.2% on JudgeBench, but only +1.3% once used as a scalar scorer in RL. — §1, §3, Fig. 1
>
> The two constructions are win counts, not scores: self-competitive ranking scores each response by its wins in a fully connected tournament over the sampled group; anchor-guided ranking scores it by wins against n anchors drawn from a fixed reference policy, forming a bipartite graph. Query cost is m(m−1)/2 for the tournament versus m·n for anchors. — §4.2, Eqs. 5 and 7
>
> Two robustness mechanisms sit on top: majority voting queries the reward model multiple times per pair and takes the modal preference; conflict-aware ranking adjustment resolves cyclic intransitivity (A beats B, B beats C, C beats A) by treating preferences as a directed weighted graph and finding a minimum-weight feedback edge set via a greedy Kemeny heuristic. Removing it costs 0.6% (3B) and 1.9% (8B) on AlpacaEval2. — §4.2, App. B.2
>
> Why this interacts with group normalization: the paper proves that if rewards are an affine transform of oracle quality, the group-normalized advantage is **unchanged**. So ordering alone is not enough — "reward differences must scale proportionally with oracle differences", because "the relative magnitudes of policy updates across sampled responses are governed by the normalized reward gaps". — App. A
>
> Anchors must come from a frozen policy, not the current one. With on-policy anchors the reward becomes parameter-dependent and the gradient gains a non-zero baseline-drift term, so "the reward assigned to a fixed response o may decrease even if its absolute quality does not, simply because anchors become stronger", forming a moving-target objective; with reference-policy anchors the reward gradient is zero and the landscape is stationary. — App. A
>
> Training recipe: reward models trained on HelpSteer3 (40.5K labeled preference examples, with human-written feedback used as rationales for the generative RM and withheld from the discriminative RM), one epoch, LR 1e-5, batch 256, on 3B and 8B LLaMA backbones. RL uses GRPO, LR 1e-6, batch 128, over a 6K SFT set and a 7.5K RL set, with group size 8, anchors 8, scaling factor 0.1, plus a binary format reward for think/answer tags incorporated only in the reasoning-augmented setting. — §5.1, App. B.1
>
> Headline results at 8B-scale reward models with thinking, against the best probability-based baseline: AlpacaEval2 35.8% → 41.3%, ArenaHardV2 8.0% → 11.2%, MMLU-Redux 52.9% → 57.3%, WildBench 50.1% → 59.8%. Offline preference baselines trained on the same data trail on AlpacaEval2 (DPO 29.8%, SimPO 31.2%) — though on MMLU-Redux with thinking, SimPO (54.7) and even the untouched SFT model (55.4) beat several RRC variants. — §5.2, Table 1
>
> A compute-matched control rules out "you just bought more judge compute": replacing majority voting in the probability baseline with soft aggregation (averaging preference probabilities over multiple CoT judge queries) uses a comparable number of generative-RM calls and still reaches only 30.1% on AlpacaEval2, versus 36.4% for the tournament and 35.8% for anchors — so "allocating additional inference computation alone is insufficient". The mechanism behind the baseline's ceiling is measured too: a point-wise generative RM puts 42.3% of preference-token probability mass in [0, 0.1] and 46.0% in [0.9, 1.0], leaving only 11.7% in between. — App. C.2–C.3, Tables 5–6
>
> Caveats the paper states: anchor scaling is not monotone — "when the number of anchors becomes very large (e.g., 256), performance tends to saturate or even slightly degrade", hypothesized to come from loss of anchor diversity yielding "redundant or low-informative comparisons" — and vote and anchor scaling both show diminishing returns, "indicating a trade-off between computational cost and performance". There is no formal limitations section, and the paper reports single runs with no seeds, variance or confidence intervals, so its smaller margins are not shown to exceed run-to-run noise. — §6.1, App. C.2–C.3

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* Appendix A's affine-invariance result first — it proves group normalization eats any affine rescaling of the reward, so under GRPO a reward's *ordering* is free and its **spacing inside the group** is the entire learning signal. That reframes how I would audit a reward channel: the question is not whether the scalar looks sane in absolute terms but whether within-group gaps track quality margins. Table 5 is the ablation that makes the claim believable, so I would read it before the headline table — they re-spend the identical number of judge calls on a strengthened baseline and it still lands 30.1 versus 36.4, which separates "ranking aggregation" from "more judge compute". Table 6's 42.3% / 11.7% / 46.0% split is a cheap diagnostic I can run directly: histogram the judge's preference-token probability, and if ~88% of mass sits at the rails, the scalar is carrying almost no margin information no matter how accurate its argmax is. And Appendix A's anchor property is the piece to read for reward-channel integrity — drawing comparison anchors from the policy being trained makes the reward gradient non-zero and turns the objective into a moving target, which is a clean formal statement of why a self-referential reward destabilizes training, and an argument for freezing whatever plays the reference role.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — anchor-guided ranking is a form of expensive-rollout reuse: a small anchor bank is generated once (~1h on 16 GPUs) and then amortized across every batch of the run as a comparison baseline, cutting query cost and bringing wall-clock to 10.6h against the 10.5h baseline while gaining +3.6 on AlpacaEval2. Its anchor property is also a sharp constraint on the reuse question that note leaves open: the reused set must stay off-policy and frozen, because regenerating it from the current policy reintroduces a non-stationary objective. **Over-reflection in search agents** — my open question is state-conditioned stop/pivot RL, and this supplies the mechanism-level warning for building that reward. Since group normalization is invariant to affine rescaling, a stop/pivot reward that assigns nearly the same scalar to "confirmed at round 3" and "confirmed at round 3 then searched twelve more times" yields no gradient after normalization; ranking those rollouts against each other, or against a frozen anchor, is precisely how you manufacture the margin that makes the difference learnable. The paper studies chat and math RLHF, not stopping behavior, so this is a transfer of the reward-construction mechanism rather than a result about agents. **AgentPlanet** — two concrete integrity mechanisms for that note's anti-Goodhart question: the conflict-aware adjustment detects and repairs cyclic, intransitive judge preferences via Kemeny-style minimum-weight feedback edge removal (worth 0.6–1.9 points), which is a checkable *consistency gate* on a judge's output; and the anchor property formalizes why a reward whose reference points are produced by the model being trained drifts. Both bear directly on a design where one model plays authoring, policy and world-model roles and could end up grading itself. Scope note: the source is open-ended chat plus math/knowledge RLHF with no tool use, retrieval or multi-turn agent trajectories, so none of my notes shares its task domain and every link above is by reward-construction mechanism only.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* steal the compute-matched control, not just the method — when claiming that a new way of turning judge output into a scalar wins, re-spend the exact same number of judge calls on the old way. The 30.1-versus-36.4 gap is what rules out the cheap alternative explanation, and it is cheap to run. The sharp open question the paper leaves: its own Appendix A proves the GRPO advantage is invariant to affine rescaling, which predicts *no* sensitivity to the reward scaling factor at all — yet the sweep shows 0.1 → 10.0 costing 3.6 points (36.4 → 32.8). The paper waves at "noise amplification" without reconciling the two. My reading is that the factor scales only the ranking term while the added binary format reward stays fixed, so it is silently setting the *ratio* between ranking reward and format reward; if that is right, the real hyperparameter is that ratio and it is never studied. Worth testing directly on any composite reward I build.

[Source (arXiv 2608.06310)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06310) · [code](https://github.com/wangclnlp/RRC)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>End-to-End Context Compression at Scale</strong> · NYU + Modal Labs + UMD + Princeton + Columbia + Harvard, June 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2606.09659v1, submitted 8 Jun 2026; cs.CL) introducing Latent Context Language Models, a
family of encoder-decoder context compressors in which a 0.6B encoder maps every N input tokens to a
single latent embedding, an MLP adapter projects it into the decoder's embedding space, and a 4B decoder
consumes those latents in place of the raw context. The authors run a from-scratch architecture search
(38B training tokens per variant at 16× compression), then continually pre-train and supervised-fine-tune
three models at 1:4, 1:8 and 1:16 compression on roughly 350B combined encoder-decoder tokens per model,
and release weights and code. The headline claim is a new Pareto frontier over post-hoc KV-cache
compression across task accuracy, time-to-first-token and peak GPU memory, together with an agentic
harness in which the model skims a 16×-compressed context and calls an EXPAND tool on individual
512-token chunks on demand.*

**From the report**

> Architecture: given compression ratio N, the encoder maps each contiguous block of N input tokens to one latent token via a pooling operator, an MLP adapter projects the latents into the decoder hidden dimension, and the decoder consumes them in place of the original tokens; the configuration selected at scale is a Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B encoder with a Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 decoder, encoder window 1024, causal masking, mean pooling. — §3, §5
>
> "End-to-end" means a four-stage unfreeze schedule, not post-hoc eviction: adapter-only (LR 1.0e-3, both towers frozen) → encoder unfrozen (6.0e-5) → end-to-end continual pre-training with the decoder at 1.0e-5 → SFT at 3.0e-5, with a per-model token budget at 16× of 38.83B + 77.65B + 182.51B + 51.05B = 350.04B combined encoder + decoder tokens, at a 4M-token global batch and 16,384 sequence length. Training the full model end-to-end from the start "underperforms the multi-stage recipe", and frozen-decoder or LoRA variants "substantially underperform" full-parameter training. — §4.2, Table 1
>
> Compression-speed headline (in-figure annotations, measured as time-to-first-token per sample on a single H200 with every method sharing the same decoder): 8.8× faster on RULER @ 4K, 6.4× @ 8K, 4.7× @ 16K, 5.2× on LongBench @ 64K and 5.2× on a long-form health QA set @ ~64K. KV-cache baselines appear as vertical lines because they prefill the full context regardless of compression ratio. — Figures 1 and 5
>
> Quality is a frontier claim, not dominance: at 16× the compressed model scores RULER 4k/8k/16k = 75.06/70.23/65.91 and GSM8K 81.05 against 94.41/93.58/93.74 and 93.25 for the uncompressed full-KV decoder, while every KV-cache baseline collapses on GSM8K at 16× (best 31.39, four of seven at 0.00–0.08); but at 4× KVzip's 94.43 on RULER 4k beats LCLM's 91.76, and at 16× one attention-matching baseline takes LongBench en16 40.49 to 39.08. — Table 6
>
> The agent use case: the input is segmented into fixed 512-token chunks, each compressed and assigned an integer id, and the model receives the whole compressed sequence in one prompt plus a single EXPAND tool — "At each turn, the model can decide to make a tool call of the form EXPAND(i), and the tool returns the original text of the expanded segment." On needle-in-a-haystack at 16× compression this expand-or-answer loop lifts the average from 76.89 to 93.99 at 4K (+17.10), 72.51 to 92.46 at 8K (+19.95) and 70.18 to 89.91 at 16K (+19.74), with per-task gains ranging from +1.40 to +45.40. — §7, Table 33
>
> Memory behaviour is the other half of the frontier: peak memory for the 16× model stays nearly flat from 128K to 512K tokens because encoder activations dominate until decoder prefill over the compressed latents overtakes them, while across 4K to 1M context one baseline runs out of memory at 1M and "fails at 512K tokens due to numerical instability in the linear solver", and all other baselines OOM at 512K and 1M within a 141GB budget. — §6, Figure 4
>
> Eval harness: efficiency is the mean of 30 measurements after 20 warmup steps on the same H200, using Hugging Face Transformers implementations with timing code adapted per method; the encoder runs at batch size 128 with window 1024, i.e. 131,072 input tokens per batched encoder pass. The paper also notes some KV-cache methods are evaluated by masking or subsampling a full cache with non-uniform per-head/per-layer eviction, so their reductions "do not necessarily translate into wall-clock speedups or memory savings in standard inference engines without specialized kernels that have not been released or even implemented". — §6, §6.1
>
> Caveats the paper states: throughput "measurements reported here are conservative relative to what optimized serving implementations could achieve" since vLLM/SGLang are not used; compression time is not reported for GSM8K because measurements are "too noisy to support reliable conclusions"; and training on reconstruction data alone lets the model "reconstruct the full sequence with negligible loss, but it generalizes poorly beyond reconstruction-style tasks. In fact, it cannot perform any other tasks, even when the LLM decoder is kept frozen". Note also that Table 5's caption claims coverage of "general reasoning, knowledge, instruction-following benchmarks, and an agentic coding benchmark" while the table lists only RULER, LongBench, a health QA set and GSM8K, and no knowledge, instruction-following or coding result appears anywhere in the paper's 33 tables and 12 figures. — §6, §6.1, §C.1

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §7 and Table 33 first, as a stopping-policy result in disguise — the paper literally calls it an "expand-or-answer loop", and at 16× it adds +17.10 / +19.95 / +19.74 average points, but the per-task spread runs +1.40 to +45.40, which is the signature of a decision rule that only fires where the compressed representation had already failed. §7 says the rule is not learned, only exposed as a tool. Table 6 before Figures 1 and 5, because the accuracy columns undercut the plots: at 4× a KV-cache baseline beats the compressed model on RULER 4k, and at 16× another takes LongBench en16, so the win is on the time-to-first-token axis and the 8.8×/5.2× annotations are the load-bearing numbers — and they come from figure labels, not from a table I can re-derive. §C.1 is the cheapest lesson in the paper: reconstruction-only training drives its own proxy loss to near zero and destroys every other capability, even with the decoder frozen, and only mixing in next-token prediction recovers it — a clean single-metric collapse with a named fix, and the shape of failure I would expect from any objective scored on one reconstruction-style proxy. And Table 1 with the appendix for the recipe I would actually copy, including the LR sweep gated on re-benchmarking the model *without* compression after SFT to confirm the decoder did not drift.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — Table 33's expand-or-answer loop is structurally my stop/pivot/answer decision: each turn the model either calls EXPAND or commits to an answer. §7 explicitly leaves that policy unlearned ("Future work may explore new mechanisms for determining which context to expand or how to learn this behavior end-to-end"), and no expand-call count is reported anywhere — so it has exactly the blind spot my 63% confirm-then-keep-searching number was built to expose, while its 16×-compressed needle-in-a-haystack setup is a cheap, fully-keyed environment for training a state-conditioned stop rule. **Wearable world model** — this is the memory half of that note and it answers the latent-encoder-versus-generative fork concretely on the encoder side: a *trained* 0.6B encoder instead of post-hoc KV eviction, with peak memory nearly flat for the 16× model from 128K to 512K tokens while every KV-cache baseline OOMs at 512K and 1M inside 141GB. It is an orthogonal lever to weight quantization — it shrinks the sequence the decoder ever sees rather than the bits per parameter. Their 8B-decoder result is mixed (substantially lower pre-training loss but no downstream gain), which the authors attribute to the data mixture and recipe being tuned around the 4B instruct decoder rather than to scale itself. **AgentPlanet** — the anti-Goodhart-gate question gets a hard datapoint in §C.1: reconstruction-only training drives its own proxy metric to negligible loss and wipes out every other capability, even with the decoder frozen, and only a mixture with next-token prediction recovers it. That is precisely the failure a world-authoring reward scored on one reconstruction-style proxy would hit. The link to the three-role factorization itself is a stretch — the paper has no policy term, no transition model, and never discusses world authoring. **Post-cutoff distillation** — two mechanism overlaps: they relabel a subset of SFT targets with stronger instruct models rather than train on stale completions, and they gate the entire LR sweep on a forgetting check, re-benchmarking without compression after SFT to confirm it matches pre-training behaviour. That gate is the same shape as my "did I only teach genuinely new content" condition, applied to capability rather than to facts, and their memory-block delimiters are a ready-made carrier for a fact cache held as latent rather than raw tokens.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the forgetting check as a standing gate on any post-training sweep — after fine-tuning, re-run the model in its *un-modified* configuration and require it to match its pre-training behaviour before accepting the LR. It is nearly free and it catches the case where a gain on the new objective was bought with drift on everything else. What to watch: the expand-or-answer loop is the most directly transferable idea in the paper and the paper deliberately does not train it. A learned expand policy — conditioned on the compressed representation's own uncertainty rather than on a prompt — is a well-posed experiment with a fully-keyed environment already built, and the +1.40-to-+45.40 per-task spread is the evidence that a *selective* rule has room to beat the always-expand default.

[Source (arXiv 2606.09659)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09659) · [code](https://github.com/LeonLixyz/LCLM)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>BiCAA: Bidirectional Credit Assignment for Search-Augmented Agent</strong> · Harbin Institute of Technology, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.01321v1, submitted 2 Aug 2026; cs.CL, 9 pages) proposing a process-reward scheme
for RL-trained multi-step search agents that replaces GRPO's single trajectory-level reward with a dense
per-search-step reward. The step reward fuses two signals computed from the policy's own log-likelihoods
— a forward "solvability gain" (change in the average log-probability of the gold answer once the step's
retrieved evidence enters context) and a backward "hindsight success criticality" (how likely the policy
was to emit that step when the gold answer is placed in the prompt) — combined by an asymmetric weighting
rule and added to the outcome reward inside a GRPO-style clipped objective. Trained on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct
and Qwen3-8B over a Wikipedia dense-retrieval harness, it reports an average F1 of 52.1 across seven QA
benchmarks against 48.3 for the strongest baseline.*

**From the report**

> Forward Solvability Gain is defined without any judge or learned scorer: f is the token-level average log-likelihood of the gold answer given the question and the trajectory prefix through step t, and the step reward is the marginal change that retrieval step induced. "Positive forward solvability gain implies retrieved evidence offers supportive clues and boosts the question's solvability, while negative gain indicates noisy irrelevant retrieval results that interfere with reasoning." — Method / Forward Solvability Gain, Eqs. 1–2
>
> Hindsight Success Criticality is also policy-computed, not judged: the geometric mean of the step's own token log-likelihoods **with the ground-truth answer conditioned into the prompt**, normalized by the trajectory-wide mean and clipped to give a criticality ratio ω. "A search action [is] relatively vital for backward inference of the ground-truth answer if ω > 1, while ω < 1 suggests this retrieval contributes marginally." — Method / Hindsight Success Criticality, Eqs. 3–5
>
> The two directions are fused asymmetrically: r = ω·g when g ≥ 0, and ω⁻¹·g when g < 0 — boosting credit for steps that both help locally and prove necessary in hindsight, while "adopt[ing] small reciprocal coefficients to reduce penalty magnitudes for critical search steps" whose negative gain may stem from "flawed semantic construction of queries." — Method / Unified Advantage Estimation, Eq. 6
>
> Process and outcome rewards are group-normalized independently, then combined with the process term geometrically discounted over a lookahead window while "the normalized outcome reward adopts no discounting and is identical across all turns." The advantage is propagated to all tokens of the turn under a clipped surrogate with a KL penalty (group-relative, no learned value model), and "we only compute gradients for decision tokens (reasoning, tool calls, answers), masking tool response tokens." — Method / Unified Advantage Estimation, Eqs. 7–10
>
> Training recipe: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B; "following IGPO, we adopt a unified mixed open-domain QA training set"; 8 GPUs, global batch 32, group size 16 rollouts, lr 1e-6, process-reward weight α = 0.25, KL coefficient 0.001. No SFT or distillation stage. Eval harness: E5-base-v2 over a local Wikipedia corpus, top-3 passages per query, maximum 10 interaction turns, word-level F1, four in-domain sets (NQ, TriviaQA, HotpotQA, 2Wiki) and three out-of-domain (MuSiQue, Bamboogle, PopQA). — Experiments / Implementation Details
>
> Headline numbers (7-benchmark average F1): on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, BiCAA 52.1 vs HCAPO 48.3, GiGPO 47.2, IGPO 45.7, Search-R1 38.5, CoT+RAG 36.4, CoT 23.4, with the largest gains multi-hop (HotpotQA 57.7, 2Wiki 51.9, MuSiQue 28.6); on Qwen3-8B, 53.3 vs GRPO 50.9. It does not sweep: on Bamboogle BiCAA scores 59.0 against HCAPO's 69.0, and on NQ 45.5 against GiGPO's 46.4. — Main Results, Table 1
>
> A step can raise the answer's log-probability and still be redundant — the quadrant table is the load-bearing evidence. Classifying retrieval steps from 100 sampled trajectories into useful / repeated / off-target: g>0 & ω>1 = 78.4 / 12.1 / 9.5; **g>0 & ω<1 = 47.2 / 36.8 / 16.0**; g<0 & ω>1 = 59.6 / 15.2 / 25.2; g<0 & ω<1 = 24.3 / 29.4 / 46.3. The paper's reading: "retrievals improving local single-step solvability often contain duplicate information that contributes minimally to the complete reasoning trajectory", while negative-gain high-criticality steps "act as critical prerequisites for accurate subsequent reasoning." Search-behavior curves show the HSC-free variant's redundancy "surges sharply to the maximum of all variants" while BiCAA's search count "peak[s] mid-training and decline[s] afterwards"; the added computation is 10–13% of total training latency. — Table 3, Figure 2
>
> Caveats read off its own tables: removing the outcome reward and training on the bidirectional process reward alone causes "the most substantial performance decline among all experimental variants", 52.1 → 45.8 across every benchmark, so "standalone process supervision is inadequate for reliable policy optimization"; and the α sweep is narrow and non-monotone — 45.4 at α=0.00, best 52.1 at α=0.25, then 51.3 / 48.7 / 44.8 at 0.50 / 0.75 / 1.00, i.e. over-weighting the dense self-computed reward ends up worse than using no process reward at all. There is no Limitations section. — Ablation Studies (Table 2), Hyperparameter Sensitivity (Table 4)

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* Eqs. 3–5 is the piece I actually want — ω is a per-step necessity score obtained by putting the gold answer in the prompt and reading the geometric mean of the step's own token log-likelihoods, then normalizing by the trajectory mean and clipping. No judge model, no learned PRM, one extra forward pass per step over rollouts I already have on disk, so it is computable **offline on keyed trajectories** rather than only inside a training loop. Table 3's g>0 / ω<1 cell is the load-bearing empirical claim — 47.2% useful against 36.8% repeated — because it says a step can raise the answer's log-probability and still be redundant, which is exactly the failure mode a pure information-gain reward cannot see. Table 4 read with Table 2 is the safety envelope: α=1.00 (44.8) lands *below* α=0.00 (45.4) and process-only training collapses to 45.8, so a dense reward the policy computes about itself only pays inside a narrow band around 0.25 and needs the outcome anchor. And the harness bounds how far the numbers travel: E5-base-v2 top-3 over a local Wikipedia corpus with a 10-turn cap is a small closed-corpus RAG setup, not live browsing, so the +3.8 average-F1 delta is the transferable quantity and the absolute F1s are not comparable to anything measured on the open web.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the tightest link in this batch. My confirm-then-keep-searching pattern at 63% is exactly the population the g>0 / ω<1 quadrant isolates: steps that nudge answer log-likelihood upward while contributing nothing, measured at 36.8% repeated queries there. The training-dynamics version is the same symptom — the HSC-free baseline's search count climbs while the full method peaks mid-training and declines — which makes ω a candidate scalar for my state-conditioned stop/pivot question, since ω<1 on a step taken *after* the answer was already stated is a computable "this round changed nothing" signal rather than a judged one. **AgentPlanet** — connects on reward-channel integrity, not world authoring. Both halves of the process reward are computed by the policy itself, so the model being trained is also the thing scoring its intermediate steps, with no external verifier anywhere in the loop.
