The paper never asks whether a policy can inflate its own ω; its only outside check is an LLM classifier applied post hoc to 100 sampled trajectories. The α sweep is the closest thing to evidence of the hazard — trusting the self-computed channel at α=1.00 (44.8 F1) is worse than ignoring it entirely (45.4). **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — complementary axis on the same cost problem. FlashSAC raises updates-per-rollout via replay; BiCAA raises *signal*-per-rollout by turning one trajectory-level scalar into T turn-level advantages, priced at 10–13% additional training latency. The open question they jointly pose: both g and ω are defined against the current policy and would drift with staleness, so it is untested whether a per-step reward like this survives being computed under an old policy and replayed. **Post-cutoff distillation** — mechanism-only, and a stretch since there is no teacher or student here. What transfers is the reward shape: the forward term is a per-step reward expressed as a delta in the model's own token-level log-likelihood of the target string, and the hindsight term is a *privileged-information channel that only exists at training time* because it requires the gold answer inside the prompt. That is the same structural pattern as gating a reward by information the learner could not otherwise have had, and the clipped ω is one concrete way to bound such a hindsight channel.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the ω ratio as a cheap offline diagnostic rather than a training signal — score already-collected keyed trajectories with the geometric mean of each step's token log-probs conditioned on the gold answer, normalize by the trajectory mean, and check whether the ω<1 tail coincides with the rounds that come after the answer was already stated and confirmed. If it does, I get a judge-free redundancy detector at one forward pass per step; if it does not, the paper's central assumption fails on longer horizons than the 10-turn Wikipedia setting it was validated in. The open question the paper leaves untouched: nothing verifies the reward channel it introduces. Both signals are read off the policy being optimized, the only external audit is a post-hoc classification of 100 trajectories, and the α sweep shows the channel becomes actively harmful at 1.00 — but there is no experiment testing whether extended training teaches the policy to raise its own ω without improving answers, which is precisely the anti-Goodhart gate a self-scored dense reward needs.

[Source (arXiv 2608.01321)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01321)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Learning from Environmental Feedback: Credit Assignment across Multiple Timescales for Agentic Reinforcement Learning</strong> · Northeastern University (Shenyang) + NiuTrans + CAS, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.08255v1, submitted 8 Aug 2026; cs.LG, cross-listed cs.CL) proposing EFCA, a plug-in
step-level credit-assignment module for long-horizon agentic RL that adds two environment-grounded process
signals on top of the usual discounted outcome return: a short-term signal reading the immediate
environment feedback string, and a medium-term signal detecting stagnation over a K-step window. Both are
extracted by pattern-matching environment-emitted text — no value critic, no learned reward model, no human
or preference annotation — and folded in as a clipped multiplicative reweighting of the base return rather
than as a new reward. Trained on Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B-Instruct over ALFWorld and WebShop, it reports an ALFWorld
overall score of 95.31 (1.5B) and 96.03 (7B) and the best WebShop Task Score under both backbones.*

**From the report**

> Core method — three timescales, one multiplier: the long-term signal is the standard discounted return; the two process signals are combined as C = α·c_fb + β·c_hist, turned into a step weight w = clip(1 + λ·C, w_min, w_max), and applied **multiplicatively** to the base return. "We do not replace the underlying optimizer or introduce an explicit value critic; instead, we modify the step-level supervision signal in a plug-and-play manner." — §3.1, §3.2, §3.5 (Eqs. 1, 2, 7, 8)
>
> Short-term signal is literal string matching on environment text: c_fb = −1 if any negative pattern matches the normalized feedback, +1 if any positive pattern matches, else 0. The ALFWorld negative set includes "nothing happens", "you are not carrying anything", "cannot", "not valid", "already"; the positive set includes "you open", "you pick up", "you put", "you clean", "you heat", "you cool". "The feedback pattern sets are constructed from environment-provided messages rather than from model-generated text… Ambiguous messages are left unmatched and therefore receive zero feedback credit." — §3.3, App. A.2, B.4
>
> Medium-term signal is a stagnation detector that is negative-only by design: over a window of the last K steps, c_hist = −1 if all K entries are negative, −η if the window contains no positive feedback at all, else 0. "Both cases are designed as negative corrections rather than additional positive rewards, which helps avoid over-shaping the environment objective." — §3.4, App. A.3
>
> Training recipe: Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B-Instruct backbones; ReAct-style think-then-act protocol; the agent keeps only the two most recent interaction steps in prompt memory while EFCA still computes credit from the full recorded trajectory logs; rollout group size 8 shared across all RL methods; ~250 training steps; 2×H100 train / 1×H100 eval at 1.5B, 4×H100 / 2×H100 at 7B. Swept: K ∈ {2,3,5}, η = 0.5, α ∈ {0.5–2.0}, β ∈ {0–1.6}, λ ∈ {0–0.8}, w clipped to roughly [0.2–0.3, 1.8–2.0]. — §4.1.3, App. B.2/B.3/B.5
>
> Headline ALFWorld: "With Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, EFCA reaches an overall ALFWorld score of 95.31, outperforming HGPO by +3.32 points, GiGPO by +4.43 points, and GraphGPO by +2.60 points. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, EFCA further improves the overall ALFWorld score to 96.03, exceeding HGPO by +4.04 points, GiGPO by +1.76 points, and GraphGPO by +0.72 points." Reference points in the same table: GPT-4o prompting 48.0, Gemini-2.5-Pro prompting 60.3, PPO 80.4, GRPO 83.33 (7B). — §4.2.1, Table 1
>
> WebShop splits the two axes and the paper concedes the loss on one: EFCA takes the best Task Score under both backbones (89.81 / 89.06) but on Task Success Rate reaches only 75.91 / 78.91 against GraphGPO's 78.65 / 80.31. "Although GraphGPO attains slightly higher WebShop task success rate, EFCA's stronger Task Score suggests better semantic adaptation to the shopping objective itself." — §4.2.1, Table 1
>
> Both process signals are load-bearing, the medium-term one slightly more so: from 95.31, "Removing the medium-term state-history signal lowers the score to 90.95 (−4.36)… Disabling the short-term feedback signal reduces the score to 91.40 (−3.91)." The stagnation coefficient is sharply non-monotone: β = 0 → 90.95, 0.4 → 91.67, 0.8 → 95.31, 1.2 → 93.42, 1.6 → 89.91 — an over-strong penalty scores below using no stagnation signal at all. Compute overhead is negative: 317.05 s per training step versus HGPO's 338.58 s (−6.36%), saving ~1.5 h over a 250-step run. — §4.3.2 (Fig. 2), App. B.5
>
> Stated limitation — the signal cannot tell productive detours from stagnation, and cannot create the evidence it needs: "real agentic tasks may contain richer temporal structure, such as subgoal completion, reversible recovery actions, delayed enabling actions, and exploration steps that are locally unproductive but globally necessary." And: "Environment-grounded credit can only refine supervision for evidence that appears in collected trajectories; it does not by itself guarantee that the agent will visit states with informative feedback." The paper also flags its pattern matching as surface-level, naming semantic feedback understanding as future work. — App. E

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3.4 and Appendix E back to back, because they describe the same object from both sides — the medium-term rule fires when a K-step window is all-negative or contains zero positives, and Appendix E then admits it cannot distinguish that from "exploration steps that are locally unproductive but globally necessary." That is precisely the discrimination my over-reflection work needs: a confirm-then-keep-searching tail and a genuinely hard multi-hop query both look like K rounds of no new evidence, and this paper hands me the detector while telling me it does not separate the two cases. §3.5 for the functional form rather than the scores: the process signal enters as a clipped multiplier on the base return, so it can only redistribute magnitude among steps that already carry nonzero return — my own inference (the paper does not spell out its per-step reward structure) is that on a wholly failed trajectory under terminal-only reward the weight multiplies zero and does nothing, which would mean the multiplicative form structurally cannot fix the second failure mode the paper itself names, useful actions inside failed trajectories being suppressed. Worth checking before porting. Appendix B.4 with B.5 prices the port: the entire process signal is a hand-curated set of environment-emitted strings, conservative by default, and it costs −6.36% wall clock rather than an extra forward pass. And read the β sweep before believing the headline — β=1.6 scores 89.91, *below* the 90.95 you get from turning the stagnation signal off entirely, and the λ sweep tops out at 93.03 against a headline of 95.31, so the sweep configuration evidently differs from the headline configuration, which the paper never states.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the closest match at the mechanism level. My note measures confirm-then-keep-searching at 63% and asks for state-conditioned stop/pivot RL; the medium-term state-history credit is a working, no-annotation implementation of exactly the *trigger* half — a K-step window with all-negative or zero-positive environment feedback, worth −4.36 ALFWorld points when removed. It is not a stop action: it applies soft downward pressure on the return of steps inside an unproductive window rather than deciding when to stop, pivot, or answer, which leaves my open question open but supplies the state feature it would condition on. **AgentPlanet** — connects through reward-channel integrity. EFCA takes its entire process signal from the world term (environment-emitted text) with no learned critic and no human label, then defends the channel with two design choices worth copying into an anti-Goodhart gate: the medium-term component is negative-only, explicitly "designed as negative corrections rather than additional positive rewards, which helps avoid over-shaping the environment objective", and the weight is clipped so process credit can never override the outcome term. It also cuts the other way as a warning about world authoring as a learnable role — whoever writes the environment's feedback strings writes the reward, so a hand-curated pattern set is exactly the hackable surface a gate would have to inspect. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — an adjacent lever on the same economics, and I should be precise that it is not the same one: EFCA is on-policy with no replay buffer; it extracts more *supervision* per rollout by mining per-step environment feedback that outcome-only training discards, rather than more *gradient* per rollout by replaying it. The genuine bridge is that the prompt memory keeps only two steps while credit is computed from the full recorded trajectory logs, so the process signal is trajectory-local and recomputable offline — meaning it could in principle be recomputed on replayed rollouts, and at −6.36% wall clock it is nearly free either way. **Post-cutoff distillation** — partial, shared shape with a different gate: both replace a human or learned annotator with a *mechanical provenance test* to manufacture a dense signal on top of a base objective — I gate a per-token reward to knowledge the student could not already have had, EFCA gates per-step credit to text the environment actually emitted, with ambiguous messages deliberately scoring zero. Both inherit the same vulnerability, and Appendix E concedes its gate is surface-level pattern matching. The domain (embodied household tasks and simulated shopping) is not one my notes cover, so I carry across the credit-construction mechanism only.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the whole shaping term is portable in an afternoon and I would try it as a bounded multiplier rather than a bonus — w = clip(1 + λ(α·c_fb + β·c_hist), 0.2, 2.0) on the existing step return. Their per-sweep best points are α=1.5 and β=0.8 (each peaking at 95.31) and λ=0.4 (peaking at 93.03 in a separate sweep), with K over {2,3,5} and η=0.5; note those three bests come from different runs and the paper never states the configuration behind its headline table. For a search agent c_fb comes off tool-response provenance — new evidence / zero-hit / repeat of an already-seen document — and c_hist fires on a K-step window with no new evidence, which keeps the outcome verifier authoritative while pushing directly against the over-reflection tail. The sharp open question: their stagnation channel cannot separate a wasted confirm-then-keep-searching tail from a legitimately slow hard query, and the multiplicative form is inert wherever the base return is zero. So the case I most want penalized — the answer found early inside an eventually *successful* trajectory, then twenty rounds that change nothing — is the case where a multiplier on a nonzero return actually bites, while a hard query mid-solve is where it will misfire. Which dominates is empirical, and the paper does not measure it.

[Source (arXiv 2608.08255)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08255)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Cognitive-structured Multimodal Agent for Multimodal Understanding, Generation, and Editing</strong> · Peking University + WeChat Vision, Tencent, July 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2607.08497v1, submitted 9 Jul 2026; cs.CV, 16 pages, CC BY 4.0) proposing a modular
multimodal agent that stops feeding every historical image back into the context window and instead
externalizes each image into an Episodic Visual Memory, retrieving only the episodes a given turn needs.
The system splits into three Qwen3-VL-8B modules — a Perceptual Abstraction Engine that writes each image
to memory as a JSON entry, a Cognitive Retrieval Engine that selects relevant episode indices, and an
executive controller that classifies and dispatches the turn — trained with a staged SFT + DAPO-RL
pipeline on programmatically generated multi-turn dialogues carrying turn-level retrieval labels. Headline
claim: an 8B agent reaches 91.4% turn-level retrieval accuracy over 20-turn sessions on the authors' own
M2CA-Bench, +8.2 points over a 32B multi-agent baseline.*

**From the report**

> Core mechanism: an episode is a single image at a dialogue turn, compressed into "a structured entry (tags, description, thumbnail) written to the Episodic Visual Memory (EVM)", so visual content "persists without occupying the dialogue context window"; only retrieved episodes are forwarded to the executor, "keeping per-turn visual-token consumption bounded regardless of dialogue length." — §1, §3.2, Fig. 2
>
> The retrieval interface makes doing nothing a legal, explicit action: "CoRE receives a list of candidate thumbnails aligned with candidate indices, textual memory entries containing tags and descriptions, and the current user query. It outputs an image-index list, **with an empty list indicating that no historical image is needed**. This interface is shared by SFT, RL, and evaluation." — App. A
>
> Training recipe (it is trained, not a training-free scaffold): three stages on a Qwen3-VL-8B backbone — (1) SFT of the retriever on 270 sessions × 20 turns = 5,400 turns (30 val sessions / 600 turns); (2) DAPO RL on the retriever with reward = difficulty-weighted Jaccard between predicted and ground-truth retrieval sets, weight 1.2 on hard/very_hard turns, with the convention r = 1 when both sets are empty (correct abstention) and r = 0 when exactly one is empty, plus soft length penalties; (3) DAPO RL on the memory *writer* with the reader frozen, rewarding memory entries by retrieval accuracy "rather than optimizing traditional captioning metrics (CIDEr, BERTScore)". RL sample filtering deliberately emphasizes turns the baseline got wrong, hard/very_hard turns, and negative samples requiring no retrieval, explicitly "mitigating shortcut learning". — §3.3, Table 2
>
> Headline retrieval numbers: 91.4% / 89.4% / 82.0% on English Full/Medium/Hard and 89.6% / 85.6% / 79.0% on Chinese, against a Multi-Agent Baseline-32B at 83.2 / 79.4 / 72.1 (EN); the unified model BAGEL reaches only 23.3% on EN Full and "collapses to below 3% on Hard". The gap over the 8B multi-agent baseline widens from +9.6% on Full to +14.2% Medium to +18.4% Hard, and the Full-to-Hard drop is 9.4% versus 18.2%. — §5.3, Table 3
>
> Staged-training ablation, English Hard subset: 63.6% (multi-agent baseline 8B) → 70.9% (+SFT on the retriever, +7.3) → 77.4% (+RL on the retriever, +6.5) → 82.0% (+RL on the writer, +4.6) — i.e. training the memory **writer** against a frozen reader is still worth 4.6 points after reader-side RL is exhausted. Text-only memory is insufficient by their own ablation: MemoryLLM-style 24.4% and ReasoningBank-style 39.0% on Hard against 82.0% with thumbnails. — §5.5, Tables 5 and 8
>
> Eval setup: M2CA-Bench = 100 held-out sessions × 20 turns = 2,000 annotated turns in EN and CN, generated with random seeds and topics disjoint from training, with hard negatives injected (near-duplicate confounder images and no-retrieval-needed queries) "to prevent shortcut learning". Subsets are Full (all 20 turns), Medium (turns 11–20), Hard (very_hard within turns 11–20). Context Usage Accuracy is "turn-level exact-match accuracy of the retrieved image index"; generation quality is a per-turn 0–10 Gemini-3-Pro score. "All methods use identical scenario sessions, turn annotations, retrieval targets, and post-processing rules." — §3.4, §5.2
>
> The label supply comes from authoring the world: a simulator samples a topic and emits the next query plus the ground-truth retrieval set and a difficulty tag; a zero-shot multi-agent system produces the candidate answer; a "GT-justify" step verifies retrieval correctness and corrects mismatches; the verified answer is fed back for the next turn. The topic ontology is 55 topics across eight domains, and flows are synthesized with structural inductive biases such as "at least 3 long-range callbacks with span >=8 turns". — §3.4, Fig. 3
>
> Caveats: on editing, the 32B multi-agent baseline is ahead on English Edit (7.49 vs 7.31); the paper lists "joint optimization, open-domain extension, and richer modalities" as future work, i.e. the modules are trained separately and the setting is not open-domain; and although the whole design is sold on bounding visual tokens, **no token count or reduction ratio is reported anywhere** — the efficiency claims are qualitative plus a wall-clock proxy, and that 23.1 s → 12.7 s row is a 32B-vs-32B comparison while the headline 91.4% is the 8B model. — §5.4, §6, Table 6, App. D

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the domain (image generation and editing) is not one my notes cover, so I would read this purely for §3.3 — the reward definition is the smallest concrete instantiation I have seen of making "do nothing" a first-class rewarded action: difficulty-weighted Jaccard with r = 1 when prediction and ground truth are both empty and r = 0 when exactly one is empty, so correct abstention scores exactly as high as correct retrieval and a spurious action is punished symmetrically. Table 5 is the number I actually want, because it separates SFT, RL on the reader, and RL on the writer, and the Hard-subset deltas (+7.3 / +6.5 / +4.6) say that training the component which *writes* context is still worth 4.6 points after reader-side RL has been exhausted — that is the evidence I would need before spending compute on training a summarizer rather than only the model that consumes the summary. §3.4 is worth reading for how cheaply the labels come out: because the generator authored the scenario, every turn ships with its ground-truth retrieval set for free, and a GT-justify step re-verifies and corrects before the turn is fed back — 5,400 SFT turns is a tiny corpus to be reaching 91.4% from, which says label density matters more than volume here. And check Table 6 against the abstract before citing anything: the 12.7 s row is the 32B configuration at 92.0% Full, not the 8B model at 91.4%, so the paper's headline sentence pairs an 8B accuracy with a 32B latency measurement.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the tightest link. My confirm-then-keep-searching problem is an agent that will not stop acting; this paper trains the opposite behavior explicitly rather than hoping it emerges: the retrieval interface makes an empty list a legal output meaning "no historical image is needed", the reward scores correct abstention as a full 1.0 and a one-sided miss as 0.0, and the RL mix deliberately oversamples negative turns that require no retrieval to block shortcut learning. That is a directly portable recipe for state-conditioned stop/pivot — give the no-action decision its own slot in the output contract, reward it symmetrically, and oversample the cases where it is correct. **AgentPlanet** — the scenario engine is a working instance of the world-authoring term: a simulator samples the initial state (55 topics across eight domains) and the rules (structural inductive biases like at least three long-range callbacks with span ≥ 8 turns), rolls a trajectory inside it, and gets ground-truth labels for free because it authored the world — authoring supplies the verifier. Two contrasts worth noting for my factorization: the roles here are split across a fleet (simulator, answerer, GT-justify verifier) rather than one model playing all three, and their anti-Goodhart gates are concrete — GT-justify correction before feedback, near-duplicate confounders, no-retrieval negatives, and train/eval splits disjoint by seed *and* topic. **Post-cutoff distillation** — same structural move on reward gating, different content: they refuse to reward the memory writer on surface similarity, explicitly rejecting CIDEr and BERTScore, and instead gate its reward to downstream retrieval success against a *frozen* reader, which is the same discipline as gating a distillation reward to the effect you want rather than the form. The frozen-reader-as-reward-channel is also a clean credit-assignment pattern for a writer/reader pair where only the reader's success is measurable. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a stretch, and I label it one: no off-policy reuse, no replay buffer, no update-to-data ratio. The only cousin is the RL sample filter that keeps turns the baseline got wrong plus hard turns plus negatives, which is prioritization of which expensive data earns a gradient rather than reuse of it.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* train the component that *writes* context against the retrieval success of a frozen component that *reads* it, never against a text-similarity proxy — and note they price it at +4.6 points on the Hard subset after reader-side RL had already run. That maps straight onto a note-taker or summarizer in a search agent optimized against a frozen answerer instead of ROUGE. The open question this leaves: they claim the whole point is bounding visual tokens but never report a single token count or reduction ratio, only wall-clock, and that comparison is 32B-vs-32B. A memory scheme sold on context economics should publish the tokens-per-turn curve against dialogue length; without it, "bounded regardless of dialogue length" is asserted from architecture rather than measured.

[Source (arXiv 2607.08497)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08497)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>ResidencyRL: Reinforcement Learning in Simulated Clinical Environments</strong> · Google DeepMind + Google Research, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.07418, submitted 7 Aug 2026; cs.AI/cs.CL) describing an online multi-turn RL method
that trains a clinical dialogue agent entirely inside a simulated telehealth encounter: an LLM patient
simulator, a seven-instrument documentation and tool API, and a structured LLM-judge reward, over episodes
hard-capped at 60 dialogue turns plus 8 tool calls. Training runs GRPO from a Gemini 3.5 Flash
initialization over 57,453 generated scenarios, with a trajectory-level scalar reward in [−3, 3] produced
by eight partitioned judge calls. The headline claim is that procedural competence learned in simulation
transfers out of distribution: +7.0 pp adversarial diagnostic accuracy in-domain, a roughly one-third
relative cut in missed red flags, improvement on all six axes of a multi-visit benchmark, and an 87.6%
blinded-clinician preference. Co-authored with clinicians at Houston Methodist, Trinity Health, Stanford
Oncology Partners and St. Luke Hospital.*

**From the report**

> Environment and caps: episodes are hard-capped at 60 patient turns plus 8 structured tool calls ("T ≤ 68 actions"). The tool API exposes seven instruments — chart review, primary diagnosis, ranked differential with reasoning chains, urgency classification, management plan, patient-facing summary, SOAP documentation — plus a separate termination action. The patient is an LLM simulator with a behavioral layer of health-literacy adaptation, proactive-vs-reactive information asymmetry and ~50-word pacing; **tool calls, agent reasoning and the rubric are withheld from it** so it cannot confirm the agent's hypotheses. — Abstract, §3.2
>
> Reward is rubric + LLM judge + rule-based, all three. R = R_primary − R_penalty in [−3, 3]. R_primary scores six dimensions over 26 Likert sub-axes, weighted 2/9 diagnosis, 3/9 management and 1/9 each for intake, communication, documentation and style. Rather than one monolithic judge prompt, **the rubric is partitioned into eight topic groups, each scored by its own judge call under structured-output constraints and dispatched concurrently** — justified by psychometric evidence that analytic scoring beats holistic scoring on inter-rater consistency. — §3.3, Table 2, Eq. 1
>
> Penalties are where red flags and adversarial conditions enter: binary critical flags for hallucinated clinical information (2.0), contraindicated actions (3.0), management-plan leakage (2.0), catch-all safety violation (1.0); adversarial scenarios enable four more (missed critical screening questions 2.0, scenario-gate contraindications 3.0, under-triage 3.0, missed safety red flags 3.0). Deterministic system feedback adds 0.5 per action-parsing failure, 0.5 for a missing termination signal, and a piecewise-linear length penalty ramping from 0.1 at 30 conversational turns to 1.0 at 40; total penalty clamped at 3.0. Without the turn-length control "the agent defaults to exhaustive symptom enumeration rather than hypothesis-driven questioning." — §3.3
>
> RL recipe: GRPO from a Gemini 3.5 Flash initialization, advantages estimated from a group of trajectories with no value function; 49,870 telehealth scenarios over 81 conditions + 5,000 targeted history-taking + 2,583 adversarial = 57,453 (the paper states the mixture as 83/15/2%, which does not match its own counts of 86.8/8.7/4.5%). Training dynamics: median encounter length grows from ~18 to 23 turns by step 100; intake completeness is weakest at init and improves fastest, rising rapidly over the first 75 steps before plateauing; documentation saturates immediately; communication shows no plateau by end of training. — §3.4, Fig. 4
>
> Headline 1, with its denominator: adversarial diagnostic accuracy — the share of encounters scoring ≥4/5 on the diagnostic rubric — rises 81.0% [75.5, 86.0] → 88.0% [83.5, 92.0] on n=200 held-out adversarial scenarios per arm with 95% bootstrap CIs. On standard telehealth the same metric moves only 86.4% → 88.4%. Crucially this is the **in-domain** set, scored by "the same automated rubric pipeline that served as the training reward signal." — §4.1, Table 4
>
> Headline 2, and it is a *relative* reduction: missed red flag rate falls 45.5% [39.0, 52.5] → 31.5% [25.5, 38.0] on the same arm — i.e. −14.0 pp, which is the ~31% relative figure quoted in the abstract, not a 31-point drop. Missed critical question rate falls 65.5% → 43.5%. Contraindicated actions fall 21.5% → 16.5% with overlapping CIs, and under-triage is unchanged (4.0% vs 4.5%). "The residual failure rates indicate room for further improvement." — §4.1, Table 4
>
> Headline 3, with its blinding protocol: 87.6% preference is on Overall Impression across n=97 side-by-side comparisons (100 sampled, 3 excluded) — 87.6% trained vs 10.3% baseline vs 2.1% ties, mean +1.21 on a [−2, +2] scale. Encounters were presented to board-certified physicians in randomized, anonymized order as "Model A" and "Model B" across eight dimensions. Both arms ran inside the *identical* expert-optimized telehealth scaffold originally tuned on the base model, so the gain is additive on top of expert agentic engineering. Strongest axis: completeness of information gathering, 90.7%; weakest: Accuracy/No-Hallucinations, 77.3% ties, mean +0.07, not significant. — §4.5, Fig. 8
>
> Transfer evals and the caveats the paper states itself: the multi-visit benchmark improves on all six axes (management reasoning 80.07 → 88.41, communication 83.69 → 92.19, and diagnosis & management 90.37 → 90.68 at p=0.213, n.s.); AgentClinic-MedQA 81.4 → 85.6 (p=0.176, n.s.) and MIMIC-IV 53.5 → 60.0 (p=0.079); CRAFT-MD gains on 4 of 5 sub-metrics, none individually significant. The paper concedes that the in-domain results "primarily validate that the agent successfully learns to optimize its training signals"; that the autorater "illustrates a form of Goodhart's law" with systematically positive deltas (mean +0.63 overall across the 97 matched cases, staying positive even on cases clinicians did not prefer — non-preferred subset median +0.31 vs +0.61 preferred, p<0.01) and metric saturation; that adversarial evals "used automated adversarial agents rather than human actors"; that only English-speaking, US-based patients were simulated; and that "whether simulation-trained competencies transfer reliably to real patient care remains an open question." — §4.2, §4.4, §5, App. C.7

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3.3 and Table 2 first, for the reward geometry rather than the medicine — an eight-way partitioned judge, one structured-output call per criteria group dispatched concurrently, is a directly portable answer to the quality-masking problem I hit whenever one rubric prompt scores tone and factual grounding in the same breath; here empathy scoring literally cannot dilute a management-safety flag because they are different calls with different artifacts in context. Read the penalty block alongside the Figure 4 dynamics as a two-sided stopping mechanism: they fight premature closure with intake-completeness reward and fight the opposite failure with a piecewise-linear length penalty ramping 0.1 at 30 turns to 1.0 at 40, and the reported behavior is that median length rises 18 → 23 and the policy consistently pushes against this soft limit. That is a scalar-penalty implementation of the stop decision — cheap, and worth comparing against a learned state-conditioned stop. Read the §4.1 preamble with Appendix C.7 as a template for honest rubric-reward reporting: they pre-emptively discount their own headline in-domain numbers and then quantify the judge's bias. And read Appendix E for the rollout-reuse question specifically — they sampled 5 trajectories from the top-10% highest-reward rollouts and prepended them as fixed few-shot context; it beat zero-shot on 3 of 4 axes but lost to RL on all of them and produced longer, lower-reward encounters, while *untrained* demonstrations were worse than no demonstrations at all.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the cleanest published instance of that factorization I have seen outside my own note. The world-authoring term is a four-stage pipeline over initial state and rules (demographics, hidden facts, adversarial addendum, ground-truth differential); the policy term is the GRPO-trained agent; the world-model/transition term is the patient simulator. And the role-sharing is literal: the same base model plays both the patient simulator and the initialization of the policy. It also supplies concrete anti-Goodhart gates a world builder must pass — an accept/modify/reject coherence judge over five dimensions, TF-IDF dedup at cosine 0.9 to block memorization, a stricter 0.45 similarity filter to decontaminate the eval set — plus an explicit reward-channel-integrity audit in the Goodhart appendix. **Over-reflection in search agents** — my failure mode inverted, which makes it the useful control. Their headline pathology is *premature* closure: the base model terminates an encounter after five exchanges and then fabricates a history it never elicited. So they pay reward to keep gathering, and then must add a length penalty to stop the agent degenerating into exhaustive symptom enumeration. Both my confirm-then-keep-searching pattern and their stop-too-early pattern are the same missing state-conditioned stop rule, and this paper shows what happens when you attack it with a hand-tuned scalar from the other side. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — supported as a cost exhibit and a partial negative result, not a solution. Their rollouts are about as expensive as agent rollouts get (up to 60 simulator calls plus 8 judge calls per trajectory, K episodes per scenario) and the recipe is strictly on-policy GRPO with no value function and no replay — every trajectory used once. The only reuse attempted is the few-shot recycling above, which loses to RL on all four axes, leaving my open question exactly where it was: nobody here tried off-policy gradient reuse of the discarded rollouts. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a stretch, and only on the SFT-versus-RL half: their claim that "unlike supervised fine-tuning, which would require curated expert trajectories, RL discovers these strategies through its own exploration", backed by untrained demonstrations actively degrading performance below zero-shot, is evidence about when behavioral exposure substitutes for weight updates. It does not touch my per-token reward in student vocabulary — their reward is a single trajectory-level scalar.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the partitioned analytic judge, worth porting wholesale to a search-agent rubric reward — split the rubric into topic groups, give each its own structured-output judge call with *only* the artifacts that group needs, and dispatch concurrently. Their stated motive is psychometric, and the practical payoff is that quality on one axis cannot mask failure on another. The pairing with hard binary flags carrying fixed magnitudes (hallucination 2.0, contraindicated action 3.0, clamped total 3.0) is a cleaner separation of graded quality from disqualifying failure than the single blended score I usually see. The sharp open question: they concede the autorater is Goodharted and then argue that consistent out-of-domain transfer is "evidence against pure reward hacking" — while warning in the same section that external grounding is needed "to prevent convergence to shared blind spots". So is OOD transfer actually a sufficient certificate against reward hacking, or does it only prove the judge's blind spots are correlated across benchmarks? Note their only *quantified* human preference panel (n=97) is itself run inside the simulator; the second human check, a blinded oncologist review of 100 cases, is reported only as narrative observations with no numbers.

[Source (arXiv 2608.07418)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07418)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>DeepVoyager-VL: Incentivizing Vision-in-the-Loop Search for Long-Horizon Multimodal Agents</strong> · PKU + HKUST(GZ) + NUDT + OUC + HITSZ + Huawei Cloud, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.01827v1, submitted 3 Aug 2026; cs.CV/cs.AI) that trains multimodal deep-search
agents so images acquired mid-search drive further retrieval, instead of appearing only at the input or
the answer. It couples a structure-before-language synthesis pipeline — a multimodal event graph over
~3.78M atomic event records aggregated into 30,337 macro-events, from which ~7K long-horizon tasks with
intermediate visual dependencies are composed — with an agent interface that separates cheap image
*discovery* (URL–caption references) from expensive image *observation* (explicit fetch and crop actions),
then fine-tunes Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B on 27,180 trajectories using supervised fine-tuning
alone. Headline claim: trajectory SFT with no RL stage raises the 30B-A3B backbone's ten-benchmark average
from 40.7 (same agentic workflow, untrained) to 58.6, and the 8B backbone from 35.3 to 54.8.*

**From the report**

> Vision-in-the-loop is defined formally, not rhetorically: a trajectory qualifies if some newly acquired visual observation resolves a variable required by a *later* action, and the task must additionally satisfy counterfactual visual necessity — the answer set is a singleton with the retrieved images and larger than one without them. — §3.1, Eqs. 2–3
>
> Token budgeting is the interface: "Search exposes lightweight URL–caption references; FetchImage materializes a selected image, and CropImage creates a reusable local observation. Thus, candidates add only references, while visual tokens enter policy context through explicit fetch or crop actions." A visual register persists observations across turns; before page-visit and image-search outputs reach the policy, a helper VLM keeps only goal-relevant conclusions and source cues — on the page-visit path the appendix names Qwen3-VL-Plus and caps extraction at 15 image references, while reverse image search returns one of four discrete match verdicts. — §3.3, App. B.2
>
> Synthesis scale and authoring gates: ~3.78M atomic event records aggregate into 30,337 macro-events, from which the pipeline builds ~7K multimodal search tasks. Programs are built backward from a visual endpoint under **constraint minimality** (the answer set is a singleton under the full constraint set but larger when any single constraint is dropped) and an explicit **anti-leak rule** requiring the retrieval cues used to relocate an image to be disjoint from any alias of the answer, with validators for program completeness, leakage, duplicates, image–text alignment and counterfactual removal. — §3.2, App. B.1
>
> Curation cascade: an 8B model answers each candidate three times with no tools and the question is discarded if any attempt is correct; survivors get eight tool-augmented rollouts, and 7–8/8 correct is labeled easy and removed while medium and hard are kept. "These rollouts are used only to estimate question difficulty: the questions are retained, but the probe trajectories themselves are never included in the supervised corpus" — every retained question is regenerated by a stronger teacher and kept only if a judge marks the final answer correct. — §3.4, App. B.3
>
> Training recipe: 27,180 trajectories, 4 epochs, 64 GPUs across 8 nodes, global batch 64 / micro-batch 1, max sequence length 131,072 with packing, LR warmed over 5% to 2e-5 then cosine-decayed to 5e-7; packing yields 3,186 sequences and just **199 optimizer steps**, with no validation split and the final checkpoint used as-is. Loss is masked to assistant reasoning, tool calls and answers only; the vision encoder and multimodal merger stay frozen and there is no RL stage. — §3.4, App. C.2
>
> Headline numbers: the 30B-A3B model goes 20.7 (direct answer) → 40.7 (agentic workflow) → 58.6 after trajectory SFT, and the 8B model 17.5 → 35.3 → 54.8; best on nine of ten benchmarks among scale-matched open agents, and eight of ten for the 8B. Gains concentrate on search-intensive sets — the 30B-A3B model gains +10.3 on average over the first four benchmarks versus +23.1 over the six from BrowseComp-VL onward (8B: +13.8 and +23.4), with the largest single deltas +22.6, +23.7 and +30.7. Under the same agentic workflow, proprietary baselines average 67.2, 65.0 and 64.7, leaving the 58.6 open model 6.1–8.6 points short. — §4.2, Table 1
>
> Eval harness: every agent evaluated in their environment gets at most 50 interaction turns; correctness is scored by LLM-as-judge using a fixed judge prompt and one judge model across all benchmarks; retrieval is a commercial search API, page access a commercial reader, and retrieved images must be materialized into multimodal context. Prior specialized agents' numbers are transcribed as self-reported under their own settings, with unreported pairs left as "–" rather than imputed. The data ablation is cumulative: 20K public multi-source trajectories raise the three-benchmark average by 21.4 and 19.2 points, and adding the 7K vision-in-the-loop trajectories adds a further 5.4 and 6.5. — §4.1, §4.3, App. C.1–C.2
>
> Stated caveat: "the memory remains cumulative and trajectory-local. It does not learn which observations should be retained, consolidated, compressed, or discarded, nor does it explicitly organize evidence according to source, subtask, or confidence. As the interaction horizon grows, redundant or low-value observations may therefore accumulate, and relevant evidence may become increasingly difficult for the policy to locate within its context." Two further caveats: goal-conditioned summarization sits on the interaction path and its quality/latency/cost trade-off is not characterized; and the work is answer-oriented search rather than full research, not assessing open-ended decomposition, hypothesis formation, or reconciling conflicting sources. — App. D.1

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* App. B.3 first — the probe model and the supervision model are deliberately different (a small model does the three tool-free attempts and the eight difficulty rollouts, a stronger teacher regenerates every retained trajectory), and the easy bucket is dropped outright rather than downweighted, which is a cleaner filter/supervision split than I usually build. §3.3 with the tool table is the transplantable mechanism: search returns only URL–caption references and fetch/crop are the *only* actions that put pixels into policy context, so the expensive modality is budgeted by an explicit policy decision rather than by tool-return volume — and the same discovery/observability split generalizes to any costly observation, full page text or giant tool dumps included, not just images. App. C.2 is worth reading for the regime it admits: 27,180 trajectories pack into 3,186 sequences and 199 optimizer steps over four epochs, with no validation split and the final step used as-is — a very small-step SFT budget and an honest no-checkpoint-selection statement I can compare directly against my own step counts. And Figure 3's design is the ablation I want to copy: roll out 1,000 queries per source with one fixed model, framework and tool environment across four datasets, so a behavioral difference (64.3% versus 40.6/30.3/10.1% visual tool calls; 16–20 versus 1–5 and 6–10 turn peaks) is attributable to the *data* rather than to the model or the harness.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the synthesis pipeline is the world-authoring term made deterministic and separable from the policy: it materializes millions of event records into macro-events, extracts subgraphs as inference programs, and only then verbalizes them, and its acceptance gates are precisely the anti-Goodhart gates the note says a world builder must pass — retrieval cues disjoint from answer aliases, a singleton answer set with every constraint individually necessary, and counterfactual removal to prove the answer is unrecoverable without the retrieved images. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — the paper burns 11 rollouts per question (3 tool-free probes + 8 tool-augmented) purely to estimate difficulty and then explicitly excludes every one from the supervised corpus, regenerating each survivor with a stronger teacher. It defends the discard as noise control and never tests reuse, which is exactly the sunk cost my note is about. **Over-reflection in search agents** — the paper treats a longer horizon as the *objective* (its data peaks at 16–20 assistant turns under a 50-turn cap, against 1–5 and 6–10 for three public sets) but never measures whether the later turns change the answer, and its own limitation section concedes the cumulative visual memory lets redundant or low-value observations accumulate with no learned retention or eviction — the same missing stop/pivot criterion, arrived at from the opposite direction. **Post-cutoff distillation** — the tool-free Pass@3 filter (three attempts with no tools, question discarded if any is correct) is a capability gate of the same shape as gating a distillation signal to knowledge the student could not already have had, though this is off-policy teacher-trajectory SFT with a token-level loss mask rather than on-policy distillation with a per-token reward in the student's own vocabulary.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the two-line authoring gate is cheap, deterministic, and I would port it as-is — require that the answer set be a singleton under the full constraint set but larger when any single constraint is dropped (so every constraint is load-bearing), and require the retrieval cue used to relocate a piece of evidence to be disjoint from every alias of the answer (so the cue cannot leak the answer). The sharp open question it leaves: 11 discarded probe rollouts per question is a large sunk cost, and the paper asserts rather than tests that excluding them beats reusing them — as rejection-sampled positives, or as negatives — alongside the stronger-teacher regeneration.

[Source (arXiv 2608.01827)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01827)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>PolyWorkBench: Benchmarking Multilingual Long-Horizon LLM Agents</strong> · Beijing Jiaotong University + Weixin AI, Tencent, July 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2607.06008, v1 7 Jul 2026 / v2 9 Jul 2026; cs.AI/cs.CL, 15 pages) presenting a benchmark
of 67 hand-authored long-horizon workplace tasks across five domains (commerce, knowledge work, legal,
localization, manufacturing) in which an agent must read a heterogeneous multilingual input bundle, run a
multi-step tool-using workflow, and emit structured deliverable files. Its defining design choice is that
instruction language, source-material language and expected-output language are deliberately allowed to
differ within a single task — 59 of 67 tasks (88%) span three or more languages across those roles, over a
ten-language corpus. Scoring is a three-axis hybrid (rule-based Grade, executable Pytest, LLM-as-Judge),
and the headline claim is that frontier agents degrade substantially once language variation is embedded
in the execution trajectory rather than isolated to a translation step.*

**From the report**

> Task construction is fully manual with an executable admission gate: each task is authored in three stages — native-language source collection ("Machine translation is never used to produce a source-language file"), instruction and end-to-end reference authoring where ground-truth facts are "back-injected into the source files as verifiable anchors", and evaluation-asset creation — after which authors run the task with ≥3 agents/harnesses and finalize it "only if (a) the deterministic evaluators separate correct from incorrect trajectories reliably, (b) every ground-truth fact is discoverable from the provided inputs, and (c) no instruction can be satisfied by string-copying from the source without cross-lingual reasoning", with failures revised or discarded rather than kept. — §3.2
>
> Scale and horizon: 67 tasks split into a baseline pool (29 tasks, 6–8 estimated steps) and a stress pool (38 tasks, 8–12 steps), difficulty 3–6 on a 1–6 scale, mean 8.5 estimated tool-use steps per task, per-task budgets of 30–60 minutes, 9.3 input files per task on average, and 3.9 output artefacts per task (262 total). — §3.3
>
> Mixed-language design: "Coverage is deliberately trilingual by construction: instruction, source materials, and expected output are drawn from potentially different languages, so cross-lingual transfer is exercised throughout the trajectory rather than at a single translation step." 59/67 tasks (88%) involve three or more distinct languages across those roles; English is touched by 66 tasks, Arabic by 1, and Chinese/Korean/Japanese/Vietnamese each by 21–51. — §3.3
>
> Grading is a hybrid of deterministic and judged: Grade (task-specific structural rules with partial credit) is the primary leaderboard metric, with Pass@1 the mean Grade over all 67 tasks in a single run and Pass@3 a second column; Pytest verifies "file formats, numerical correctness, schema validity, API outputs, program behaviour" where deterministic verification is possible; the judge covers "coherence, completeness, faithfulness to the task instruction, and overall output quality". — §3.4, §3.5
>
> Harness is a first-class variable: 18 model × harness pairs over four standardized harnesses sharing identical inputs, a 1,800-second per-task timeout, and evaluation protocol; "the choice of harness moves scores by 8–21 Pass@1 points on the same underlying model" — the top model spans 0.921 → 0.712 across two harnesses, a 0.209 range. The paper's own conclusion: "Reporting a model's benchmark score without disclosing the harness is therefore not meaningful." — §4.1–§4.3
>
> Headline leaderboard: best entry 0.921 Pass@1 / 0.927 Pass@3; "only three entries exceed 0.79; every other model falls below 0.77"; and "No model is within a few points of ceiling on all five domains simultaneously" — strong models hold 0.85–0.90 on Knowledge/Legal/Manufacturing while collapsing to 0.63 and 0.50 on Commerce, where a single arithmetic or format error voids the entire deliverable. Sampling headroom splits by tier: the top entry gains only +0.007 from best-of-three while mid-tier entries gain up to +0.206, so "a substantial fraction of their Pass@1 failures are therefore transient stochastic errors rather than fundamental capability gaps." — §4.2, §4.3, §A.1
>
> Language spread scales inversely with capability: the best-minus-worst per-language mean Grade for a fixed model is 0.14 for the top entry (0.822 → 0.963) but 0.60 for a mid-tier open model (0.352 → 0.951); "The gap between the best and worst per-language mean of a fixed model can exceed 30 Grade points — larger than the overall Pass@1 differences between adjacent leaderboard rows." Two named failure modes: pure comprehension errors (dominating the Russian and Spanish drops *for mid-tier open models*), and cross-lingual coordination errors where "comprehension is correct but the agent fails to keep source-language content and target-language output aligned across a multi-step trajectory, so the final artefact drifts even though every intermediate step looked plausible." — §4.3, §A.2
>
> The caveat the paper states about its own judged axis: across 1,206 (entry, task) observations Grade and Pytest agree at r=0.85 but the judge correlates only r=0.18 with Grade and r=0.13 with Pytest, and "if we restrict attention to tasks where the deterministic evaluators indicate at least partial success (Grade ≥ 0.5, 81.6% of the population), the Grade–Judge correlation collapses to r = −0.04"; the judge is bimodal (60.1% ≥ 0.8, 22.5% ≤ 0.5, 15.4% exactly 0) and "even conditional on Grade = 1.0, mean Judge is 0.70", so "Judge is therefore not a suitable ranking metric on its own, but it is the only component sensitive to the semantic degradations that pass every deterministic check." The appendices frame the low correlation as "the design goal, not a metric failure". — §4.3, §A.3–§A.4

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the §3.2 admission gate is the part I would actually run — authoring a task, then screening it with ≥3 agent/harness pairs and discarding it unless the deterministic evaluator separates good from bad trajectories *and* no instruction is satisfiable by string-copying, is a cheap executable filter I could apply to synthesized tasks rather than trusting a generator's own self-report. I want §4.3's evaluation-consistency section and its appendices as a template for auditing any judge before it becomes a reward: conditioning on the deterministic score being ≥0.5 and watching the judge correlation collapse to r = −0.04, plus 70 cases of fluent-but-numerically-wrong output scoring judge ≥0.8, is a directly reusable measurement of a channel that rewards presentation over correctness. The harness-sensitivity result is the number I would cite in any eval-hygiene argument — 8–21 Pass@1 points from scaffold alone on a fixed model; by my own arithmetic on their leaderboard that dwarfs the 0.002–0.004 gaps between adjacent rows, though the paper makes that adjacent-row comparison about the *language* spread, not the harness. And the Pass@1-versus-Pass@3 split tells me where best-of-N buys anything: it separates stochastic near-misses from real capability ceilings, which is exactly the discrimination I need before deciding whether a failed rollout is worth keeping.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the closest match. §3.2 is a hand-run version of the world-authoring role with an explicit anti-Goodhart gate: ground-truth facts are back-injected into the source files as verifiable anchors so a correct trajectory must recover them exactly, and a task is admitted only after ≥3 agent/harness pairs demonstrate the deterministic evaluator actually separates correct from incorrect trajectories. The reward-integrity half is measured rather than asserted — r = −0.04 between Grade and Judge within solved tasks, plus 70 pairs scoring judge ≥0.8 on artefacts that fail the arithmetic or schema check, is a quantified case of a scoring channel a policy could optimize away from the intended behavior. **Over-reflection in search agents** — mechanism-level, not domain-level: their cross-lingual coordination errors are defined as cases where comprehension is correct but the agent fails to keep source content and target output aligned across a multi-step trajectory, "so the final artefact drifts even though every intermediate step looked plausible." That is the same shape as confirm-then-keep-going — correct state reached early, then eroded by subsequent steps that each look locally fine. Horizons differ though: 8.5 mean tool-use steps here is far shorter than the many-round searching in my traces. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — the sampling-headroom result is direct evidence for the premise. Mid-tier entries recover 14–21 Grade points from three samples while the frontier entry gains +0.007, and the paper concludes those failures are transient stochastic errors rather than capability gaps. Under a 1,800-second-per-task budget that is a large pool of expensive near-miss rollouts discarded by single-sample evaluation — precisely what an off-policy replay scheme would want to reuse. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a stretch, and I label it one: no distillation, RL or post-training happens here. The only shared mechanism is the gating idea — admission criteria (b) every ground-truth fact is discoverable from the provided inputs and (c) no instruction is satisfiable by string-copying are structurally the same move as gating a per-token reward to knowledge the student could not already have had: certify that the credited behavior could not have been produced by the shortcut path.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the two-line judge audit, portable to any verifier I build — (1) condition on the deterministic score being ≥0.5 and recompute the judge correlation; if it collapses toward zero, the judge is measuring a different axis and must never be summed into a single scalar reward; (2) check the judge's score histogram for bimodality, since 60.1% ≥0.8 / 22.5% ≤0.5 / 15.4% exactly 0 and only ~6% in the middle band means it is emitting pass / near-pass / hard-reject verdicts rather than a graded signal, which mechanically caps how much credit-assignment resolution it can ever carry. Note the paper's own position is that this low correlation is intended design, not a broken metric — the judge is the only axis sensitive to semantic degradation that passes every deterministic check — so the lesson is about not *summing* the channels, not about discarding one. The open question it leaves me is cost: this is 67 hand-authored tasks screened by hand, and the paper says nothing about how to run the same admission gate at synthesis scale.

[Source (arXiv 2607.06008)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06008)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>HealthAgentBench: A Unified Benchmark Suite of Realistic Agentic Healthcare Environments for Challenging Frontier AI Agents</strong> · Microsoft Research, June 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2606.31179v1, submitted 30 Jun 2026; cs.AI, 49 pages) packaging 54 healthcare tasks
across 7 categories as individual containerized terminal environments, where an agent receives a few
sentences of instruction and must explore raw patient data — 2D radiographs, 3D CT volumes, gigapixel
pathology slides, free-text clinical documents and structured EHR tables — and execute a multi-step
solution rather than answer a prompt. Every task reduces to a single binary success/failure reward emitted
by a verifier that runs outside the agent's container against hidden expert-derived gold. The headline
claim is that the suite is far from saturated: across 10 frontier agents spanning 3 harnesses at 3
attempts on all 54 tasks, the strongest resolves only about 42% of tasks.*

**From the report**

> Architecture: each task is packaged as a container-framework task with a standardized Docker image, agent-visible instructions, hidden test labels, and "a verifier that emits both a binary success/failure reward and richer diagnostic metrics"; the 54 tasks split as X-ray Report Correction 10, CT Abnormality Classification 10, Pathology Tumor Area Selection 10, Clinical Trial Matching 9, EHR Data Quality Auditing 8, EHR Event Modelling 6, EHR Format Conversion 1. — §3.3, App. B Table 2
>
> Anti-cheat by construction: "Gold labels and verifier code are mounted only into the verifier step, never into the agent container. Agent-visible identifiers are made opaque (e.g. case_NN, study_NN_<timestamp>) and corpus names are scrubbed from filenames so an agent cannot map back to the upstream dataset and look up answers. We also disable web browsing capabilities from agents." Tasks are screened so that balanced single-label binary classification is excluded outright, leaving chance success "typically below 10%". — §3.1, §3.3
>
> Grading is deterministic for 6 of 7 categories and judged for exactly one: format conversion = all verifier checks pass including canonical per-shard content hashes; CT = every abnormality label correct; pathology = tile-F1 ≥ 0.90 against the hidden mask; trial matching = recall over the agent's 50 most-confident picks = 1.0; data-quality auditing = every injected error cluster flagged (recall = 1.0) with precision ≥ 0.01; event modelling = test AUROC ≥ a human-engineered count+GBM baseline. Only X-ray Report Correction is judged, by a 5-vote majority with pass iff ≥3/5 votes report zero clinically-significant errors. — §3.3, App. B Table 3, App. E.2
>
> Construction recipe (no model training in this paper): tasks come from existing benchmarks redesigned so agents solve them end-to-end "without the human scaffolding or task-specific engineering used in the original benchmark evaluations", plus new tasks built by rule-based injection (data-quality auditing injects impossible physiological values, cross-table conflicts and demographic contradictions with a seeded recipe at image-build time). Data is never redistributed: a one-click script fetches it at runtime, and credentials live only in a bootstrap service that terminates before the agent's main service starts. — §3.2, §3.3, App. E
>
> Harness and headline: 10 frontier agents from two model families across 3 harnesses at xhigh reasoning effort with browsing disabled, 3 attempts × 54 tasks = 162 trials per agent, Wilson 95% CIs. The leader reaches 42%, then 36%, 35%, 32%, down to 16%. Harness matters independently of model: one model drops 42% → 35% moving between harnesses while another rises 32% → 36%. Sampling is deliberately small — "typically 5–15 samples per task category" — and expert time-to-solve ranges from 15–60 min to over 8 hours. — §4.1
>
> Modality split is the dominant axis: averaged across agents, mean success is 17% on the three imaging categories versus 49% on the text categories, and "no agent exceeds 40% on any single imaging task category". Cost and effort do not buy capability — the most expensive agent runs $4.8 per task and the slowest 24 min per task, both scoring well below the leader, and the Pareto frontier is traced entirely by one model family. — §4.2, Fig. 6, Fig. 8
>
> Two paired ablations isolate why the search-heavy categories fail. Data-quality auditing spans 8 tables and >800k rows; each of four error scenarios ships in **two instruction variants over identical data, labels and scoring**, and disclosing which table to inspect lifts recall for every agent (one agent climbs to 0.97). Separately, running three single-error tasks and pooling their catches recovers substantially more errors than the combined task whose label set is exactly their union. A qualitative theme runs alongside: "verification is a first-class activity" — successful agents "spend far more steps on checking than on writing". — §4.2, App. D.3, App. F.8
>
> Stated caveats: for data-quality auditing "a planned /etc/hosts egress block is not currently active, since /etc/hosts is read-only at image-build time", leaving source-name obfuscation plus an instruction-level prohibition as "the live defense against pulling a clean copy"; one harness's results are confounded because that agent "can call subagents from Opus, Sonnet and other hardcoded models"; task selection deliberately discards tasks every agent solves trivially; and the conclusion concedes "the benchmark is not exhaustive for all aspects of healthcare agents". — §3.4, §4.1, §5, App. E.7

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* healthcare is not a domain any of my notes touch, so I would read this purely as an environment-authoring spec. §3.3 with §3.4 and App. E.7 read together are the most explicit anti-cheat write-up I have seen for an agentic benchmark — gold and verifier code never mounted into the agent container, opaque IDs, corpus names scrubbed from paths, credentials confined to a bootstrap service that exits before the agent starts — and then E.7 admits one gate that never actually fires, which is the honest version of the audit I want on my own synthesized environments. App. D.3 is the part I would port verbatim: two paired ablations that hold data, labels and scoring fixed and vary only the instruction (clue vs no-clue) or the composition (one combined pass vs three focused passes), which separates "cannot navigate the search space" from "cannot detect the error" instead of collapsing both into one success rate. App. B Table 3 is a compact catalog of how to turn six different continuous metrics into one binary reward, and the two moves worth studying are pegging the gate to a *human-engineered baseline* instead of a constant, and pairing a recall=1.0 gate with a precision floor so an agent cannot pass by flagging everything. And App. F.8's finding that successful runs spend far more steps verifying than writing raises the question the paper shows but never answers: is that a policy a model can be trained toward, or only a scaffold someone hand-built?
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — §3.1–§3.4 is a written-out procedure for the world-authoring term my note treats as a learnable role: selection criteria that exclude balanced single-label binary classification outright and leave chance success typically below 10%, construction from a vetted source, then anti-Goodhart gates (verifier-only gold, opaque IDs, scrubbed corpus names, browsing disabled) and post-creation checks that reject tasks with unintended shortcuts or alternative valid gold. App. E.7's admission that the planned egress block "is not currently active" is precisely the reward-channel-integrity failure my note flags: a gate that exists in the design and does not fire at runtime. **Over-reflection in search agents** — this reports the opposite polarity to my 63% number in the two search-heavy text categories, where failure is incomplete recovery under a recall=1.0 gate (trial-matching recall 0.82–0.93; data-quality mean recall above 0.85 that rarely reaches 1.0) and App. F.8 finds successful agents spend far more steps checking than writing. It does not generalize across the suite — on CT the paper reports over-calling as roughly as common as under-calling, with 85 failed trials that are pure false-positive errors. Read against my note it sharpens the stop/pivot question: the lever is not "search less" but state-conditioned, and App. D.3's decomposition result is direct evidence that pivoting into focused passes beats one wide pass over the same label set. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — no RL happens here, but the paper prices exactly the rollouts my note says get discarded: 162 trials per agent across 10 agents, $4.8/task at the top end, 24 min/task at the slowest. A container that already emits a binary reward *plus* richer diagnostic metrics per trial is the natural place to hang a replay buffer, and the fact that most trials score 0 while carrying continuous partial signal (tile-F1 0.845, recall 0.93, AUROC 0.72–0.77) is the off-policy reuse opportunity stated in the source's own numbers. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a stretch, and inverted: my note gates a reward to knowledge the student could not already have had; here the environment is gated so the agent cannot reach knowledge it might already have, since four of the seven task categories run on public corpora (three distinct datasets) plausibly in pretraining while the other three are credential-gated. Same underlying question — could the model already have this? — answered on the eval side by construction rather than measured; the paper never tests memorization, so the gate is asserted, not verified.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the paired-instruction difficulty knob — ship each task twice over identical data, labels and scoring, once naming only the high-level error family and once disclosing the sub-types and which tables to inspect. The gap is a per-task measurement of how much failure is navigation versus detection, and the same pair doubles as a free curriculum axis for RL without authoring any new environment. Two verifier-design moves are worth copying as well: peg the binary gate to a human-engineered baseline rather than a constant (they used a count+GBM bar because the neural reference was too slow for the one-hour budget), and attach a small precision floor to any recall=1.0 gate so flag-everything cannot pass — then check that the anti-gaming clause is inert in practice, as they do when reporting that no agent over-flags to reach the floor. That last step is the cheap empirical test I should run whenever I add a guard clause to a reward.

[Source (arXiv 2606.31179)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31179) · [code](https://github.com/microsoft/HealthAgentBench)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Diagnosing Search Behavior and Failure Modes in Long-Horizon Search Agents</strong> · Renmin University of China + NUS, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.01913v1, submitted 3 Aug 2026; cs.AI/cs.CL/cs.IR, CC BY 4.0) that opens up the
search trajectory instead of scoring only the final answer. Six open-weight agents run through one
shared ReAct harness, tool interface and dense retriever on BrowseComp-Plus (830 questions, ~100K-document
fixed corpus), producing 4,980 trajectories in which every retrieved document is scored against
human-annotated document-level relevance judgments. The headline claim is that search effort and answer
quality are only weakly aligned: accuracy tracks cumulative evidence-qrels recall at K=25 (Pearson
r=0.99) rather than search volume or context spend (r=0.16), and errors split into retrieval gaps
(evidence never surfaced) and utilization gaps (evidence surfaced but not converted) that call for
opposite fixes.*

**From the report**

> Per-step evidence judgment is deterministic set membership against human qrels, not an LLM judge: BrowseComp-Plus supplies two layers, a broader evidence set E(q) and a stricter gold set G(q) ⊆ E(q) whose documents suffice to derive the gold answer, and "a retrieved document counts as evidence iff its corpus document id appears in E(q), regardless of surface relatedness". Only end-to-end answer correctness uses a judge (gpt-4o-2024-08-06, temperature 0, official BrowseComp protocol), so retrieval-side metrics "are not confounded with answer-judging noise". — §3.2
>
> Harness recipe: a ReAct loop with exactly two tools — search returns top-K=5 results from Qwen3-Embedding-8B over the official corpus with snippets truncated to 512 tokens, and visit fetches full document text by id; every rollout is capped at 128 turns and 150 minutes, and hitting either cap without an answer is recorded as incomplete. All six agents (Tongyi DeepResearch, Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, gpt-oss-120b served locally; Kimi K2.6, GLM 5.1, DeepSeek V4 Pro via provider APIs) run the same harness. — §3.1
>
> Failure taxonomy is a set-membership rule over the cumulative gold hits: correct = success; **utilization gap** = failure with a non-empty gold set retrieved; **retrieval gap** = failure with none retrieved. Each splits again — RG is "directional" when no evidence document is reached at all (fix = hypothesis re-anchoring) and "last-hop" when evidence is reached but the gold subset is missed (fix = terminal-query precision); UG is "true extraction failure" when all gold was retrieved and "boundary" when only part was. — §4.3
>
> Cumulative evidence recall at K=25 correlates with accuracy at Pearson r=0.99, visit precision at r=0.50, and final context size at r=0.16; a paired bootstrap over the 830 shared questions with B=10,000 plus leave-one-agent-out gives CIs of [0.96,1.00], [0.37,0.62] and [−0.06,0.35]. Effort inverts across agents: GLM 5.1 has top accuracy (74.1%) at the lowest cost (median 14 search calls, 52K context) while gpt-oss-120b has the lowest accuracy (38.0%) and searches most (33 calls, 86K context). — §4.1, §4.2
>
> Retrieval gap dominates errors for five of six agents at 51.6–64.1%, with Kimi K2.6 the exception at 52.0% UG; directional RG accounts for 55–67% of each agent's RG, Kimi K2.6 is the most extraction-bound at 71.4% true UG, and Kimi K2.6 and DeepSeek V4 Pro "land within a point of each other in accuracy yet on opposite sides of the RG/UG split". Mean gold recall spans 52.0% to 78.7% on a fixed shared retriever — a 26-point spread the paper argues "can only be query-side". — §4.3
>
> Redundant search measured at episode granularity: only 6–23% of episodes are productive, 20–29% redundant and 50–70% unproductive, so 77.5–93.6% add no new evidence; the median productive episode count is 2–3 everywhere. After the first gold hit, 57.9–74.0% of later episodes add no new evidence. But the paper reads the long tail as "rational continuation under failed retrieval, not simply a stopping failure": 48–64% of incorrect trajectories never surface a gold-bearing document at all, so stopping rules bind only the UG subset, where the 58–74% post-gold redundancy "bounds the reclaim". — §4.4
>
> Within-question test with question fixed effects over an agent × question panel of 4,919 usable trajectories: on the 366 questions where agents disagree about whether gold was retrieved, the retrieving agents answer correctly 73.8% of the time and the others only 2.2%; cumulative gold recall carries a +30 pp effect per SD with t=34, an order of magnitude above every behavioral coefficient, and controlling for recall absorbs 56–60% of each stronger agent's edge over gpt-oss-120b. Pivoting is not the waste — pivot rate correlates ρ=+0.31 with accuracy and per-turn query batching ρ=+0.75. — §4.5, §4.6
>
> Stated caveats: the oracle ladder that puts the ceiling at Oracle-E 87.5% (Hard UG 12.5%) "swaps in GPT-4o, [so] Net Gain is a joint retrieval + reader ceiling, not pure retrieval headroom"; the K=20 budget bound "assumes the eventual answer is unchanged under early stopping; hard truncation may yield lower accuracy"; the within-question search-volume result is read "associationally"; the four-agent open-web check is "directionally consistent but underpowered for the ordering"; and overall "our numbers are upper bounds rather than realized gains". — §4.2 fn. 2, §4.3, §4.6, §4.7, §5

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3.2 with the §4.3 definitions first — the load-bearing design choice is that the per-step evidence label is set membership on a document id, not an LLM judgment, which confines judge noise to the accuracy axis alone. That is exactly the property I want in a process reward channel, and the two-layer E(q)/G(q) split is the part to copy carefully, since using the broad layer versus the strict layer changes what counts as a utilization gap at all. §4.4 is the bullet I need to read against my own framing, because it argues the opposite causal story for most of the tail. §4.6's within-question panel is the methodology I should be using for my own A-vs-B claims: question fixed effects on an agent × question panel with errors clustered by question, plus a paired bootstrap over the same 830 items rather than a six-point cross-agent correlation — and the coefficient magnitudes are the useful part, recall at +30 pp/SD with t=34 against repeat-query at −1.9 pp/SD says the training signal belongs on retrieval coverage, not on behavioral surface statistics. One number to handle carefully: the r=0.99 headline is *evidence*-qrels recall at K=25, while the +30 pp/SD regressor is *gold* recall — the paper distinguishes them explicitly and it is easy to quote the wrong one.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the same phenomenon measured with a harder instrument, and it partly refutes my framing. My note counts confirm-then-keep-searching at 63% over 23,110 keyed trajectories using answer-string matching; this paper counts no-new-evidence episodes at 77.5–93.6% and post-gold redundancy at 57.9–74.0% using qrels set membership, so it can separate two populations my string match cannot: trajectories that continue after gold is in hand (stoppable) and the 48–64% of incorrect trajectories that never surface gold and continue because they are still failing. That partition is exactly the state variable my open stop/pivot question needs, and the paper's prescription — saturation-with-gold → stop, saturation-without-gold → reformulate — plus its four RG/UG subtypes is a ready-made version of my per-failure-type repair question. **AgentPlanet** — the mechanism link is reward-channel integrity: the whole design rests on making the process-level signal deterministic (a document id is in the qrels set or it is not) and pushing the only LLM judgment to the single outcome axis, which is a concrete anti-Goodhart gate — a per-step credit signal a judge cannot drift on. It also shows what a materialized world buys: the fixed corpus with per-question evidence and gold sets is what makes retrieval-vs-utilization attribution decidable at all. The world-authoring framing is mine, not the paper's; it never treats the corpus as a learnable role. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — partial stretch, since the paper does no RL, but it quantifies how much of an expensive search rollout is dead weight and gives a per-step label to salvage the rest: only 6–23% of episodes are productive, and a K=20 episode cap retains 86.3–92.4% of accuracy. The productive/redundant/unproductive triple is a dense per-step credit label that would let me keep and reweight the productive prefix of a failed rollout instead of discarding the whole trajectory on an outcome-only signal. **Post-cutoff distillation** — stretch, but one clean number transfers: on the 366 gold-disagreement questions the non-retrieving agents answer correctly only 2.2% of the time, which bounds closed-book answering. The paper's own limit is the honest one — recall "cannot separate query skill from knowledge-informed querying" — so 2.2% bounds parametric answering without isolating it.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the episode-utility triple as a dense process label wherever I have a materialized corpus with evidence sets — define an episode as one search plus the visits before the next search, then label it productive if it surfaced new evidence, redundant if it re-surfaced known evidence, unproductive if it surfaced none, plus k\* (last productive episode) and the wasted tail. It is computed from set membership, costs no judge calls, and gives both a rollout-pruning filter and a per-step reward that outcome-only training cannot produce. The open question the paper explicitly leaves: its numbers are "upper bounds rather than realized gains", and nobody has shown that a policy trained on the diagnosed trigger actually recovers the 58–74% post-gold redundancy or closes any of the 26-point query-side gold-recall spread on a fixed retriever. That is a well-posed RL experiment with a pre-computed ceiling — and the ceiling is per-agent (Net Gain +13.4 to +49.5 pp), not a single global target.

[Source (arXiv 2608.01913)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01913) · [code](https://github.com/liuqi6777/search_agent)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Do Agent Benchmarks Measure Capability? Protocol Validity in the Age of Agentic AI</strong> · Tencent Hunyuan + HKUST + Duke Kunshan, July 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2607.22368v1, submitted 24 Jul 2026; cs.AI) that defines "protocol validity" — the
requirement that a benchmark's evaluation protocol keep the intended capability *necessary* for
success — and introduces HackDetect, a post-hoc audit that attributes an Expose → Exploit → Mislead
evidence chain from retained run records and quantifies score inflation with the Mislead gap
G = S_exploit − S_intended. The authors audit 2,385 retained task-to-score traces across 15 agent
benchmarks spanning web research, software repair, repository reconstruction, terminal use, system
optimization and ML research. Headline claim: 67.0% of audited Frontier Science traces and 66.7% of
audited AutoLab tasks carry a supported score-relevant protocol finding, and where a paired comparison
exists the measured score inflation is 0.45–1.00.*

**From the report**

> Core framing: a benchmark protocol is P = {E, I, S, V} — environment, information flow, scoring function, verification mechanism — where "Scoring and verification are distinct: S assigns credit, whereas V supplies evidence that the credit was earned through a path consistent with the capability claim."
