A validity failure follows Expose → Exploit → Mislead, and "A mislead=yes judgment requires all three links; incomplete evidence is recorded as no or partial with the missing link identified." — §3.1, §3.2, §4.2
>
> Taxonomy: five numbered Exposure *sources* — Answer source, Hidden state, Generation mechanism, Interaction feedback, Evaluation pipeline — specialized into seven Exposure *values* (benchmark_overexposure, held_out_readable, setting_exposure, generator_regularity, feedback_inference, harness_loophole, invalid_scoring_path). A separate, independent agent_engagement axis records none / passive / active / engineered: "agent_engagement records behavior, while mislead records measurement validity. They are independent, not successive severity levels." — §4.1, App. A
>
> Audit pipeline: HackDetect operates on a replayable bundle of benchmark spec, trajectory, submitted artifact, score record and an optional comparison score. Evidence selection "deliberately favors recall and identifies where the audit should look without assigning a label"; a fixed-prompt LLM judge then processes one candidate at a time with a bounded read_file(path, start, end) and "cannot execute commands, access the network, modify files, or re-score the submission." Schema and pointer validation reject citations whose event/file/line/grader record does not exist, and the Mislead gap is computed outside the judge — "HackDetect does not infer S_intended from the judge label." — §4.1–§4.3
>
> Headline prevalence, where the metric is the share of *audited traces* (tasks, for AutoLab) carrying a supported score-relevant finding: "Supported protocol findings range from 0% to 67.0% across audited cohorts. Frontier Science has 331 positives among 494 traces (67.0%), and AutoLab has 24 among 36 (66.7%). Every other audited cohort is at or below 21.7%, and five contain no positive trace." Frontier Science and AutoLab are **not** among the five rows marked as preselected suspicious traces. — §5.2, Table 2
>
> Score inflation: "All five paired cases show score inflation of 0.447–1.00" — G = 1.00 for a WildClawBench empty-submission evaluation-pipeline failure, 0.859 and 0.835 for two EdgeBench cases, 0.621 for a generation-mechanism causal-topology probe, and 0.447 for the Frontier Science answer-source subset. On Frontier Science, source-paper rubric overlap correlates with awarded score at r = 0.625, and traces closely matching source content average 0.850 versus 0.403 for the rest. Across 352 Mislead-positive traces, Answer source accounts for 303 (86.1%) — yet Appendix F rates it the *easiest* exposure to detect, while generator_regularity (7 traces) is rated maximum difficulty on all five dimensions. — §5.3, App. F
>
> Detector validity against human labels: HackDetect matches all 21 human labels on a held-out MLS-Bench slice that includes negative controls "in which an agent reads a held-out file but does not use it in the submitted artifact." On 53 hand-labeled Frontier Science traces it reaches precision 0.94, recall 0.76, F1 = 0.84. No inter-rater agreement between multiple human annotators is reported anywhere. — §5.1, Table 1
>
> Eval setup and harness: "By default, audited traces were generated by Claude Opus 4.8, and HackDetect used GPT-5.5 as the judge." Benchmarks audited are Frontier Science, AutoLab, the SWE-bench family, FrontierSWE, NL2Repo-Bench, Terminal-Bench, WildClawBench, DeepSWE, BrowseComp and MLS-Bench, plus five internal suites. A cross-model check on Frontier Science over 960 rollouts gives Mislead rates of 65.0% (95% CI 59.4–70.6) for GPT-5.5 and 69.7% (63.5–75.9) for Kimi-k2.6, with overlapping intervals that "do not support a statistically separable difference" — read by the authors as structural exposure rather than model deficiency. — §5, Table 3, App. G
>
> Self-stated caveats: "Five benchmark rows contain preselected suspicious traces, so their rates do not estimate benchmark-wide prevalence. Audit batches use the same schema but differ in judge configuration and review policy, and hand-label calibration shows imperfect recall. The Mislead gap is available only when a defensible comparison score exists." The 0.76 recall means "reported Answer source counts may remain conservative"; the Appendix F difficulty scores "are ordinal judgements assigned by the authors from the case evidence, not measured quantities"; and validity is framed as maintained rather than established once — "Benchmark validity therefore cannot depend on a current agent declining to exploit an available path." — §5.1, §6, App. D.4, App. F

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §4.1–§4.3 as a spec for an acceptance gate, not as an audit story — the three-link rule (exposure + use-in-artifact + grader credit) plus pointer validation is exactly the filter I lack when deciding whether a synthesized trajectory was solved by capability or by a leak in the environment I built, and their negative control is the part that makes it usable: an agent that reads a held-out file but never lets it reach the submitted artifact stays non-positive, which is how you keep a leak detector from flagging every private-path read. Read Table 2 next to my own eval numbers, but read the metric definition before the percentages — and note the zero rows are as informative as the headline ones, since five cohorts including 139 MLS-Bench Lite and 300 internal SWE traces came back clean, making this a protocol property rather than a universal indictment. §5.3 with Appendix F say opposite things about where to spend effort: Answer source is 86.1% of positives but the *easiest* exposure to detect, while generator_regularity is 7 traces at maximum difficulty — so auditing my own synthetic environments only for answer leakage clears the cheap 86% and leaves the seed-predictability and warmup-cache classes untouched, which are precisely the ones a strong RL policy would find. And §5.2's Table 3 is the falsification test worth copying: the same failure at 65.0% and 69.7% across two unrelated models with overlapping CIs is what turns "this model cheats" into "this protocol leaks."
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the most direct hit, and it supplies the missing piece of the world-authoring term. Their P = {E, I, S, V} splits scoring from verification precisely so that credit assignment and the evidence that credit was *earned* are separate objects; that is the formal shape of the anti-Goodhart gate I said a world builder must pass. The five Exposure sources map one-to-one onto failure modes of a learned initial-state-and-rules term (public answer embedded in the world, readable hidden state, predictable generator, oracle-ish feedback, corruptible scorer), and their per-source design controls read as pass/fail conditions for a generated world. Appendix I.1's "adaptive benchmark generation" — refresh instances and evaluator parameters while preserving the capability construct — is literally the open problem of making world authoring a learnable role whose output stays measurement-equivalent. **Over-reflection in search agents** — mechanism-level and uncomfortable. Their Frontier Science conclusion is that the reported score reflects shortcut access and search persistence in addition to independent derivation, with 88% of capability-drift labels reading "intended independent derivation → retrieval and transcription of the source." That is a reward-side explanation for confirm-then-keep-searching: if the environment exposes the answer, extra search rounds are not wasted compute, they are the mechanism that finds the leak — and a stop/pivot policy trained in such an environment learns to keep searching for exactly the wrong reason. Their four-level engagement axis, held independent of whether the score is misleading, is a cleaner label set than my binary. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — narrower, on the integrity of what goes into the buffer: their replayable audit record (tool calls with commands, touched paths, timestamps, artifacts with content hashes and line maps, grader message and item id) is the retention format that makes an old rollout re-auditable rather than merely re-usable, and their rule that re-audit is warranted after any change to tasks, agents, harnesses or scorers names the failure mode replay invites — a stored high-reward transition whose reward came from an exposure keeps paying off long after the leak is patched. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a stretch, and I'll label it one. The only real contact is their held-out "no-tool memorized" category, three traces deliberately kept outside the positive class because the model answered from parametric memory rather than from an exposed artifact. That is my discrimination in mirror image, and their answer is evidentiary rather than statistical — cite the pointer into the retained trace or don't make the claim.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the paired source-free rerun as a required release number for any environment I synthesize — they define G = S_exploit − S_intended only when a defensible comparison exists and compute it *outside* the judge, never inferring S_intended from the judge's own label. The Frontier Science instance (removing answer access drops the score by 0.447) is the number to beat: if an ablated twin of one of my environments moves the score by anything near that, the environment is measuring retrieval, not the capability I claimed. Computing the gap outside the judge is the part I'd steal hardest, since it keeps the detector from grading its own homework. The sharp open question: the result rests on a single *default* judge configuration validated against human labels at recall 0.76, with no inter-rater agreement between human annotators reported at all, and the paper itself concedes audit batches "differ in judge configuration and review policy" — so the prevalence numbers inherit an unmeasured judge-variance term, and by their own Appendix F the exposures the judge is worst at seeing are the ones rated hardest to detect. The 86.1% Answer-source concentration may therefore be partly a detector artifact. Anyone reusing this as a data-audit gate needs the judge-ensemble ablation the paper does not run.

[Source (arXiv 2607.22368)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22368)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Efficient RL Training for LLMs with Experience Replay</strong> · FAIR at Meta + NYU, April 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2604.08706v1, submitted 9 Apr 2026; cs.LG, 34 pages, CC BY-NC-ND) studying experience
replay for LLM RL post-training, pairing a bias-variance convergence analysis with asynchronous RL
fine-tuning runs on Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3-8B and Llama 3.2 3B (H100/H200, 1–4 eight-GPU nodes,
at least 4 seeds per configuration). It formalizes replay-buffer design as a three-way trade-off between
staleness-induced variance, sample diversity and the compute cost of generation, and derives the optimal
staleness horizon as a variational problem with the replay ratio in closed form given it. Its headline
claim is that strict on-policy sampling is suboptimal when generation is expensive, and that a simple
FIFO buffer saves up to 40% of the compute budget while matching or sometimes surpassing on-policy
accuracy.*

**From the report**

> Optimal design is stated as two ratios: the staleness horizon N/R = argmin over x>0 of σ̄²(x)·(√(1/μ) + √(ρ + 1/x))², and the replay ratio B/R = √(μ/(ρ + 1/x\*)) — revealing "a three-way trade-off between staleness-induced noise growth (σ̄²), coupling between replayed samples and the parameter iterates (ρ), and the rollout-vs-training compute imbalance (μ)". The horizon itself is an argmin, not a closed form; a closed-form x\* is given only under a power-law assumption on σ in Appendix C. — Theorem 4.5, §4, App. C
>
> The quantity that decides on-policy vs replay is μ, "the compute cost of generating a rollout divided by the compute cost of processing it through a gradient update": when μ is small or variance/correlation are high "the optimal staleness horizon x\* approaches zero… it is more effective to remain on-policy". Measured μ = 5.28 (IQR [5.12, 5.48], 129 runs) for Qwen2.5-7B and 6.84 (IQR [6.45, 7.07], 242 runs) for Qwen3-0.6B, with a stated general range of "~4 to ~10 depending on the model, task and implementation". — §4, §3.2 fn. 3, §D.4 Table 2
>
> Importance sampling does **not** remove the replay bias: replay "introduces a bias in the gradient estimate through the correlation introduced by the buffer, even with importance ratio correction," because the needed object is the old policy *conditioned on the fact that its own samples produced the current weights* — "typically not computable". With a buffer, the joint distribution over the training batch "is not corrected in expectation by the importance sampling factor". — §4 fn. 4, §D.2
>
> Compute accounting: without a buffer an update costs ~C(1+μ), with a buffer ~C(1+W/T), giving γ = (1+W/T)/(1+μ); at μ = 5.28 for Qwen2.5-7B, γ = 1.29, 0.65, 0.43, 0.32, 0.22, 0.18 for (W,T) = (7,1), (6,2), (5,3), (4,4), (2,6), (1,7). Motivating scale: RL inference "often dominates the training budget, and may consume more than 80% of post-training GPU hours". — §1, §3.2 Table 1
>
> Headline result: "a simple replay buffer can reduce the compute budget by up to 40% while maintaining or even surpassing the accuracy of on-policy baselines" (§6; §1 and §5.2 carry differently worded variants). The Figure 1 run is Qwen2.5-7B on OpenR1-Math-220k evaluated on MATH with buffer size N=84, median and IQR over 10 seeds, where "baseline runs display increased instability" — its caption gives (W,T) = (5,3) while the in-plot legend reads (6,2), an internal inconsistency in the paper that matters, since γ is 0.43 versus 0.65 for those two settings. — §6, §1, §5.2, Fig. 1
>
> Training recipe: GRPO with group size G=16, no KL regularization term, ε_low = ε_high = 0.2, Adam with constant LR (3.37e-7 for Qwen3-0.6B, 6.8e-8 for Qwen2.5-7B), batch size 60 (63 when T=7), generation temperature 1.0; critically, "the advantage is computed when the rollout is generated (and not when it is used to compose a gradient update)". — §5.1, §D.2
>
> Eval setup: train on OpenR1-Math-220k, evaluate on its test split or MATH, at least 4 random seeds per configuration reporting median and IQR, on Nvidia H100/H200 across 1, 2 or 4 eight-GPU nodes with data parallelism and no tensor parallelism; pass@1 at temperature 0.1 / top_p 0.95 and pass@k (k>1) at temperature 1.0; generalization checks are Qwen3-8B on miniF2F Lean tasks and Llama 3.2 3B on OpenR1-Math-220k. Replay improves pass@k for k>1 "in absolute terms… but also comparatively: using a buffer helps the pass@k for large k even more than it helps the pass@1". — §5.1, §5.2, §D.1, §D.2
>
> Stated caveats: "while our results are consistent for the model scales evaluated in this study, further work is needed to validate these efficiency gains on larger frontier models"; all runs with or without buffer "culminate in a global maximum accuracy, followed by a decline in performance," with models that "initially overfit before ultimately collapsing into a nonsensical policy"; the positive-bias/AsymRE refinements need "larger-scale experiments… to validate the robustness of these findings"; sampling without replacement was "inconclusive"; and the compute metric explicitly "ignore[s] the effects of important implementation details, such as tensor parallelism, data parallelism, sharding". — §5.2, §5.5, §6, §D.3

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* Theorem 4.5 with the μ table (§4, §D.4) is the part I would actually use — it converts "should I reuse rollouts?" into measurable numbers: μ estimated as (K_training/T)/(K_inference/W), and the staleness horizon N/R. My search-agent rollouts (multi-turn tool calls, long horizons, retrieval latency) sit well above the μ = 5.28–6.84 they measured on single-turn math, which is precisely the regime where their optimum moves away from on-policy. Footnote 4 of §4 with §D.2 is the warning to read before porting any of it: the PPO/GRPO importance ratio does not correct the buffer-induced bias, because the object you would need is the old policy conditioned on having produced the current weights, which is uncomputable — so any replay I bolt onto a verifier-scored agent pipeline inherits an uncorrected bias term and I should budget for it rather than assume clipping handles it. The §D.2 detail that the advantage is frozen at generation time and never recomputed at replay time is the cheapest implementation decision to copy — it is what makes group-relative advantages compatible with a buffer at all, since the group's reward statistics no longer exist by the time a sample is replayed. And §5.3 is a benefit I would take even if I never changed W/T: run the buffer at W/T = μ with expected replay ratio 1, purely to decouple producers from consumers, because my tool-using rollouts have wildly variable latency (search calls, timeouts, retries), which is exactly the stall pattern they say the buffer smooths.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — direct hit, and it supplies the formula the note is missing. My note fixes an update-to-data ratio of 2 updates per 1024 transitions empirically; Theorem 4.5 derives the LLM-post-training analogue as B/R = √(μ/(ρ + 1/x\*)), so the optimal replay ratio grows with the rollout-to-gradient-step cost ratio μ. It also answers the note's open question about off-policy reuse with a concrete accounting (γ = (1+W/T)/(1+μ)) and a caution the note does not have: the importance ratio does not fix the buffer bias. **Post-cutoff distillation** — on-policy distillation is exactly the pipeline this argues against as a default: every student rollout is generated fresh, scored, used once, discarded, and teacher scoring pushes generation cost up further. Their claim is conditional on μ, so the transfer is that a per-token-reward distillation loop with expensive generation sits in the large-μ regime where a FIFO buffer with a bounded staleness horizon should be near-optimal — with the caveat that σ̄ is assumed, not measured, and a stale student rollout scored under an updated teacher may violate it. **Over-reflection in search agents** — two mechanisms connect. Their positive-bias sampling is reward-conditioned buffer retention (keep the freshest (1−δ)N rollouts plus the freshest δN correct ones, δ ∈ {0, 0.2, 0.5}) on the stated intuition that "the utility of correct rollouts is less affected by off-policiness," which is the same shape as retaining trajectories that stopped at the right time for a stop/pivot objective. Separately, replay improving pass@k for k>1 more than pass@1 while preserving entropy is a direct counter to the diversity collapse that drives degenerate search behavior. Treating their correct-rollout rule as a stop/pivot retention rule is a stretch — they never study multi-turn or tool-using agents. **AgentPlanet** — a stretch, but principled: Assumption 4.2 bounds bias by how much the buffer's own samples influenced the current iterate, and footnote 4 identifies the uncomputable object as the old policy conditioned on having produced θ_t. When one model authors the world, acts in it, and models transitions, that self-influence loop is structurally tighter than in their single-role math setting, so a replay buffer holding self-authored worlds should compound exactly the correlation term ρ their theory penalizes. The paper does not study this.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* positive-bias sampling as a retention rule — keep the freshest (1−δ)N rollouts plus the freshest δN correct ones. The verifier-grounded version for search agents would retain trajectories whose answer was confirmed against a real retrieval hit rather than merely marked correct, though the authors say plainly that "larger-scale experiments are now needed to validate" these refinements. The open question I would want closed before trusting the 40%: σ̄(staleness) is an *assumption* in their analysis, never measured. For agent rollouts the per-sample variance plausibly grows much faster with staleness than for single-turn math, because a stale tool-call trajectory can be outright invalid — dead link, changed index, expired state — rather than merely off-distribution. Empirically fitting σ̄ on agent data is what decides whether their staleness horizon transfers or collapses to zero.

[Source (arXiv 2604.08706)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08706)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Single-Rollout Asynchronous Optimization for Agentic Reinforcement Learning</strong> · Tsinghua University, July 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2607.07508v1, submitted 8 Jul 2026; cs.LG, CC BY 4.0) proposing SAO, an asynchronous
RL algorithm for LLM agents that replaces GRPO's group-wise sampling with exactly one rollout per
prompt and recovers the missing baseline from a trained token-level value model rather than a group
mean. Experiments run on a Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 backbone across four math-with-Python-tool
benchmarks and SWE-Bench Verified under an OpenHands scaffold, plus a simulated non-stationary
online-writing study. The headline claim is stable training for roughly one thousand steps with
consistent gains over GRPO and its variants, where vanilla GRPO collapses at about 160 steps.*

**From the report**

> Core method: group-wise sampling is replaced by one rollout per prompt (group size = 1), fed to the learner immediately upon completion, and the group mean is replaced by a learned token-level value model — the advantage comes from GAE over a critic, not from within-group normalization. The paper is explicit about the cost: "single-rollout optimization inherently suffers from high variance in gradient estimation, similar to REINFORCE. To reduce variance requires a sufficiently good value model." — §3.2
>
> Why group-wise sampling is blamed for async instability: "The group-wise sampling induces latency-driven off-policy behavior because the group has to wait for the slower one to finish before fed into training. In addition, group-wise sampling is incompatible with online or complex agentic settings where the environment often provides only a single trajectory feedback per prompt." §5.1 restates it as an architectural fault — group normalization "introduces an implicit synchronization barrier: updates must wait until all group members are generated, exacerbating staleness and off-policy drift under asynchrony." — §1, §5.1
>
> Staleness handling (DIS, Direct Double-Sided Importance Sampling): because a rollout engine "may undergo multiple updates during a single trajectory generation," tracking exact behavior probabilities would require an "extensive history of model checkpoints", so SAO drops π_old entirely and takes the importance ratio directly against the rollout engine's own logged log-probs, r_t = exp(log π_θ(a_t|s_t) − log π_rollout(a_t|s_t)). Stability then comes from a hard double-sided token mask f(x) = x if 1−ε_l < x < 1+ε_h, else 0 — divergent tokens are zeroed out, not clipped to a boundary. — §3.1, Eqs. 1–3
>
> Three value-model designs that make single-rollout practical: (1) a faster critic than actor, K=2 value updates per policy update; (2) a frozen-attention critic — instability was traced to the Full Attention layers ("the gradient norms of the value model are significantly larger than the corresponding policy model… this instability originates primarily from the Full Attention layers, whereas the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) layers remain relatively stable"), so attention params are frozen and only MoE projections are optimized; (3) a skip-observation token-level GAE that bridges the action-to-action boundary so credit never propagates through observation tokens the model did not generate. A fourth strategy, scaling value pretraining, addresses the critic "cold start" the paper calls "a major bottleneck". — §3.2, Eqs. 4–5
>
> Training recipe: the math-with-Python policy **and** value model are both initialized from Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 fine-tuned for 3 epochs on Tool-Integrated Reasoning data produced by GPT-OSS-120B. RL uses batch size 128, group size 1, 128k max length, policy lr 1e-6, token clipping ε_low=0.3 / ε_high=5.0, length-adaptive GAE with α=1.5, critic lr 5e-6, 10-step warmup, K=2. GRPO baselines are compute-matched at 16 prompts × 8 rollouts = the same batch size of 128; the coding run uses the base model directly with ε_low=0.8 / ε_high=3.0. — §4.1
>
> Eval setup: four math benchmarks (AIME2025, BeyondAIME, HMMT Nov 2025, IMOAnswerBench) as Pass@1 at temperature 1.0, top-p 1.0, 128k max generation, up to 50 turns for tool calls; SWE-Bench Verified under the OpenHands scaffold with up to 300 interaction turns and a 128k context budget. Variance is controlled by averaging 16 evaluation runs for AIME2025 / HMMT / IMOAnswerBench and 4 for BeyondAIME. — §4.1
>
> Headline numbers: on AIME2025 / BeyondAIME / HMMT Nov 2025 / IMOAnswerBench, SAO scores 97.3 / 74.8 / 88.3 / 74.0 versus GRPO(+DIS) at 93.5 / 70.8 / 84.0 / 70.0 and a DIS-only ablation at 94.2 / 71.5 / 86.7 / 71.3; on SWE-Bench Verified, Qwen3-30B-A3B 23.0 → GRPO(w/ DIS) 27.0 → SAO 29.8. The critic is what carries it: a running-mean baseline (sliding window of the 8 most recent rewards per prompt) scores only 79.8 / 55.3 against SAO's 97.3 / 74.8. On stability, vanilla GRPO "suffers from a performance collapse at approximately 160 training steps" and vanilla VAPO without DIS collapses "at approximately 90 steps" while holding a near-zero clip ratio; SAO and GRPO(w/ DIS) are comparable early and diverge "after approximately 400 training steps". — §4.2–§4.4, Tables 1–4
>
> Caveats the paper states: "Our experiments focus on large-scale agentic reasoning, coding, and simulated online writing tasks with a Qwen3-30B-A3B backbone. The conclusions therefore may not transfer directly to smaller models, non-agentic RLHF settings, or environments with dense rewards and shorter rollouts. In addition, SAO depends on a trained value model and rollout log-probabilities, so deployment requires infrastructure that can reliably preserve token-level behavior probabilities during asynchronous generation." The online-learning result "uses a controlled simulated preference shift", and the *vanilla*-GRPO row reports its final valid performance before collapsing. — Appendix B, §4.2

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3.2's skip-observation GAE (Eqs. 4–5) first — it is the cleanest statement I have seen of where credit assignment should stop at the environment boundary, linking the last token of one action straight to the first token of the next so the critic is never asked to price a state the policy did not author. That is exactly the boundary I keep drawing by hand in multi-turn search trajectories. Read §3.1 next for the infra contract rather than the math: DIS consumes the rollout engine's logged token log-probs as the behavior distribution, which makes my serving layer part of the training loop and turns any silent log-prob drift between engine and trainer into a correctness bug — Appendix B admits this dependency explicitly. Read §4.3's Table 4 for the number that decides portability: the running-mean single-rollout baseline lands at 79.8 AIME versus SAO's 97.3, so the win is not "drop the group", it is "train a good critic" — and a critic is exactly what I do not currently maintain for tool-using search agents. On reading the headline: the collapse caveat applies only to the vanilla-GRPO row (84.2, final valid score before collapsing at ~160 steps); the SAO 97.3 vs GRPO(+DIS) 93.5 comparison is already the clean single-config one, both DIS-stabilized and both trained through ~1000 steps.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — the LLM-agent analogue of the same bet, arrived at from the opposite side. FlashSAC reuses old rollouts via a replay buffer to raise the update-to-data ratio; SAO keeps the data ratio at one but removes the synchronization barrier so each expensive rollout enters the learner the instant it finishes, and pays for the resulting staleness with an importance ratio taken directly against the rollout policy, hardened by a double-sided mask that zeroes divergent tokens — what it discards is the separate π_old term, not importance weighting itself. §4.4 gives the failure mode of not gating: VAPO holds a near-zero clip ratio and collapses at ~90 steps. Both notes converge on the same open question: what a principled off-policy budget looks like for agent rollouts. **Over-reflection in search agents** — my open question is state-conditioned stop/pivot RL, and the blocker is that a group-normalized outcome reward hands one scalar to an entire trajectory, so it cannot say which of the post-confirmation search rounds was worthless. SAO's token-level critic plus skip-observation GAE is exactly the machinery that produces a per-action-boundary advantage across turns. The paper also reports that coarsening this to step-level (one advantage per turn) loses 2.5 AIME points at matched steps, which cuts against my instinct to score at turn granularity. **AgentPlanet** — the skip-observation GAE is a mechanism-level statement about the policy/world-model split in my factorization: SAO deliberately excludes the transition term from the credit path because the model does not generate the observation, so the value function is never asked to price an environment-authored state. §4.5's non-stationary reward channel (a writing-style preference cycled mid-training, with an LLM judge) is also a small live test of reward-channel integrity under a moving target — the critic re-tracks the shift while a 128-window running-mean baseline lags. **Post-cutoff distillation** — partial stretch. The distillation half matches: §4.1 initializes both policy and critic from a model SFT'd on tool-integrated reasoning data generated by a larger external model, then does RL from that init. The tighter link is DIS's requirement that a per-token ratio be computed in one consistent vocabulary from preserved token-level behavior probabilities — SAO sidesteps my cross-tokenizer alignment problem by construction, but Appendix B naming log-prob preservation as a deployment prerequisite is the same infra constraint my per-token reward scheme inherits.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the frozen-attention critic. They localized the instability rather than damping it globally — critic gradient norms blow up in the Full Attention layers while MoE layers stay calm, so they froze attention and trained only the MoE projections. That is a cheap diagnostic to run on any value or reward model before reaching for gradient clipping or a lower LR, and here it is worth 6.7 AIME points (90.6 → 97.3). The sharp open question: SAO's whole advantage over a critic-free single-rollout baseline (79.8 → 97.3) rests on the value model, and §3.2 admits value cold start is a major bottleneck fixed only by "significantly increasing the scale of the value pretraining corpus" — with no number, no corpus description and no ablation. For long-horizon search agents where reward is sparse and terminal, the cost and data recipe for that critic pretraining *is* the entire question, and this paper does not answer it.

[Source (arXiv 2607.07508)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07508)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>On-Policy Self-Distillation without Any Supervision</strong> · UC San Diego + Georgia Tech + UMD + ByteDance, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.06296, v1 6 Aug 2026, v2 9 Aug 2026; cs.LG, CC BY 4.0) proposing u-OPSD, which
removes the ground-truth solution from on-policy self-distillation by letting the model build its own
privileged teacher context out of majority-vote agreement among its own rollouts. Scope is deliberately
narrow and reproducible: one model family (Qwen3 4B/8B in thinking and non-thinking modes, plus two
instruction-tuned variants), one domain (competition math), a 30k-prompt OpenThoughts subset used
prompts-only, LoRA rank 64, and 150 training steps. The headline claim is that a label-free method
matches or beats its own supervised counterparts (SFT, GRPO, OPSD-with-gold) — by 8.5 and 10.7 points
over base in non-thinking mode and 3.2 and 2.3 points over supervised OPSD at 4B and 8B.*

**From the report**

> Core method and the exact gate: G rollouts are sampled per prompt, answers parsed from \boxed{…} with normalization, the pseudo-answer is the plurality over valid answers (ties broken uniformly at random), and the self-consistency score c(x) is the fraction of **all G** rollouts matching it — not just the parsable ones, "where truncated generations reduce confidence". If c(x) < τ the prompt "is treated as unlabeled and contributes no gradient in the training step"; default τ = 1/2, "an absolute majority". — §3.2, Alg. 1
>
> Which token positions are supervised: the longest agreeing rollout replaces the gold solution as the teacher reference, and the loss runs over a selected subset of the *disagreeing* rollouts (k=1, the longest, in every reported run) at every token position, with agreeing rollouts contributing no gradient. Two classes are skipped — c(x) < τ (vote untrusted) and no disagreement (nothing to correct) — so "training therefore focuses on the model's competence frontier… This curriculum emerges directly from the model's own voting statistics." — §3.2, Eq. 5
>
> Training recipe: a 30k subset of OpenThoughts using "only the problem statements, never the solution field"; LoRA rank 64 (α=128) on all attention and MLP projections, lr 5e-6, gradient-norm clipping 0.1, sampling at temperature 1.1 / top-p 0.95 / top-k 20; forward KL over the full vocabulary with per-token pointwise clipping; teacher frozen at the initial policy; G=8 rollouts per prompt; max completion length raised from 1,024 to 4,096 tokens; 150 steps with a checkpoint every 25. — §4.1, §4.3
>
> Headline non-thinking numbers: Qwen3-4B five-benchmark average 40.96 base → 49.49 u-OPSD, against 46.29 for supervised OPSD, 45.86 GRPO, 42.73 SFT; Qwen3-8B 43.57 → 54.31 against 52.04 OPSD. Label-free RL baselines under the same rollout budget move at most 1.5%: TTRL 41.77/43.54, RENT 42.45/42.97, Intuitor 41.98/43.52. In thinking mode the margin shrinks sharply — 4B 74.85 → 77.05 and 8B 76.09 → 77.99 versus OPSD's 77.97, a 0.02 tie. — §4.2, Tables 1–3
>
> Eval setup: AIME24, AIME25 and HMMT25 at avg@12, MATH500 and AMC23 at avg@4; vLLM decoding at temperature 1.0 with 38k max generation length, in the same reasoning mode the model was trained in; checkpoints evaluated every 25 steps to 150 with the **best** score reported for u-OPSD and OPSD, while GRPO gets peak-within-500-steps. All OPSD numbers are the authors' own rerun with released code and hyperparameters under identical conditions. — §4.1
>
> The supervision channel is where the signal lives: full-vocabulary logit distillation scores 57.10 versus 43.45 for the student-sampled-token variant, and top-100 truncation of the teacher is the best variant at 59.01. Separately, stripping the teacher reference down to the boxed pseudo-label instead of the full agreeing trace costs 10.3–15.8% and puts all three label-only settings **below** the base model's 43.57 — "effective distillation requires the teacher to condition on the longer, full reasoning trace rather than only the final answer." — §4.3, Table 4, Fig. 5
>
> The advertised gate is best when loosest: the threshold sweep is monotone in the loose direction — τ=0.3 scores 58.59 against 57.10 for the τ=0.5 default and 44.40 at τ=0.9, a 14.2% spread, and the paper states plainly that "the default τ=0.5 is therefore not the best setting in this sweep". Divergence choice matters as much: forward KL 57.10, symmetric JSD 43.34 (level with the untrained model), reverse KL does not converge — a length blowup rather than incoherence, with generations averaging 2.7k characters at step 25, 12k at step 50, 76k at step 75 and 99k (the token ceiling) from step 125, while the parsable-answer rate falls 99% → 33%. — §4.3, Table 5, Fig. 4
>
> Stated caveat on wrong consensus: "Majority-vote supervision reproduces whichever answer the base model already produces most often, so it is bounded by that answer's accuracy. Our probe measured 13.3% wrong pseudo-labels in-domain, and we did not measure training dynamics under deliberately corrupted votes." Also stated: the advantage is regime-dependent (+3.2/+2.3 over OPSD non-thinking but +0.9/+0.02 thinking), scope is one model family and one auto-checkable domain, and "seed-replicated error bars for every training setup are pending and will be added in a revision." — §5

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the single most transferable result is Fig. 5(a) — stripping the teacher's privileged context down to the boxed answer alone costs 10.3–15.8% and drops all three label-only settings *below* the untrained base. That kills the cheap version of any "condition the teacher on the answer" scheme I might build: the reference has to be a full reasoning trace, not a label, which changes what I would need to store per prompt. Table 4 belongs next to my own token-level distillation choices — full-vocabulary forward KL at 57.10 versus 43.45 when you only score the token the student drew is a 13.7-point gap, and top-100 truncation at 59.01 actually beats full vocabulary, so the dense channel is doing the work and top-k is a free cost lever rather than a compromise. §4.3's reverse-KL trace is the diagnostic I would steal outright: characters per generation going 2.7k → 12k → 76k → 99k while the parsable-answer rate falls 99% → 33% is a clean, cheap early-warning signature for length blowup under a self-generated teacher, and it is exactly the failure shape I watch for in long-horizon runs. And the threshold sweep is the uncomfortable read: τ=0.3 beats the paper's own default τ=0.5 monotonically, which means most of the gain comes from having *more* prompts in the distillation set rather than from filtering out bad votes — worth checking before copying the gate.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Post-cutoff distillation** — this is the same skeleton that note sketches, minus the external fact source: dense per-token forward KL from a privileged-context teacher onto student-visited prefixes. Two direct hits on my open questions — cross-tokenizer alignment vanishes by construction because teacher and student are one network scored under different conditioning, and their gating analogue is the pair (c(x) ≥ τ, non-empty disagreement set), i.e. supervise only where the model is confidently wrong. Their Table 4 result that full-vocabulary beats sampled-token by 13.7 points argues my per-token reward should stay a full-distribution divergence rather than collapse to a scalar at the sampled token. **Over-reflection in search agents** — the skip rule is the training-time twin of my stop condition: when all valid rollouts already agree, the prompt yields no gradient because there is nothing to correct, and c(x) is a verifier-free scalar for "the model has already converged on an answer". That is the state-conditioned stop signal my note wants, computed from agreement rather than from a reward model. Stretch on transfer: their setting is single-turn math with a canonicalizable boxed answer, so mapping c(x) onto a tool-using trajectory needs a consensus function over answers extracted mid-rollout, which the paper explicitly does not have. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a different answer to the same waste problem. GRPO turns G rollouts into one scalar advantage per rollout; u-OPSD keeps the majority rollout as teacher context and spends the gradient on the minority, which their probe says is under 10% of valid rollouts, so the extraction per sampled batch is much richer — though the rollouts are still consumed on-policy and discarded, not replayed. Their G ablation (G=4 and G=8 tied at 56.99/57.10, G=12 +4.7%, sampling cost linear in G) is the cost curve my off-policy-reuse question is trying to beat. **AgentPlanet** — mechanically this is one model playing two roles under asymmetric conditioning, which is the same factorization trick my note applies to authoring/policy/world-model, and it shows the roles can be separated by context alone with no extra parameters. It also supplies a concrete reward-channel-integrity data point: the paper admits majority-vote supervision is bounded by the base model's modal answer, measures 13.3% wrong pseudo-labels, and states it did *not* test deliberately corrupted votes — the self-generated signal has no integrity check at all.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the two-sided gate as a verifier-free data selector for my own on-policy distillation — sample G, drop prompts with c(x) < τ (vote untrusted) *and* prompts with zero disagreement (nothing to correct), then spend gradient only on the disagreeing completions. It is an automatic difficulty curriculum with no labels and no scheduler, though I would sweep τ downward first given their own Fig. 4. The open question they hand me is the reward-integrity experiment: they measured 13.3% wrong pseudo-labels but explicitly did not run training under deliberately corrupted votes, and propose vote-margin down-weighting only as a "natural next step". Injecting controlled wrong-consensus rates and measuring where self-distillation goes from self-correcting to self-confirming is a cheap study on a 4B model and speaks directly to my anti-Goodhart gate question.

[Source (arXiv 2608.06296)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06296) · [code](https://github.com/williamium3000/u-opsd)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>The Next Challenge for Agentic Cybersecurity: A Realistic, Contamination-Free Reverse Engineering Benchmark</strong> · Columbia + UC Berkeley + Vals AI + Tufts + UCLA, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.11469v1, submitted 11 Aug 2026; cs.CR/cs.AI/cs.SE) presenting SRE-Bench, a
defensive evaluation benchmark for agentic binary reverse engineering built to be contamination-free by
construction: 19 programs (321,401 total LoC, averaging 16,915.8 each) written from scratch against
private design specifications that are never released, paired with a from-scratch 27K-LoC anti-analysis
suite of 44 protection primitives. Compiling and hardening those programs yields 262 binary instances
and 1,572 deterministically graded tasks across five domains. Its headline claim is that strong
source-code security capability does not transfer to binary analysis — across five frontier models the
best scores 3.69/6 (61.4%) and fully solves 31.5% of instances — and that controlled ablations show
contamination control and real-world scale are each individually necessary for the benchmark to
separate models at all. The benchmark deliberately excludes downstream tasks such as exploit
development, because including them "would conflate an agent's RE ability with its separate
cybersecurity skill".*

**From the report**

> Construction is clean-room by design: 19 programs written from scratch by RE experts averaging six years of experience, each "independently implemented from scratch based on a private design specification that is never released", spanning C/C++/Rust/Go with distinct library stacks — "No two programs share source code, and none is derived from a public project" — totalling 321,401 LoC at 16,915.8 average, i.e. "30–470× larger than prior RE benchmarks". More than 5,000 expert hours cover the programs, their evaluation infrastructure **and** the protection layer. — §3 (Development Methodology), §5, Table 2
>
> The adversarial reward-hacking audit is a build-time loop, not a post-hoc check: "Adversarial reward-hacking audits further close the loop by pitting agents against in-progress instances under the real grader and patching every shortcut that earns credit without requiring genuine RE"; the same evaluation pipeline is reused during development to run these audits (with Opus 4.8 as the auditing agent), "whose findings drive iterative hardening of both the programs and the infrastructure". — §3, Fig. 1 caption
>
> In the one worked example, the shortcut is fixed *and* frozen into a deterministic gate: "an early build retained the internal name of a hidden behavior (i.e., the target of an RE task) as a readable string in the binary; an agent therefore located the corresponding trigger through string scanning without doing any actual RE. We removed the string and added a regression check that fails the build on any similar leak." Comparable build-time gates exist elsewhere — a hygiene check that greps the shipped stripped binary for forbidden identifiers, and an encoder-only symbol-scan and import-graph gate — though the paper does not attribute those to the audit loop. Separately, "Every program ships with a private reference solution that scores a perfect 6/6, which serves as both a solvability proof and a regression gate." — §3, §A.2, §A.3
>
> Verifier design is per-domain, held-out and silent — the grader never lives in the artifact the agent can read: a live server scoring protocol state-space depth over a single metered TCP connection capped at 64 messages with a fresh per-session key; a headless verifier that replays a submitted action trace; byte-and-metadata-exact round-trip decoding with "no partial credit within a level"; a sandboxed infect-then-clean grader whose scoring is "conjunctive, with one point awarded per family only if the tool removes the malicious state, preserves the benign state, and durably neutralizes the corresponding behavior"; and for firmware, "The scoring logic resides in the emulator rather than the firmware and is never exposed to the agent." — §3.1, §A.1–§A.5
>
> Eval harness is deliberately minimal so scaffolding does not confound the comparison: five frontier models (GPT-5.6-sol, Claude-Opus-5, GPT-5.5, Grok-4.5, GLM-5.2) each run "inside a standardized mini-SWE-agent harness whose only tool is bash", at highest reasoning effort, capped at 500 model steps and six hours wall-clock, one isolated container per instance holding the binary and a standard RE toolkit. "Grader code, reference solutions, and any development artifacts are removed from the container, and agents receive no scoring feedback during a run." — §4
>
> Headline results: GPT-5.6-sol 3.69±0.14 of 6 (61.4%), 80 instances fully solved (31.5%), 41 scoring zero (16.1%), 196 calls / $42.5 / 88.1 min per instance; Claude-Opus-5 1.91 (31.8%), 32 solved (12.5%), 118 zero (46.1%); GPT-5.5 1.02 (17.1%); Grok-4.5 0.45 (7.6%); GLM-5.2 0.21 (3.4%), 0 solved, 217 zero (82.8%). "The ordering is wide as well as strict: GPT-5.6-sol scores 1.9× the next model and 17× the last." Total sweep cost $31.4K and 1,812 sandbox-hours. Effort does not buy capability — GLM-5.2 spends $26.6 per instance against GPT-5.5's $17.8 "for a fifth of the score". — §4, Table 4
>
> Both design axes are load-bearing, shown by ablation: "Minimal", a clean-room compressor of ~1.1k LoC, is fully solved 8/8 by both models for under $1.60 and seven minutes; "Gzip-variant", the gzip encoder with roughly 200 lines of its critical path modified, is also recovered 8/8 for roughly $2 in ten minutes despite being a deployed real-world program; only "RevCompress", which is both clean-room and at real-world scale, separates the models (5.62 and 7/8 vs 3.75 and 3/8) at 10–30× the cost and time. "Recognition therefore substitutes for analysis." Separately, symbol stripping costs the top model 0.48 points and takes GPT-5.5 from 2.53 to 1.14, while optimization costs only 0.08 — "current agents lean on lexical anchors… far more than on the instruction-level analysis that optimization degrades." — §4, Table 5, Fig. 3
>
> Stated limitations: "SRE-Bench is built from 19 programs. This is a small pool in absolute terms, and a larger one would tighten per-domain estimates, most of all for firmware, where three programs yield only six instances," and the authors argue the constraint is inherent because the cheap construction routes are exactly the ones their own ablation rules out. Ungradeable runs (refusals on cyber-security framing plus context-window overflow) are excluded from all averages — Grok-4.5 alone lost 44 of its 262 runs, 58 across all five models — and the protection suite "makes no claim of unbreakability". — §4, §5, App. B preamble

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3's Development Methodology paragraph with the Figure 1 caption — the audit loop is the transferable mechanism: run the auditing agent against an in-progress instance under the *real* grader, and when it earns credit without doing the work, patch the artifact and add a build-breaking regression check. That converts a one-off reward-hack fix into a permanent property of the generator, which is exactly the gate my synthetic environments lack. Table 5 is the control experiment I want to copy into dataset auditing: hold the eight build configurations fixed and vary only the target program, showing that gzip with ~200 lines of its critical path rewritten is solved 8/8 for ~$2 in ten minutes — as cheap as a 1.1k-LoC toy. That is a direct, cheap measurement of how much of a score is recognition rather than analysis, and I can run the same clean-room-vs-derived pairing on my own task sets instead of arguing about contamination in the abstract. §4's metrics plus Table 4 are the right instrumentation for a nearly-binary reward: reporting Score alongside Solve and Zero, with per-instance calls, cost and time in the same table, and a Zero column running 16.1% to 82.8% that tells me partial credit is the exception — which changes advantage estimation and curriculum choice far more than the mean does. §A.4's malware grading pipeline is the sharpest verifier template here: capture a private pre-infection baseline, plant benign and near-match fixtures, and make any modification to a pre-existing baseline object globally fatal, so "delete everything" scores zero. The conjunctive requirement that the agent both remove the bad state *and* preserve the good state is how I would make a verifier score restraint as well as action.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the most concrete instance I have seen of the anti-Goodhart-gates open question. The world-authoring term here is human-executed — program, protection layer and grader all authored from scratch against a private spec — and its integrity gates are two artifacts I can port directly: a private reference solution scoring a perfect 6/6 that doubles as solvability proof and regression gate, and an adversarial audit that pits an agent against the in-progress world under the real grader and patches every credit-earning shortcut. It is the reward-channel-integrity half of that note, made operational. **Post-cutoff distillation** — their contamination argument is the mirror image of my gating problem: I gate a distillation reward to knowledge the student could not already have had, they gate an eval to targets the model could not already have seen. Table 5 gives the quantity I have been missing — a real-world program with only ~200 critical-path lines changed is recovered as cheaply as a 1.1k-LoC toy, so "derived from public source with local edits" provides essentially zero gating. Their own paper-level control is also a usable recipe for writing publicly about a held-out set without leaking it: they verified that everything disclosed in the construction sections was independently recoverable from the binaries by an agent within 200 requests, and deliberately withhold exact constants, trigger conditions and reference solutions. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — the economics here make the case for off-policy reuse better than any argument I have written: $31.4K and 1,812 sandbox-hours for a single five-model sweep, $42.5 and 88 minutes per instance for the strongest model, and a reward that is near-binary. Long-horizon, expensive, mostly-zero-reward rollouts on a deterministic grader are precisely the regime where a replay buffer and a high update-to-data ratio should pay, and this paper supplies the per-rollout cost numbers to justify it. **Over-reflection in search agents** — partly a stretch, since the paper never studies stopping behavior. What it does supply is the decoupling of effort from outcome on the axis I care about: 115–196 API calls and 46–149 minutes per instance with no corresponding score, one model spending $26.6 against another's $17.8 for a fifth of the score, and runs lost outright to context-window overflow under a 500-step / six-hour cap.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the "patch becomes a build gate" discipline into environment and task synthesis — every shortcut the auditing agent finds should end as a deterministic check that fails the build, not as a manual fix, paired with their solvability invariant (a private reference solution that must score full marks on every build) so solvability and unhackability are both enforced continuously rather than sampled. The open question the paper leaves is a stopping rule for the audit itself: they say they patch "every shortcut that earns credit without requiring genuine RE" but never report how many were found, per round or in total, so there is no measure of audit saturation. If the grader is going to be an RL reward rather than a leaderboard, I want shortcuts-found-per-audit-round tracked and trending to zero before I trust the channel — that metric costs almost nothing to log and is the difference between "we audited" and "the audit converged".

[Source (arXiv 2608.11469)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11469)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Agent Memory: Characterization and System Implications of Stateful Long-Horizon Workloads</strong> · Stanford + KU Leuven + MIT, June 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2606.06448v1, submitted 4 Jun 2026; cs.AI, 12 pages) presenting a systems-measurement
study of "agent memory" — the external, mutable state that long-horizon agents write, read and update
across sessions — positioned as the first systems rather than accuracy characterization of this class.
It defines a taxonomy of four paradigms along four axes, builds a phase-aware profiling harness that
attributes tokens, calls, GPU utilization, latency and energy to construction / retrieval / generation,
and profiles ten memory systems on two benchmark suites. Its headline claim is that the dominant cost
of agent memory is not query-time serving but memory **construction** — the write path — which is
prefill- and embedding-dominated and in direct scheduling conflict with latency-sensitive QA when
co-located on one serving stack.*

**From the report**

> Method: agent memory execution is decomposed into seven stages — ingestion, memory construction, storage, retrieval, prompt assembly, generation, maintenance — and systems classified into four paradigms (I long-context, II flat RAG, III.a append-only structure-augmented, III.b consolidating structure-augmented, IV agentic control flow). A phase-aware harness records API telemetry (call type, latency, prompt/completion/embedding tokens) and hardware telemetry (NVML + DCGM-class GPM: device power, GPU utilization, VRAM, SM activity, tensor-core activity, HBM bandwidth) on one monotonic timeline, with phase energy computed by integrating device power over each interval. — §2.1, §2.2, §3.3
>
> Measurement recipe: two construction regimes — remote (GPT-4o-mini or GPT-4.1-mini + text-embedding-3-small, matching systems' original release configs) and local (one NVIDIA H100 80GB HBM3 via vLLM, six Intel Xeon Platinum 8480C cores, one isolated Slurm job per system), with an LLM ladder of Qwen3-32B / 14B / 8B / 1.7B and Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B. Qwen3-32B and 14B use FP8 weight quantization specifically to keep KV-cache headroom for long prefill; LLM server gets 75% of GPU memory, embedding server 15%; thinking mode disabled everywhere. — §3.2
>
> Construction dominates the lifecycle: on LongMemEval (Qwen3-32B, 5 samples totaling 1.8M tokens of history, n=300 queries), end-to-end energy spans over 26× from BM25 at 582 kJ to Letta at 15,429 kJ, wall time from 16.3 min to 14.36 h, and for LLM-mediated systems construction energy exceeds the total query-phase energy of all 300 queries. Normalized as energy per correct answer the Table 3 spread is 45×, from BM25 at 4,128 J to Letta at 185,873 J (A-Mem 116.1 kJ, MIRIX 144.6 kJ) — note the §4.2 prose quotes a 4,145 J floor and a 197 kJ top that its own table does not support. — §4.2, Table 3, Fig. 4
>
> The write path is prefill- and embedding-shaped, not decode-shaped: median decode share of total construction tokens is 4.6% (0.9% for Letta to 28.5% for SimpleMem), making construction a repeated long-read / short-write workload. Embedding traffic is bimodal by paradigm — GraphRAG issues ~2,300 sequences per embedding call while Mem0 must embed each extracted fact before its similarity search resolves ADD/UPDATE/DELETE, a 1:1 call-to-sequence ratio — and Fig. 5(a.ii) shows this directly as HMMA%, DRAM-bandwidth% and device-power traces (long batched plateaus vs sequential per-fact spikes). — §4.3
>
> Serving latency spans two orders of magnitude across systems on identical local hardware (vLLM, single H100); in the remote OpenAI-API regime the same spread appears as Mem0 answering in under 0.1 s per query against ~38 s for long-context. Effective time-to-first-token runs ~0.10 s to ~22.6 s, and tail shape splits by regime: algorithm-bounded pipelines sit at p95/p50 ≈ 1.3× (BM25, embedRAG) and 1.6× (HippoRAG v2), versus LLM-bounded pipelines at 5.9× (GraphRAG) and 3.9× (Letta). — §4.1, §4.8, Figs. 2, 10–11
>
> Downscaling the construction LLM is an algorithm-constrained lever, not a free one: with the QA LLM and embeddings fixed, systems without hard output contracts (Mem0, SimpleMem, A-Mem) degrade smoothly down the ladder and GraphRAG holds ~47–48% from Qwen3-1.7B all the way to GPT-4o-mini, while MIRIX fails entirely at Qwen3-1.7B because its sub-agent pipeline requires well-formed JSON schemas and legal tool-call syntax — falling below that capability floor "produces a corrupted store from which the QA model can recover no useful evidence". — §4.4
>
> Freshness-latency tradeoff under continuous session arrival (20 multi-session tasks, traces replayed at a controlled 5 s inter-session interval): staleness is defined as the number of prior sessions not yet persisted at query time, and under asynchronous scheduling SimpleMem, MIRIX, Letta, Mem0 and A-Mem accumulate one or more sessions of staleness while Paradigm II stays fresh in both modes. Per-session construction time spans five orders of magnitude, from ~10⁻³ s (BM25) to ~10 s (Mem0, MIRIX) with tails exceeding 100 s when inputs trigger multiple consolidation rounds. Storage grows monotonically because no evaluated system prunes or forgets by default. — §4.6, §4.7
>
> Stated caveats: BM25's top macro-average (55.8%) "should not be read as a universal result" because many datasets are recall-heavy and reward exact-match lexical retrieval, and on paraphrase-matching, multi-hop and temporal-inference categories the gap narrows or reverses; "a perfectly apples-to-apples comparison is not possible" given implementation degrees of freedom; and the characterization is single-node, excluding multi-node/multi-agent consistency and multimodal memory. Two things a reader has to supply themselves: accuracy throughout is *LLM-judge* accuracy (an axis label in Figs. 2/6/7b) and the paper never names the judge model or its protocol; and the paper reports no measured prefix-cache hit rates, KV-cache occupancy figures, or read/write amplification numbers — that material is qualitative motivation only. — §3.1, §4.5, §5

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §4.2 and Table 3 first, for the joules-per-correct-answer normalization — pricing total lifecycle energy by *correct answers* rather than by calls is exactly the denominator I keep wanting for synthesis and eval fleets, where a stage that looks cheap per call hides a low accept rate, and the 45× spread in that column is what per-call accounting would conceal. §4.4 is a design rule for any pipeline stage I try to downscale: extractors without hard output contracts degrade smoothly down the Qwen3 ladder, but a system whose JSON-schema and tool-call contracts stop being satisfiable collapses to zero — a cliff, not a curve, and it corrupts the store rather than just lowering quality. §4.3's token decomposition belongs read before co-locating anything: a median 4.6% decode share means the memory write path is prefill/embedding-bound and behaves like background indexing, which is the concrete argument for not sharing one endpoint between a state-building job and a latency-sensitive rollout or eval server. And §4.6 for the staleness metric rather than the systems conclusion — "number of prior sessions not yet persisted at query time", measured by replaying real per-session traces at a fixed arrival interval, is a cleanly computable quantity I could drop straight into a multi-session agent eval harness. One caution before quoting any accuracy number from this paper: they are all LLM-judge accuracies with the judge never specified.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **The energy floor of inference** — §3.3 is a working instrument for the open half of that note: phase energy computed by integrating NVML/DCGM device power over phase-marked intervals, reported as kJ per lifecycle and joules per correct answer. It also supplies a quantization data point in the same frame — FP8 on Qwen3-32B/14B specifically to keep KV-cache headroom for long prefill on a single 80 GB card, i.e. quantization buying sustained-workload feasibility rather than just throughput. **AgentPlanet** — the seven-stage pipeline separates the maintenance/construction write path from the policy's read path, and §4.2 prices them: for LLM-mediated systems, construction energy exceeds the query-phase energy of all 300 queries combined. That is a direct empirical statement about my factorization — the state-update/transition role, not the action role, is where the compute actually goes — and §4.7's finding that Paradigm IV construction cost grows super-linearly with store size says the transition term gets more expensive the longer the world has existed. **Over-reflection in search agents** — §4.8's algorithm-bounded vs LLM-bounded distinction is the same phenomenon I measure as confirm-then-keep-searching: a phase that runs until the LLM decides it is done, producing p95/p50 tails of 5.9× and 3.9× against 1.3× for fixed-depth pipelines. Their remedy is an external iteration cap; mine is a state-conditioned stop/pivot policy — and their p95/p50 tail ratio is a cheap proxy metric I can compute over my own rollouts to quantify unbounded continuation. **Wearable world model** — partly a stretch: their argument and §4.7's measurement together say external memory decouples capacity from context length, since retrieval latency stays nearly flat as the store grows to ~1M tokens while full-history prefill scales quadratically and prefix caching fails across sessions. For constrained silicon that favors a small model plus an external store over a longer context window, but the paper never evaluates edge-class hardware, so the transfer is mine, not theirs.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the joules-per-correct-answer normalization to cost-per-*accepted*-unit across my synthesis and eval pipelines — the paper shows systems with near-identical accuracy differing by an order of magnitude on that axis, which is precisely the failure mode a per-call or per-token cost table cannot surface. The open question the paper asserts but never measures: its own recommendation says high-volume query workloads against stable histories should move work into construction and continuous-ingestion/sparse-query workloads should not, yet no break-even query count is computed anywhere. The amortization threshold is the number I would actually need, and it is the same shape as the reuse question in **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — how many downstream uses justify an expensive one-time build.

[Source (arXiv 2606.06448)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06448)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>HopRefusalBench: Diagnosing Refusal Failures in Search-Augmented Agents for Multi-Hop Reasoning</strong> · ShanghaiTech + CASIA + Ant Group, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.01358v1, submitted 2 Aug 2026; cs.CL, 20 pages, CC BY 4.0) that builds a
controlled benchmark of 889 **unanswerable** multi-hop questions by fusing single-hop unanswerable
seeds into entity paths traversed on the KILT Wikipedia hyperlink graph, crossing three causes of
unanswerability with three defect positions at chain depths of 2–5. It pairs a four-way final-outcome
taxonomy with source-aware trajectory metrics that time-stamp the first turn at which sufficient
evidence for the correct non-answer is available, then measure what the agent does afterward. Across
ten frontier proprietary and open-weight models run in paired no-search / search-augmented modes, the
best search-mode target-aware correct halting rate is 42.9%, and turning retrieval on overturns
correct refusals far more often than it rescues incorrect ones.*

**From the report**

> Construction and composition: 889 questions built by fusing AU/FP/UC seeds (CoCoNot, BIG-Bench, KUQ, FalseQA, (QA)², WorldSense) with paths on the KILT hyperlink graph — backlink traversal builds a valid prefix toward a "backward anchor", outgoing-link traversal a dependent suffix from a "forward bridge"; candidate edges restricted to hyperlinks in opening body paragraphs, filtered to node degree in [10,200], an LLM ranking the five most coherent candidates with one sampled uniformly; 1–4 KG hops plus the non-hop unanswerable step give depths 2–5. The set is 486 false-premise / 317 answer-unknown / 86 underspecified-context, and by topology 442 Terminal / 216 Root / 231 Middle. — §2.1–2.3, §2.5, Table 1
>
> Metric stack: refusal reliability (TCHR, PPHR, URHR, WRHR, SHDR, SEHR over all N items); post-trigger efficiency anchored at an accounting turn A_i derived from the first sufficient-evidence turn T_trap (A_i = T_trap if the assistant itself states the gold rationale, else min(T_trap+1, L_i)), giving PTOS@>0, PTTW and EIFR; and paired-mode shift metrics SIOR (no-search TACH → non-TACH under search) and SIRR (the reverse). — §3.2–3.3, Table 2
>
> Headline: across ten models in search-augmented mode the best target-aware correct halting rate is only 42.9% (Qwen3.5-397B-A17B); GPT-5.4 25.2%, Claude-Sonnet-5 26.8%, GLM-5.1 lowest at 13.8%. — §4.2, Table 3
>
> Search destabilizes more than it rescues: TCHR falls under search for nine of ten models (Qwen3.6-27B 34.4% → 16.2%, DeepSeek-V4-Pro 31.6% → 17.0%); search overturns 20.4–67.3% of initially correct refusals (SIOR) but rescues only 4.2–16.9% of initially non-target-aware outcomes (SIRR). — §4.2 Key insight 1, Table 3
>
> Over-search after decisive evidence: 11.6–57.9% of evidence-exposed trajectories issue at least one more tool call after the accounting turn, generating 704–10,321 post-trigger tokens on average (Kimi-K2.5 57.9% / 10,321 tokens; GPT-5.4 11.6% / 704). EIFR — observing sufficient evidence, searching more, then answering through the defect — reaches 42.4% for GLM-5.1; non-SEH evidence coverage is 40.7–60.4%. — §4.2 Key insight 4, Table S2(a)
>
> Bottleneck localization: pooled across categories, 84.7–98.4% of each model's refusal-like responses give the gold rationale (cTCHR); GLM-5.1 attains only 13.8% TCHR yet 123 of its 125 refusal-like responses are target-aware, so low TCHR reflects failure to commit to a non-answer rather than rationale error after commitment. — §4.2 Key insight 4
>
> Harness: local retriever over a 2018 Wikipedia snapshot with E5 dense embeddings, top-3 documents per query, at most eight search calls per trajectory, native tool-calling with parallel calls preserved, no system message and no instruction to abstain, explicit thinking modes disabled and greedy decoding *where supported*; GLM-5.1 in thinking mode is the behavior + timeline judge, with human agreement 87.2% (75/86) on behavior state (Cohen's κ 0.821, macro-F1 0.814) and 95.3% (41/43) on existence of a sufficient-evidence point. Each item is run once per model per mode and the trajectory used exactly as returned. — §4.1, App. B, App. C
>
> Caveats the paper states: a manual 10% audit retains 80 of 89 sampled questions (89.9%) under the core criteria (category 98.9%, refusal need 98.9%, question coherent 95.5%, gold rationale exact 94.4%) — the exact chain-depth label (85/89, 95.5%) is audited but deliberately excluded from retainability "because a counting error can leave the question's refusal signal intact"; the diagnostic case studies were chosen after the aggregate patterns and "are not used to estimate prevalence"; EIFR for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B rests on only 16 observation-sourced triggers versus 90–187 for other models; and the KILT-vs-2018-snapshot mismatch is checked by rerunning the strongest model on a KILT-aligned 2019 snapshot, moving TCHR 42.9 → 44.4 (Δ +1.6 pp, a rerun that "conservatively includes ordinary run-level trajectory variation"), with the largest absolute change across all columns being 1.9 pp on SIRR (16.9 → 18.8). — §2.4, App. A.2, App. C.1, Table S1

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §3.2–3.3 with App. B's timeline qualification rules first — their accounting turn A_i (T_trap if the model itself states the rationale, else the turn after the retrieved evidence) is a cleaner zero point than the one I use for confirm-then-keep-searching, and the rule that sparse results, generic uncertainty and repeated failed searches do *not* qualify as evidence is exactly the discipline my post-evidence-waste counter is missing. Table 3's paired SIOR/SIRR columns are a cheap ablation I can run on any checkpoint — same items, tools on vs tools off — and 20.4–67.3% of correct refusals overturned against only 4.2–16.9% rescued is a tool-use tax a single-mode eval structurally cannot show. Key insight 3 is the one to read before designing a reward: SHDR and SEHR trade against each other (GPT-5.4 72.7% / 0.1% vs Qwen3.6-27B 26.1% / 54.8%, with six of ten models flipping their dominant channel between FP and UC), so a verifier that only penalizes hallucinated completion pushes mass into budget exhaustion rather than into halting. One reading caution: the sentence "retrieval can suppress abstention and compound over-searching" appears in this paper only in §5, describing prior work — the paper's own equivalent result is Key insight 1, so quote the SIOR/SIRR asymmetry, not that line.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — this is my phenomenon measured on unanswerable items: PTOS@>0 and PTTW are the direct analogue of the 63% confirm-then-keep-searching rate, but anchored to a judged first-sufficient-evidence turn instead of a first answer mention, and EIFR up to 42.4% is precisely the labeled negative a stop/pivot policy needs; cTCHR at 84.7–98.4% (GLM-5.1, 123/125 target-aware at 13.8% TCHR) says the trainable object is the commitment decision, not the reasoner. **AgentPlanet** — the SHDR/SEHR trade is a reward-channel-integrity failure of exactly the kind my anti-Goodhart gates are for: suppressing one failure channel migrates probability mass into another rather than into correct halting, so a gate has to score both channels *plus* a no-decision state; their topology-controlled authoring (root/middle/terminal defect insertion into an otherwise coherent KG-generated chain, with a cause-fidelity audit) is world authoring as a controllable generative process with a validity gate, though mapping it onto the three-term factorization is a stretch since the paper models neither policy nor transitions. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — stretch, but pointed: up to 54.8% of items (Qwen3.6-27B) burn the entire eight-call budget and return nothing, and this paper keeps those SEH trajectories as a first-class labeled state rather than discarding them as failures; a six-state taxonomy over exactly the rollouts a pipeline would normally throw away is the labeling layer that would make reuse of expensive search rollouts gradable. **Post-cutoff distillation** — stretch: their Answer Unknown category includes future/unknowable facts (the case cards use a 2050 Olympic host and the next economic bubble, though the paper reports no breakdown of how much of AU is future-dated), and the model must separate "not knowable by anyone" from "not known to me" — the same knowledge-boundary detector my gating needs, run in the opposite direction.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the post-trigger accounting rule into my over-search metric — condition the denominator on evidence-exposed trajectories only, set the zero point at A_i, and require that an observation directly contradict the false premise, expose the missing specification, or establish that the fact is not known before it counts. That turns "wasted rounds" from a heuristic count into something with a defensible start time, and their 704–10,321 token spread is a scale I can compare against. The open question they leave: with 84.7–98.4% of refusal-like outputs already carrying the correct rationale, the deficit is entirely at commitment — yet the paper reports no fine-tuning or RL baseline at all, no attempt to train a stop head on (state at T_trap → halt) pairs, which its own T_trap annotations would supply almost for free.

[Source (arXiv 2608.01358)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01358) · [code](https://github.com/JiananXie/HopRefusalBench)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>HALT: Verification-Aware Stopping for Retrieval-Augmented Search Agents</strong> · Independent + KAIST, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.02009, v1 3 Aug 2026, v2 4 Aug 2026; cs.AI) that reframes the stopping decision
in iterative retrieval agents as an evidence-coverage test rather than a generator-confidence test.
HALT (Hop-Aligned Loop Termination) bolts a small supervised per-hop verifier onto a frozen Self-Ask
agent: it labels each (expected hop claim, retrieved sentence) pair match/partial/null, keeps a
running-max label per claim across loops, and halts at the first loop where every expected hop claim
is match. Evaluated on HotpotQA, 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue (n=1000 each) with 3B and 7B Self-Ask
backbones under an 8-loop budget, it reports 20–45% fewer search loops in the diagnostic gold-claim
setting — where 2 of 3 cells pass exact-match non-inferiority — and shows the same signal flags
under-search in the other direction.*

**From the report**

> Core rule: cumulative evidence E_i is the union of retrieved sentences; a verifier V_θ labels each claim–sentence pair match/partial/null and "for each claim, HALT keeps the strongest label observed so far", ordered match > partial > null; the default all_match policy halts at the first loop where every expected hop claim is match. It is a **between-loop runtime gate** over a frozen agent — the host policy, retriever and generator are never retrained, and there is no RL-learned or value-style controller over trajectory state — but the per-hop verifier itself is trained once offline. "HALT leaves the host search agent unchanged." — §3.1
>
> Positioning against prior stopping signals: Self-RAG uses reflection-token critics, Stop-RAG "learns a value controller over trajectory state", SIM-RAG scores sufficiency of a (question, evidence, intermediate answer) tuple, SeaKR uses internal-state uncertainty — "a common thread is that these signals estimate generator confidence, internal-state uncertainty, or global answer sufficiency", whereas HALT's predicate "asks whether each required hop is covered, not whether the current trajectory or candidate answer is globally sufficient." — §2
>
> Training recipe: Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct + LoRA (r=16, α=32, dropout 0.05, 4-bit NF4), supervised fine-tuning to emit the label string with no classification head; at inference each label is scored by length-normalized token log-probability and argmax is taken. "The balanced training set contains 9,626 rows from 4,903 questions", 1,000 steps, 0.9304 macro-F1 on a question-disjoint dev split of 2,374 rows. The verifier is trained once on HotpotQA discrepancy labels and reused unchanged across all datasets and both agent scales. — §3.3, App. A
>
> Headline, 3B agent with gold claims: 2Wiki EM 0.184 → 0.199 (+1.5 pp) at 5.52 → 3.46 loops (−37%), PASS; HotpotQA 0.306 → 0.297 (−0.9 pp) at 5.71 → 3.15 loops (−45%), **FAIL** non-inferiority because its 95% CI [−2.6, +0.8] pp crosses the margin; MuSiQue 0.083 → 0.086 (+0.3 pp) at 5.56 → 4.47 loops (−20%), PASS. The deployable generated-claim condition passes EM non-inferiority 3/3 at smaller savings: −17% / −18% / −2% loops. — Tables 1 and 4, §5.1
>
> Bidirectional use of the same signal: on the 7B agent, all_match is never satisfied at the agent's native stop on 51% / 64% / 79% of HotpotQA / 2Wiki / MuSiQue questions. Forcing continuation on the HotpotQA under-covered subset (n=511) roughly doubles EM from 0.162 to 0.323 (+16.0 pp), and beats continuing to the full 8-loop budget (0.311 EM, i.e. 1.2 pp worse at more loops). Full-population deltas from selectively continuing only flagged trajectories: HotpotQA +8.2 pp gold / +7.1 pp generated, all six cells with strictly positive 95% CIs. — §5.3
>
> Eval harness: host agent is Self-Ask with Qwen2.5-3B/7B-Instruct, max 8 loops; Setting A is a closed pool — per-question BM25 sentence index over the benchmark's gold plus distractor paragraphs, top-K=3 — chosen to "isolate the stopping problem from open-corpus retrieval failure"; n=1000 dev questions per dataset. Primary endpoint is Std-Ext EM from a frozen Qwen2.5-3B extractor over cumulative chunks at the stop loop; paired bootstrap with 1000 resamples, non-inferiority margin ε = 2 pp, Holm correction with m=12 (3B family) and m=6 (7B), seed 13. — §3.4, §4
>
> Cost accounting the paper states against itself: "Raw token accounting is net positive for HALT on all six arms (net +4,993 to +8,503 raw tokens/query): the verifier's cheap parallel-prefill scoring processes more raw tokens than the expensive sequential decode it avoids. We therefore state our cost claim in search iterations and wall-clock, not raw tokens." Measured end-to-end on HotpotQA (n=100): full 47.40 s/query vs 30.94 s (−35%) for HALT-gold and 40.62 s (−14%) for HALT-gen, verifier only 5–7% of total time. — §5.7
>
> Caveat the paper itself states: "Our 3B Self-Ask agent rarely halts on its own — it runs to the search budget on 78–88% of questions — and HALT removes much of that redundancy, whereas the 7B agent already stops natively on nearly all questions, leaving little headroom, and HALT's savings shrink accordingly" (7B budget exhaustion 0.0–0.5%; 7B gold savings fall to −6.3% / −9.3% / −6.6%). It further concedes the claim set "is fixed before search begins", and that main statistical claims hold only in closed-pool Setting A and "should therefore be read as evidence that evidence-coverage stopping can reduce redundant search in controlled multi-hop QA, not as a complete deployment study." — Limitations

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §5.3 before anything else — the same coverage predicate that cuts loops also emits a per-question "this trajectory stopped too early" label, and their force-continue experiment shows continuing *to coverage* (0.323 EM) beats continuing to the full budget (0.311 EM at ~8 loops).
