That is a stop-versus-pivot target I can compute offline on existing rollouts, not just a runtime gate. §5.7 is the section I would make myself read before quoting any efficiency number of my own: raw tokens go *up* by 4,993–8,503 per query, and the only honest wins are search iterations and wall-clock — any stopping mechanism pays a verifier prefill tax, and the accounting unit decides whether the result exists at all. App. A's input-component ablations are the check I care about most for verifier integrity: masking the answer surface costs 0.002 macro-F1 while dropping the hop claim costs 0.218, direct evidence the verifier scores claim–evidence alignment rather than recognizing the answer string. And §5.6 tells me where this generalizes: swapping BM25-closed-pool for E5 over open Wikipedia collapses the fire rate 67.6% → 6.4%, which the paper reframes as selective prediction (EM 0.391 on the fired subset vs a 0.109 population baseline, a 3.58× lift) — coverage verification is a distribution-bound signal, so porting it means re-deriving discrepancy labels under my own retriever, not reusing a checkpoint.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the same phenomenon measured from the retrieval side instead of the trajectory side. My 63% confirm-then-keep-searching rate has a direct analogue in their logged stop reason (the 3B agent runs to budget rather than halting natively on 87.5% / 78.1% / 81.2% of questions), and the all_match gate removes 20–45% of those loops with 2 of 3 gold cells passing paired EM non-inferiority. It answers part of my open state-conditioned stop question concretely: the state they condition on is a running-max match/partial/null vector over *pre-declared hop claims*, not policy hidden state or generator confidence — and §5.3 adds the failure mode my taxonomy does not yet cover, under-search on 51/64/79% of 7B native stops. **AgentPlanet** — the link is my anti-Goodhart-gate question, not the factorization. Their §6.1 / App. E.9 battery is exactly the "does the gate fire for the intended reason" test I want a world builder to have to pass: swapping in another question's hop claims collapses the HotpotQA fire rate 0.676 → 0.196, shuffling word order inside retrieved chunks collapses it to 0.077, and on 2Wiki restoring only the gold page field recovers almost nothing (0.034 → 0.105). That is validating a verifier channel by falsification rather than by asserting its accuracy. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — their §5.3 design is selective recompute rather than off-policy reuse: they re-run only the flagged under-covered subset and leave the rest at native stop, buying +8.2 pp full-population EM from a fraction of the budget, with a mechanism control (+26.2 pp where coverage recovered vs +2.7 pp where it did not) showing the flag targets the payoff. Stretch on the learning side: no replay buffer, no update-to-data ratio, and no gradient step *inside* the search loop — the verifier is trained once offline and then frozen, and the agent itself is never trained.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the negative-control battery wholesale as a gate on any stop/pivot verifier I train — cross-question claim swap, page-field-only restoration, within-chunk word shuffle. Their numbers give me a pass bar: if my verifier's fire rate does not collapse from ~0.68 to under 0.20 when handed another question's targets, and under 0.10 under word shuffle, it is matching surface form and does not belong in a reward. The sharpest open question is in their own Limitations: "The claim set is also fixed before search begins; re-generating claims conditioned on the accumulated evidence is a natural extension that we leave to future work." That re-generation step is precisely where a stop rule becomes a pivot rule — the agent that discovers mid-trajectory that its decomposition was wrong needs new hop targets, not a verdict on the old ones.

[Source (arXiv 2608.02009)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02009) · [code](https://github.com/Noverse0/HALT)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Abstention as an Action Can Kill Both the Reward Gradient and the KL Anchor: Collapse Law and Repair for Error-Penalized Reinforcement Learning</strong> · UNC Charlotte + George Mason + Auburn + North Texas, July 2026</summary>

*A 21-page arXiv preprint (2608.00301v1, submitted 31 Jul 2026; cs.LG, cross-listed cs.CL — note the
2608 ID prefix, but the stated submission date is July) that analyzes error-penalized scoring rules
(+1 correct, −λ wrong, 0 abstain) not as a decision rule but as an RL objective for a KL-anchored
gradient learner. Its central claim is a "collapse law": when abstention is a discrete action behind a
saturating gate, the reward gradient and the KL anchor's restoring force are multiplied by the same
factor σ'(v) and die together, so the policy drifts toward refusing everything while mean training
reward rises toward zero like 1/t. The proposed repair moves abstention out of the action space into a
mandatory confidence report trained with a strictly proper score plus a correctness reward at weight
α > 1, thresholded only at deployment; evidence is theory plus exact enumeration, simulation, and
Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B RL runs on TriviaQA and PopQA.*

**From the report**

> Core mechanism: for the anchored objective, ∇J_β = ∫ σ'(v_θ)[g − β·p·(v_θ − v_0)] ∇v_θ ds — "Reward and anchor share the factor σ'(v_θ): where the gate saturates, both vanish." Collapse needs exactly three ingredients: blanket answering loses score in expectation (B1: E[q] < t* = λ/(1+λ)), prompts share a bounded readout, and abstention is action-level; removing any one eliminates the collapse in the analyzed class, and Proposition 1 shows tabular policies with one free logit per prompt converge to Chow's rule instead. — Lemma 1, §3, §1 Contributions
>
> The law, and why the anchor cannot stop it: under gradient flow, c₁/(t log²t) ≤ L(t) ≤ c₂/t on a finite window — mean training reward rises to zero like 1/t while readout gain grows only as O(log t); the zero-crossing time "stabilizes as β goes to 0 (measured: 3.2% over two decades)", so the collapse clock is essentially independent of anchor strength. Corollary 1 names the responsible class: the entropy bonus and RLHF's forward KL(π_θ ‖ π_0) both have gradients that vanish at the boundary with the reward's, but reverse KL(π_0 ‖ π_θ) does not — its vertex cost diverges and it floors the answer rate at Θ(β). Forward KL leaves only P_floor ~ exp(−D_eff/β), about 1e-13 at β = 0.01. — Theorem 1, Corollary 1, §3
>
> The advantage estimator rewrites the rule: with group-std normalization there is a knee at per-prompt answer rate p ~ 1/G. Above it, drift is Θ(√p) and L decays as 1/t²; **below** it a group contains at most one answer whose advantage is sign(r)·√(G−1) independent of magnitude, so "the designed penalty is erased, λ_eff = 1 for every nominal λ" and the drift changes sign at q̄ = 1/2 rather than t* — at λ = 4.5 the optimizer is trained to answer the whole band (0.50, 0.82) on which the rule assigns negative gain. The abstention rate on the contested band is set by G, not λ. — Propositions 6–7, Table 1, §3
>
> Resampling filters cannot fix it: DAPO-style dynamic sampling that discards degenerate zero-variance groups "conserves the expected reward drift per rollout exactly, for every p, G, q̄, λ", while spending about 1/(G·p) times more rollouts per step; per optimizer step the law becomes exponential, but per rollout nothing changes. "Consequently no resampling scheme that discards zero-gradient groups can repair the collapse: only changing the score or the advantage estimator can." — Proposition 7, §3
>
> The repair and its weighting condition: score every episode with r_α = α·1{correct} + 1 − (Y − c)², so ∂E[r]/∂c = 2(q − c) and ∂E[r]/∂q = α − 1 + 2c. Pure Brier (α = 0) makes the calibrated accuracy gradient 2q − 1 < 0 for every q < 1/2 — "the model gains score by degrading accuracy on hard prompts and reporting the degradation honestly" — while any α > 1 makes it strictly positive everywhere. Remark 1 gives the general design test: "The operative variable is whether the training signal contains a reachable region of identically zero gradient", not properness — clipping the strictly proper Brier signal below t* reinstates the dead zone. — Propositions 8–9, Remark 1, §4
>
> Eval setup: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (three seeds) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (single seeds) on short-form QA from TriviaQA and PopQA, graded programmatically, in two tiers. Tier 1 is theorem-grade and head-only — a four-parameter gate head on a frozen answer/abstain logit difference with rewards from pre-graded answer banks, sweeping the estimator menu across G ∈ {64, 256} and λ ∈ {1, 4, 9}. Tier 2 is full-parameter RLOO fine-tuning where the abstain action is a binary first-token gate in the model's own vocabulary, on an 80% low-accuracy (q̂ ∈ [0.15, 0.35]) / 20% high-accuracy (q̂ ≥ 0.90) mixture chosen to satisfy B1. All runs single-node, single-GPU. — §5, App. L
>
> Headline collapse numbers: on a held-out 500-prompt high-accuracy cohort the median answer probability falls from 1.000 to ≤ 0.008 within ten optimizer steps and to ≤ 2e-5 by step 8000 in three of three seeds, while forced-answer correctness on those same prompts holds at 0.94–0.97 throughout (256 rollouts per prompt). At the collapsed terminal state the task-gradient norm is 0 and the gate-KL-anchor gradient norm is below 2e-10, against a task-gradient norm of 108 in the paired attribution control at the same step — "the reward gradient and the restoring force die together, and only where the collapse happened." — §5, App. L.3, Fig. 4a
>
> Headline repair numbers: trained on the same mixture with the same estimator (α = 2, three seeds), the 1.5B report channel moves coverage 0.47 → 0.78–0.81, accuracy 0.43 → 0.69, and Brier 0.27 → 0.22 jointly; the 7B report channel ends at coverage 0.976, accuracy 0.942, Brier 0.052. In simulation at λ = 4.5 the action-level mechanism deploys nothing at any signal quality (utility and coverage 0.000 at weak, medium and strong signal) while the report-level mechanism captures 97% of the strong-signal optimum's utility (0.194 against 0.199). — §5, App. L.3, Fig. 4b, Table 2
>
> Stated limitations: "Both live backbones are one model family (Qwen2.5) on short-form QA, with training mixtures constructed to satisfy (B1)." Free-form refusals with no designated decision position remain untested; production estimator features (sampled-token variance, ratio clipping, the ε floor in the normalizer, length normalization) sit outside the analysis; the report is read out through a linear probe, so the verbalized-confidence channel is untested; and "with an LLM judge crediting hedged text at rate ν, properness degrades by O(ν): our claims are scoped to programmatic grading." At 1.5B the paper concedes "the binding constraint is the readout, not the mechanism." — §7

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* Propositions 6–7 and Table 1 first, as a direct audit of my own group-normalized agent RL. Below an answer rate of about 1/G the normalizer replaces every designed penalty with λ_eff = 1 and moves the effective threshold to 1/2 — so whether a magnitude I put on a rare-event reward term survives is set by where that event's rate sits relative to the knee p ~ 1/G, not by the weight I pick. Proposition 7 is the part I would not have guessed: discarding zero-variance groups conserves the pathology *exactly* per rollout, so dynamic sampling buys nothing but rollouts — and the paper's own remedy is a change of score or estimator, explicitly not a larger G. Remark 1 in §4 is portable well beyond abstention: if a tool-call-parsed gate, a search-hit gate, or an evidence gate multiplies the whole reward and can saturate, Lemma 1 says my reward gradient and my KL anchor go to zero on the same coordinate at the same time. Read the §5 Tier-2 protocol for the harness rather than the numbers — forced-answer rollouts at 256 per prompt with a one-sided 95% Clopper–Pearson lower bound above t* to certify per-prompt capability, plus an attribution control that zeroes only one tier's reward-channel advantages while holding the KL channel and paired randomness fixed. That is the cleanest separation of "lost capability" from "lost willingness" I have seen written down. And read the §7 judge limitation before trusting any calibration-style reward built on a judged pipeline: a judge that gives partial credit to a hedge is exactly what breaks the strictly proper score the repair depends on.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — my open question is state-conditioned stop/pivot RL, and this is the sharpest warning I have found about the obvious implementation. If "answer now vs. keep searching" is trained as a discrete action behind a learned gate with a KL anchor, Lemma 1 says reward gradient and anchor die together, and Theorem 1 says the training-reward curve rises toward zero like 1/t while the behavior degenerates — a curve that reads as improvement. The repair maps directly onto my setting: train a continuous "do I have the answer" confidence readout with a proper score plus a correctness reward, and threshold it at deployment, rather than making stop a rewarded action; Chow's t* = λ/(1+λ) then gives a principled stop threshold from the cost of a wrong answer relative to more search. The direction is mirrored — my measured failure is over-answering and over-searching, theirs is universal refusal — so the transferable object is the mechanism and the repair, not the sign. **AgentPlanet** — Propositions 6–7 are a reward-channel-integrity result of exactly the kind that note asks for: the advantage estimator, not the designed rule, sets the effective penalty and the effective threshold, so a reward I author in the world-authoring role can be silently rewritten downstream by the normalizer, and their five falsifiable estimator-indexed predictions are a ready-made integrity check to run on logs. Proposition 9(b) sharpens the anti-Goodhart framing too: under pure Brier the model gains score by degrading accuracy and honestly reporting it, and the paper notes this is *not* a proxy gap because the score is the deployment utility — a self-defeating objective rather than a hacked proxy. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — Proposition 7 gives the per-rollout accounting my note is about: a filter that discards degenerate groups spends ~1/(G·p) times more rollouts per step while conserving the drift per rollout exactly, so an exponential-looking per-step improvement is pure rollout dilution. Table 1's separate L(t) and L(N) columns say the honest axis for any rollout-economy claim is per rollout, not per optimizer step. Partial link: the paper discards zero-gradient rollouts, it does not replay them. **Post-cutoff distillation** — moderate stretch, precise on the gating question: Remark 1 says clipping a strictly proper score below a threshold reinstates the dead zone even though the rule stays proper, and a reward zeroed wherever the student "could already have known" the fact creates precisely such a reachable zero-gradient region — with Corollary 1 adding that the forward-KL anchor I would rely on has vanishing gradient on the same boundary.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the capability certificate plus attribution control as a standard ablation pair — certify per-prompt capability with forced-answer rollouts under a one-sided 95% Clopper–Pearson bound, then run a paired arm that zeroes only the suspect subpopulation's reward-channel advantages while keeping the KL channel and seeds identical, and report both arms' diagnostic-batch gradient norms at the same step (here 0 and sub-2e-10 against 108). That turns "the model stopped doing X" into a mechanism claim, and in a stop/pivot ablation "it can no longer find the answer" and "it no longer tries" are otherwise indistinguishable. The open question that matters most for a production stack is one they explicitly exclude: ratio clipping, the ε floor in the normalizer, length normalization and sampled-token variance are all outside the analysis, as are free-form refusals with no designated decision position — which is exactly what a real search agent's "give up" looks like.

[Source (arXiv 2608.00301)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00301)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>DAPD: Dual-Anchored Policy Distillation</strong> · Shanghai AI Laboratory (+ SJTU / USTC), August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.01735, v1 3 Aug 2026, v2 13 Aug 2026; cs.AI, 15 pages) on on-policy
self-distillation (OPSD), where the same model acts as its own teacher after being conditioned on a
privileged reference solution the student will not have at inference. The paper names the resulting
failure "privilege illusion" — the student asserts unsupported answers as if the reference were still
in context — diagnoses its root cause as information asymmetry between teacher and student, and
proposes DAPD, which adds a self-conditioned distribution as a trainable bridge and applies the
alignment paths in both reference-to-rollout and rollout-to-reference directions. Experiments cover
Qwen3 at 1.7B/4B/8B/14B/32B on six reasoning, coding and instruction-following benchmarks, with a
headline of +2.00 average points over OPSD on Qwen3-4B and gains that persist at scale (+2.78 at 32B)
where OPSD's gains collapse.*

**From the report**

> Core method: OPSD supervises the reference-free student with a reference-conditioned teacher, L_OPSD = E[(1/|y|) Σ_t D(sg[p_Cross] ‖ p_None)]. DAPD adds a third, trainable Self distribution p_Self = p_θ(· | x, s_<t, s) that conditions the student on the full completion being predicted, giving three directed objectives — Entangled Distillation D(sg[p_Cross] ‖ p_None), Inference Anchor D(sg[p_None] ‖ p_Self), and Privileged Anchor D(sg[p_Cross] ‖ p_Self). — Eqs. 1–4, §2.1, §3.1
>
> The two anchoring levels: Dual-Path Anchoring composes an unconditioned path with a privileged path; Dual-Source Anchoring then applies that in both completion directions, L_DAPD = λ·L_DPA^{y→y*} + (1−λ)·L_DPA^{y*→y}, with the reference giving reliable off-policy guidance and the rollout giving student-reachable on-policy guidance. — Eqs. 5–7, §3.2–3.3
>
> Diagnostic that motivates the method: across five Qwen3 scales, wrong claims under OPSD rise from 13.0 to 37.0 per 10,000 generations while mean Avg@12 falls from 59.56 to 53.24; swapping only the student side to the information-matched Self distribution cuts late-stage wrong claims by 45% and improves mean Avg@12 by +6.22 points. — §2.1–2.2, Fig. 2
>
> Training recipe: Qwen3 1.7B–32B trained on OpenThoughts (math for Reasoning, code for Coding, math+code+science for Instruct, benchmark eval examples excluded) with LoRA rank 64 / scale 128, lr 5e-6, linear 500-step schedule without warmup, grad-norm clip 0.1, effective batch size 32 on eight A100-80GB; one rollout per problem at temperature 1.1, top-p 0.95, top-k 20, max 1,024 new tokens; divergence is a component-clipped full-vocabulary forward KL, Σ_v min{q_v(log q_v − log p_v), c} with c = 0.05. — §4.1, App. A.2/A.4/A.6, Eq. 8
>
> Headline 1 (Qwen3-4B, six benchmarks): DAPD 57.34 average vs OPSD 55.34 (+2.00), Base 54.46, Purified OPSD 56.25, SDFT 54.17, SDPO 53.19, DOPD 53.49; per-benchmark deltas over OPSD are AIME24 +0.55, AIME25 +4.44, HMMT25 +3.06, LCB v5 +1.05, BFCL v3 +0.59, IFBench +2.33. — Table 1, §4.2
>
> Headline 2 (scale): OPSD's gain over Base collapses from +5.19 at 1.7B to +1.39 at 4B and at most +0.28 from 8B through 32B, while DAPD beats the scale-matched OPSD policy by +1.94, +2.69, +2.41, +2.04 and +2.78 reasoning Avg@12 points at 1.7B/4B/8B/14B/32B. — Fig. 1b, Table 6, App. C.1
>
> Headline 3 (behavior): at training steps 250–300 DAPD produces 9 detected wrong claims vs 33 for OPSD, a 73% reduction, and its step-300 Avg@12 is 7.66 points higher; pooled over all checkpoints OPSD produces 58 detected claims in 32,400 generations. The probe counts only generations that first state the derivation has failed and then attribute a concrete answer to memory or an external source while returning an incorrect final answer — generic uncertainty, ordinary guesses, recalled formulas and phrases like "after research" are excluded. — §4.2, App. C.2
>
> Eval setup: AIME24, AIME25 and HMMT Feb 2025 scored Avg@12 from 12 thinking-enabled samples per problem at temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95, up to 38,912 new tokens with verified final answers; LCB v5 on its official code-generation split at Pass@1, BFCL v3 on the official multi-turn function-call harness, IFBench on the released verifier-based score. The wrong-claims probe uses 90 problems × 12 generations per scale-checkpoint cell with a **problem-cluster bootstrap that keeps all 12 generations of a problem together**. Ablation on divergence choice (Qwen3-4B): forward KL with component cap 0.05 = 65.28, forward KL uncapped = 62.50, reverse KL capped = 62.50, reverse KL uncapped = 63.33 — clipping matters more than divergence direction. — App. A.1, C.2, Table 8
>
> Stated limitation: "DAPD constructs multiple anchored distributions during training, increasing training-time computation but adding no inference-time cost. Its weights may need recalibration across scales or architectures. The verified-rollout extension requires an automatic correctness signal, which may be unavailable for open-ended tasks." — §6

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the whole mechanism is one extra distribution, so read §3.1's Eqs. 2–4 next to Eq. 5 — the claim that the Inference Anchor plus Entangled Distillation implicitly pulls the two reference-free distributions together only holds because p_Self^{y*} and p_Cross^{y} are the same parameters under the same privileged condition, and App. B.1's Proposition 1 tells me exactly what has to be small (the bridge TV ε) before that argument is load-bearing rather than decorative. App. A.4/A.7 is the reproduction-critical part I would not have guessed: the Entangled teachers come from a periodically-refreshed LoRA-on snapshot but the two anchor teachers come from the detached LoRA-off base policy, so the anchors pull toward the pre-run policy rather than a moving target — an asymmetry in teacher provenance I would have to decide for myself before porting any of this. §4.5's reference-free variants matter when there is no curated solution: two independent rollouts already recover +2.41 at 4B and 8B over OPSD, and the verifier is used only to choose which of four candidates becomes the guidance source rather than as a reward term — a cheap way to spend a verifier, and I want to know whether it survives when correctness is *graded* rather than checked. Finally §4.4: the best swept reference weight λ drops from 0.5 at 1.7B to 0.2 at 4B, 8B and 14B (the 32B run adopts λ = 0.2 as configuration without a sweep), which the paper frames as a reliability-reachability trade-off — a prior for the mixing weight at my own model size rather than a blind sweep.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Post-cutoff distillation** — this is the direct hit. That note's setup *is* OPSD with post-cutoff facts as the privileged context, and DAPD names the failure it invites: the student copies behavior that depends on context it will never see and then asserts facts it cannot support, measured as wrong claims rising 13.0 → 37.0 per 10,000 generations. More usefully, it argues my open gating question is being attacked at the wrong layer — Purified OPSD (a pointwise-mutual-information correction) and DOPD (advantage-gap routing) both *filter or route* the privileged signal and land 1.09 and 3.85 points below simply matching what information the two sides condition on. The Self distribution is a structural gate rather than a reward-side one. **Over-reflection in search agents** — the wrong-claims probe is the same instrument class as my keyed-trajectory counting: name one specific pathological pattern, count it per fixed denominator, and plot it against the capability score across training steps so the two can be shown coupled (37.0 per 10k coinciding with Avg@12 falling 59.56 → 53.24). The failure modes differ — confabulate-after-failure vs confirm-then-keep-searching — so the link is methodological, not a shared mechanism. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — Dual-Source Anchoring's reference-to-rollout direction promotes the on-policy rollout from prediction target to guidance source, and the dual-rollout variant extracts usable supervision from a second sample with no curated reference at all. That is squeezing more gradient out of samples already paid for, the same economics as my note — but DAPD stays strictly on-policy with a fresh rollout each minibatch and no replay buffer, so the off-policy reuse question stays open. **AgentPlanet** — stretch, but real on the reward-channel-integrity axis: a supervision channel can carry information the policy will not have at deployment, and the leak surfaces as a *named behavioral pathology* rather than only as a score drop. The verified-rollout variant is the cleaner transferable pattern — a correctness verifier selects which rollout becomes the guidance source and is never exposed to the policy, keeping the verifier out of the channel it would otherwise corrupt.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the behavioral-probe protocol, verbatim — a tightly-scoped failure definition, an explicit exclusion list, counts normalized per 10,000 generations, 90 problems × 12 generations per cell, and a problem-cluster bootstrap that keeps a problem's 12 generations together. That last piece is the correct CI for repeated-sampling evals and I would port it straight into how I decide whether an eval delta is real. The open question this leaves is the one I care most about: every experiment injects privileged information as a *worked textual solution*, and App. E only lists retrieved documents, tool traces, verifier feedback and intermediate plans as future work. Whether the Self-distribution anchor still works when the privileged context is a retrieved page or a tool return — where the student could in principle have fetched it at inference, so the asymmetry is partial rather than total — is untested, and that partial-asymmetry case is exactly the one a tool-using search agent lives in.

[Source (arXiv 2608.01735)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01735) · [code](https://github.com/uanu2002/DAPD)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Quo Vadis, World Modeling? Towards Interactive World Proxies for Continually Improving Agents</strong> · Shanghai AI Lab + Zhejiang U. + NUS, August 2026</summary>

*A 38-page position-and-survey article (arXiv 2608.02713v1, submitted 3 Aug 2026; cs.CV, 216
references, 6 tables, 10 figures, 20 authors, **no experiments**) arguing that world modeling for
agents should be reframed from predicting physical state transitions to predicting agent-usable
information transitions. It defines an "Agent-Centric World Proxy" as an environment-grounded
mechanism ŝ_{ℓ+1} = WP(s_ℓ, u_ℓ^F) indexed by interaction step rather than physical time, and
organizes the design space as six proxy functions crossed with three empowerment levels. Its headline
claim is that a world model should be judged by how much better it makes the agent that queries it,
not by fidelity or realism in isolation.*

**From the report**

> Core definition: "An Agent-Centric World Proxy is an environment-grounded proxy that models or predicts information transitions conditioned on agent-initiated interactions, aiming to provide information gain for agent improvement", formalized as ŝ_{ℓ+1} = WP(s_ℓ, u_ℓ^F) — generalizing the classical world model ŝ_{t+1} = WM(s_t, a_t). — §2.1, §2.3
>
> The generalization runs along exactly three axes: physical time step t→t+1 becomes interaction step ℓ→ℓ+1 ("agent interaction does not always advance physical time"); state transition becomes information transition; and the externally given condition becomes an agent-initiated interaction u_ℓ^F. — §2.2, Table 2
>
> Six functional forms, each named by the input it consumes and the feedback it returns: dynamics (state/history + action → future state, rollout, predicted reward), spatial (scene + viewpoint query → rendered view), execution (code/command/click/API/tool call → post-execution state + stdout/stderr), memory/experience (task context + retrieval query → retrieved experience, failure cases, constraints), skill (goal + skill query → skill suggestion, action prior), reward/verification (plan/trajectory/answer/action → reward, critique, preference, verification). — §4.1–4.7, Table 5
>
> The three levels are given as distinct update rules, not slogans: L1 leaves parameters untouched and only concatenates context, s_ℓ^{agent+} = s_ℓ^{agent} ⊕ ŝ_{ℓ+1}^{guide}; L2 turns proxy output into a gradient, agent⁺ = Train(agent, ŝ_{ℓ+1}^{opt}); L3 updates both sides against real-environment evidence, (agent⁺, WP⁺) = CoEvolve(agent, WP, ŝ_{ℓ+1}^{proxy}, s_{ℓ+1}^{env}). — §3.1–3.3
>
> The nearest thing to a training recipe is L2's four implementation modes: Proxy-as-Reward (verifier scores trajectories to form rewards), Proxy-as-Critic (failure causes, error diagnoses, constraint violations), Proxy-as-Simulator ("generates synthetic trajectories or constructs preference pairs for DPO / RLHF / GRPO"), and Proxy-Guided Curriculum ("generates harder or more targeted tasks based on the Agent's current failure modes"); the signals "can be converted into objectives such as SFT, DPO, PPO, or GRPO". — §3.2
>
> The Functions × Levels matrix is an 18-cell map with representative systems per cell and two cells the authors mark as unfilled: Spatial × L3 labeled "underexplored" and Reward/Verification × L3 "emerging" — "The blank corners, spatial and reward proxies at the co-evolution level, are not accidents but invitations." — §4.8, Table 6
>
> Eval critique levelled at the field: "Current benchmarks score realism, fidelity, controllability, or human-aligned quality, but still grade the proxy in isolation. We need agent-centric benchmarks: did the feedback help the agent plan, learn, or improve?" Table 3 separately lists five criteria for an effective proxy, starting with groundedness — "relies on real environment data, rules, trajectories, or interaction evidence, not detached generation". — §5 Open Challenge 4, Table 3
>
> Stated limitations: errors compound over long rollouts and "calibrated uncertainty, not just sharper pixels, is the missing ingredient"; an agent "must decide, online, whether to act on proxy feedback or return to the real environment… treating the proxy as an oracle invites silent failure"; and "when the proxy becomes the reward or verifier (L2), the agent is incentivized to exploit blind spots" — the same sandbox "that makes risky exploration safe also opens a new attack surface". §2.4 adds that "confidently wrong" proxies "can be worse than none". — §5 Open Challenges 1–3, §2.4

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the Execution row of Table 6 (§4.8), read together with the worked example in §3.3, because that is the only line that lands entirely in my setting — Table 6's own cells are terse ("simulate actions before acting" / "synthesize experience for RL" / "self-improve via a coevolving model") while §3.3 climbs one web agent up the same three rungs with the same execution proxy: imagine the resulting page before clicking Purchase, then score and replay those imagined rollouts into synthetic trajectories and preference pairs, then let real deployment trajectories retrain the proxy. It is the cleanest statement I have seen of the three distinct things a simulated tool environment can be used for. §3.2's four L2 modes read as a checklist for what a proxy can hand a training loop, and Proxy-Guided Curriculum is the one I would actually wire: it makes task difficulty a function of the current policy's failure distribution rather than a fixed synthesis config. Table 3's five criteria are worth reading as an acceptance gate rather than prose. And §5 Open Challenge 2 is the sharpest paragraph for me — deciding online whether to act on proxy feedback or go back to the real environment is a state-conditioned stop/pivot/act decision, and the paper concedes agents rarely make that judgment well without proposing any mechanism for learning it.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — their WP(s_ℓ, u_ℓ^F) is my world-model term widened from state transitions to information transitions, and L3's CoEvolve is exactly co-training the policy term and the world term against real evidence. But the factorization has no world-authoring term at all — no initial state, no rules — so the planet role is precisely the piece this taxonomy leaves out, which is useful to know as a gap rather than a competitor. Their Open Challenge 3 ("the agent is incentivized to exploit blind spots" once the proxy becomes the reward) is my reward-channel-integrity concern stated from the other side, and Table 3's five criteria are a ready-made candidate anti-Goodhart gate list. **Over-reflection in search agents** — Open Challenge 2 is the same class of state-conditioned decision as when to stop, pivot, or answer, and the L1 execution proxy (imagine the resulting page before clicking) is the cheap-probe form of confirm-before-continuing; a stretch, in that the paper never studies redundant continuation and reports no rate for it. **Post-cutoff distillation** — L3's "Proxy → Agent Internalization" makes distillation the explicit transfer channel, "turning external feedback into internal capability", which is the same shape as moving cached post-cutoff facts from an external store into the student's weights; the paper stops at the slogan and says nothing about token-level alignment or gating what gets distilled. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — L2's Proxy-as-Simulator argues from the same economics I do, the agent's own rollouts becoming training fuel at a scale collecting real interactions could never reach, except their reuse mechanism is a learned proxy standing in for the environment rather than a replay buffer over real transitions — the model-based cousin of off-policy reuse, inheriting the compounding-error caveat of their Open Challenge 1.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port Table 3 verbatim into my environment-synthesis accept gate as five boolean checks (grounded / controllable / feedback-useful / cost-scalable / forward-looking) — "not detached generation" is exactly the property my template-depth failures violate, and it is cheap to test per environment. The open question they leave is the one I would want to answer with a number: they call for benchmarks that measure information gain — did the proxy's feedback make the agent plan or learn better — but propose no metric. The obvious operationalization is a paired delta on identical items with the proxy channel ablated, which is a measurement I could run and they did not.

[Source (arXiv 2608.02713)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02713) · [project page](https://worldbench.github.io/awesome-agentic-world-model)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Model Card: Grok 4.6</strong> · SpaceXAI, August 2026</summary>

*A 36-page model card (cover date August 12, 2026; revision stamp 2026-08-12) for Grok 4.6, described
as the latest release in SpaceXAI's "1.5T-scale model family" and developed in collaboration with
Cursor, which supplied anonymized workflow data used in supplemental training. SpaceXAI is stated in
the card as a doing-business-as name of xAI LLC. It is a text-and-image-in, text-out model with a
January 2026 pretraining cutoff, evaluated across 38 numbered evaluation subsections (40+ named
suites) spanning agentic coding, engineering/CAD, office knowledge work, internal AI-R&amp;D
acceleration, search factuality, cyber and bio/chem dual-use, jailbreak robustness, mental health and
behavioral propensities. The headline claim is that it completes longer autonomous tasks "with fewer
steps and fewer output tokens than other frontier models", while scoring below the dual-use capability
thresholds defined in xAI's Frontier Artificial Intelligence Framework.*

**From the report**

> Training recipe, disclosed at one paragraph of detail: "Supplemental training ran longer than for Grok 4.5, combining model-generated data curated for reasoning and advanced technical concepts, high-quality engineering corpora, and an improved optimizer and recipe. Grok 4.5 regenerated the SFT trajectories across reasoning efforts, agent harnesses, and domains spanning STEM, software engineering, and knowledge work, with model-based checks screening out problematic traces." Post-training is "supervised finetuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) on human and synthetic reward signals", and "Agentic RL covered knowledge work, general coding, and purpose-built environments for kernel optimization, web development, and computer-aided design"; pretraining cutoff is January 2026. — §1.2
>
> Autonomous ML acceleration: an earlier Grok 4.6 checkpoint was tasked with speeding up its own chat inference and was "free to experiment but required to verify end-to-end gains before opening a pull request. In five hours it worked through 297 candidate optimizations across fused MoE, FMHA, kernel scheduling, and communication, discarded those without measurable end-to-end gain (including several that passed microbenchmarks), and opened seven pull requests; three now serve Grok Chat production traffic, for a combined 1.5% decode and 3.1% prefill throughput gain." — §5
>
> Verifier design that refuses partial credit: InferenceEval "combines a hidden GPU unit score (exact tensors, state, and CUDA-graph behavior) with a hidden integration probe that must pass or the unit score is zeroed, plus a check that the patch implements the stated production contract"; Grok 4.6 (high) 46.9% vs GPT-5.6 Sol 44.1%, Opus 5 43.0%, Grok 4.5 41.3%. — §5.2
>
> Internal AI-R&amp;D eval and its scoring rules: the SpaceXAI MTS eval covers "diagnosing reward hacking in training runs, generating and auditing training data, debugging large-scale training infrastructure, and creating evaluations for emerging capabilities", with agents "scored on task reward under a fixed rollout budget (time and tokens)"; Grok 4.6 61.1% vs Grok 4.5 57.1%, Opus 4.8 55.8%, Opus 5 52.6%, GPT-5.6 Sol 52.1%, Grok 4.3 40.6%. The section notes it "covers the standard (non-GPU) suite", implying a GPU suite that is not reported. — §5.1
>
> Search numbers move in opposite directions: DeepSearchQA 81.6% (Opus 4.8 84.8%, GPT-5.6 Sol 75.0%, GPT-5.5 63.7%, Grok 4.5 38.4%), run "on an internal implementation of DeepSearchQA" — while the Factuality hallucination rate (lower is better) goes from Grok 4.5's 0.98% to Grok 4.6's 1.7%. — §6.1, §6.2
>
> Long-horizon agentic work is the weakest reported area: SWE-Marathon v1.1 resolution rate 31.9% vs Opus 5 50.0%, Opus 4.8 48.8%, Kimi K3 48.1%, Fable 5 45.0%, GPT-5.6 Sol 42.5% (the card notes "scoring uses multi-layer verification designed to resist reward hacking and shortcuts"); Terminal-Bench 3.0 task success 26.0% vs Opus 5 43.5%. — §2.5, §2.6
>
> Eval-setup provenance and effort matching: many headline benchmarks are third-party-run (Mercor for APEX-SWE, Datacurve for DeepSWE on the mini-swe-agent harness, Artificial Analysis for the AA GDPVal and AA Briefcase Elo scores, Vals AI for the Harvey Legal Agent Benchmark), while §5's R&amp;D suites, HackerBench, the factuality/sycophancy/self-harm suites and the bio/chem evals are internal and unreleased; peer numbers "use the best result available from model cards or evaluator-reported sources", with §7.1's CyberGym peers taken from four cited competitor system cards. Reasoning-effort tags are not matched across models — Grok 4.6 is tagged (high) 37 times across the document and (xhigh) exactly once, at EEBench (60.0% xhigh vs 53.0% high), the card's only same-model effort ablation. — §2.2–2.4, §3.5, §4.1–4.2, §4.9, §5.1, §7.1
>
> Safeguards and the caveat the card states about itself: §10 discloses a layered defense — safety SFT, RLHF, verifiable rewards and model-based grading, plus runtime input and topical filters for CSAM, self-harm and bio/chem pathways — and asserts "On these suites, Grok 4.6 generally refuses more reliably than Grok 4.5"; §9 hedges that "We treat residual failures as cases for ongoing monitoring and patching as new techniques emerge." §1.1 states plainly: "Grok 4.6 is not intended for autonomous high-stakes decision-making in domains such as medicine, law, finance, or safety-critical systems without appropriate human oversight and domain-expert validation." Read off the card's own tables, three propensity metrics nonetheless land worse than Grok 4.5 — HackerBench harmful/dual-use compliance 16.7% vs 7.8%, MASK-Rectified dishonesty 3.8% vs 0.67%, self-harm compliance 3.7% vs 0.5% (a composite the card defines as also failing a model that "refuses without redirecting the user to help") — a tension the card does not comment on. — §1.1, §7.4, §9, §10, §11.1, §12.1

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §5.2's zeroing rule is the piece I actually want — a hidden integration probe that, if it fails, zeroes the unit score outright rather than docking it. That is a structural answer to the failure mode where a trajectory banks partial rubric credit for a patch that passes unit checks and breaks the system, and it is cheaper to implement in a verifier than a full outcome re-grade. §5's discard rule is the reward-channel lesson stated as an anecdote: the checkpoint threw away optimizations that passed microbenchmarks but showed no measurable end-to-end gain — which is exactly the proxy-vs-outcome gap I keep hitting when a step-level verifier rewards a tool call that merely looks right. The card does say the end-to-end requirement was imposed by the task spec; what it does not say is whether that preference was also trained in, how the requirement was enforced, or how the agent measured end-to-end gain — and those are the details that would make it reproducible. §6.1 next to §6.2 is the pairing worth staring at: DeepSearchQA went 38.4% → 81.6% across one model generation while the single-response hallucination rate went 0.98% → 1.7%, a directly comparable data point for the accuracy-vs-grounding tradeoff I measure in my own agents. And §1.2's "model-based checks screening out problematic traces" plus regeneration of SFT trajectories "across reasoning efforts, agent harnesses, and domains" is the same audit-then-filter stage I run before SFT, disclosed at one sentence — useful as external confirmation that trajectory-level screening is standard practice, useless as a recipe, and a fair marker of how little of it anyone publishes.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — §1.1 claims results "with fewer steps and fewer output tokens than other frontier models" and §2.1 reports CursorBench 3.2 only as score-vs-average-output-tokens and score-vs-cost Pareto curves, making token spend per task a first-class reported axis rather than a footnote. That is the same efficiency framing my 63% confirm-then-keep-searching finding argues for — but the card reports the metric without any stop/pivot mechanism, so the link is to the measurement, not the method. **AgentPlanet** — §1.2 discloses purpose-built environments for kernel optimization, web development and CAD as agentic-RL supply, i.e. world authoring treated as a training input, while §5.1 lists "diagnosing reward hacking in training runs" as a *scored capability* and §5.2 specifies a hidden probe that zeroes the score on failure. That pairing of authored environments with an anti-Goodhart gate is the closest public instance I have of the reward-channel-integrity requirement I want a world builder to satisfy. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — §5.1 scores agents "under a fixed rollout budget (time and tokens)" and §2.5's SWE-Marathon trajectories run to millions of tokens and multiple hours, so the card prices long-horizon rollouts explicitly enough to make the waste visible; a stretch, because it says nothing about replay, off-policy reuse, or update-to-data ratio. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a January 2026 pretraining cutoff on an August 2026 release is a roughly seven-month staleness window, and the card addresses recency only through search-grounded answering with no weight-level knowledge update disclosed. That is the retrieval branch of the problem my note attacks with distillation, so the connection is to the problem statement rather than to a shared mechanism.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* port the §5.2 gate shape into my agentic reward — a hidden integration check that *zeroes* an otherwise-earned unit score rather than subtracting from it, so no trajectory can accumulate partial credit on a change that breaks the surrounding system. It is a one-line change to a verifier and removes a whole class of partial-credit hacking. What to watch: the card documents no autonomy or self-directed-capability *risk* evaluation — no scheming, sabotage, self-exfiltration, self-replication or oversight-subversion suite anywhere, and the Framework threshold framing is confined to the bio/chem section — yet §5 opens the R&amp;D chapter by reporting an earlier checkpoint autonomously optimizing its own production inference stack and shipping three PRs to production traffic. Autonomous self-improvement is demonstrated and celebrated here, and never evaluated as a risk.

[Source (PDF)](https://media.x.ai/v1/website/card-4p6-4cd2dc57.pdf)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Output-Aware Rotation for INT2 KV-Cache Quantization</strong> · USC + Seoul National University + Inha, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.02691, v1 3 Aug 2026, v2 5 Aug 2026; cs.LG, CC0) proposing OptR, an
output-aware rotation method for 2-bit KV-cache quantization: instead of picking the rotation that
best reconstructs cached keys and values, it learns per-head orthogonal corrections that minimize the
attention-output error measured **after** the output projection W_O, and prepends an
attention-equivalent key centering step that shrinks channel-wise offsets without changing the softmax
distribution. It is calibration-only — model weights stay frozen — and is evaluated on three reasoning
models across five reasoning/coding benchmarks plus RULER-NIAH up to 128K tokens. The headline claim
is that at an effective 2.32 bits per KV element it lifts both the QuaRot and OSCAR INT2 baselines
substantially toward BF16 accuracy while preserving the paged KV-cache format and staying within 2% of
the base pipeline's decode latency, throughput and prefill time.*

**From the report**

> Core method: the post-W_O attention-output error is decomposed exactly into a key-induced term (Δp carried through V and W_O) and a value-induced term (quantized p̂ carried through ΔV and W_O); OptR then learns a per-head orthogonal correction R = R⁰·exp(A − Aᵀ) on top of any base rotation, optimizing keys first because the INT2 keys determine the attention distribution under which values are aggregated. — §3.2, §4.2, §4.5, Eqs. 8 and 11
>
> Attention-equivalent key reparameterization: keys are centered by a per-channel calibration mean μ before rotation and quantization, which "adds a query-dependent constant to all logits and leaves the softmax distribution unchanged"; it is applied only to keys, "since shifting values would alter the attention output", and it works because large channel-wise key offsets inflate the dynamic range of group-wise INT2 with only four levels. — §4.1, App. "Equivalent Inference Implementation" (Eq. 24)
>
> Recipe is one-time offline calibration, not training: "All model weights remain frozen; only the per-head rotation generators are calibrated" — 80 Adam steps at lr 0.02 per layer and per KV head, with a straight-through estimator for the non-differentiable INT2 rounding. Rotation optimization takes 6.25 / 7.05 / 11.03 minutes on a single A100 for Qwen3-4B / Qwen3-8B / Phi4-14B, a figure the table explicitly states *excludes* BF16 trace collection — which requires dumping QKV traces from all 198 GPQA-Diamond prompts plus 28 full generated traces for the centering statistics. — §5 Implementation Details, App. Table D
>
> Headline number: on Qwen3-8B AIME25, OptR raises QuaRot-INT2 from 17.33% to 66.67% and OSCAR from 54.67% to 66.00%, against 68.00% for BF16. — §1, Fig. 1, Table 1
>
> Five-benchmark means at the same 2.32 bits per KV element (AIME24, AIME25, GPQA-Diamond, MBPP+, LiveCodeBench v6): Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507 2.61 → 61.63 (QuaRot) and 62.71 → 65.38 (OSCAR) vs 67.58 BF16; Qwen3-8B 25.96 → 64.13 and 61.34 → 65.68 vs 65.66 BF16; Phi4-14B-reasoning-plus 52.98 → 55.26 and 52.63 → 55.60 vs 58.57 BF16 (μ ± σ over five seeds). — Table 1
>
> Objective ablation isolating *where* the error is measured — same OSCAR base rotation, same KL term, only the error term changed: cache reconstruction gives 65.33 AIME25, pre-W_O attention readout gives 62.67, and post-W_O attention output gives 70.67 on Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507. — Table 4
>
> Serving overhead is quantified: OptR "remains within 2% of the corresponding base pipeline in decode latency, end-to-end throughput, and prefill time across the evaluated settings, while retaining the same maximum batch sizes", and at batch 128 on a 2K-in/4K-out workload it "increases GPU memory by only 18 MiB, from 36,205 MiB to 36,223 MiB"; the paged format survives because centering folds into the Triton cache-write kernel as a precomputed rotated mean and the value rotations are absorbed into W_V and W_O. Harness: single A100-SXM4 40GB, TP=1, 8-token page size, decode latency over 1,024 generated tokens excluding prefill, five timed runs after a discarded warm-up, custom SGLang with PyTorch 2.9.1 / CUDA 12.8 / Triton 3.5.1. — §6 Efficiency Analysis
>
> Stated caveats: (a) calibration is drawn from the eval pool — "The calibration and held-out chunks do not overlap, although their source prompts come from the GPQA-Diamond evaluation pool", one of the five reported benchmarks; (b) even with OptR, long-context retrieval stays far below BF16 — RULER-NIAH on Qwen3-8B at 128K is 41.87 (QuaRot+OptR) and 40.43 (OSCAR+OptR) against 80.89 BF16; (c) the best key-loss weight is base-rotation dependent (λ_K = 1 best for OSCAR, λ_K = 10 best for QuaRot) but λ_K = 1 is used throughout, so the QuaRot rows are reported off their own optimum. — App. "Calibration details", App. Table E, App. B.2

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* Table 4 is the whole paper for me — same base rotation, same KL term, only the placement of the error term changes, and AIME25 moves 65.33 (reconstruction) → 62.67 (pre-W_O) → 70.67 (post-W_O). That is a clean, ablated demonstration that a surrogate objective measured where the error is *introduced* can be worse than useless compared to one measured where the downstream model actually *reads* it — the same failure shape I worry about when a verifier or reward scores an intermediate artifact instead of the readout. §4.1 with the appendix's equivalent-inference implementation is the part I would actually build: key centering is three lines of algebra, it applies only to keys, and at serving time it collapses into one precomputed μ·R_K subtracted inside the existing Triton cache-write kernel — zero format change, so it does not fight paged attention or prefix caching. Table 2 with appendix Table E is where I would set my own go/no-go for running an agent on an INT2 cache: reasoning-benchmark means look nearly BF16-clean (Qwen3-8B 65.68 vs 65.66), but RULER-NIAH at 128K is 41.87 vs 80.89 — the capability that degrades first is exactly reading back a long history. And the calibration-details block is where I would push back before trusting the GPQA column: the rotations are calibrated on QKV traces from the GPQA-Diamond evaluation prompts themselves, and the paper says so plainly. Note the paper's own token budget does not reconcile — §B.1 adopts 6.6k calibration tokens as the default (an ablation run only on Qwen3-4B), while the appendix reports per-model calibration sets of 2,554 / 2,711 / 4,151 tokens; it never explains the gap.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Wearable world model** — the closest match in my notes and a direct one. OptR is exactly the KV-cache half of that pipeline: INT2 at 1/8 BF16 storage (2.32 effective bits/element at 64K), no weight training, calibration in minutes on one GPU, and no change to the paged cache layout. It is orthogonal to the weight-quantization half — the paper leaves model weights in BF16 throughout — so it stacks with, rather than substitutes for, whatever weight quantization I would use on wearable-class silicon. **The energy floor of inference** — the honest mechanism link is that OptR is not itself the energy lever; it is what makes the lever usable. The INT2 pipeline is what cuts KV storage and the memory traffic re-read every decode step, and OptR's own measured contribution is deliberately near-zero (within 2% on decode latency, throughput and prefill; +18 MiB at batch 128). So the paper is evidence that the accuracy tax on aggressive KV quantization can be paid *offline in calibration* rather than *online in sustained power*, which is the direction my note needs. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a mechanism-level rhyme in objective design, not in subject matter: OptR's key loss is a KL between the full-precision attention distribution and the quantized one, plus a term measured in the post-W_O space the next layer actually consumes, and my note's per-token reward has the same shape — expressed in the space the receiving model reads, not the space where the signal was generated. Table 4's 8-point spread quantifies what that placement choice is worth in one setting. **Over-reflection in search agents** — a stretch, but a supported one: the long-context table shows retrieval from long history is the first thing INT2 destroys, and confirm-then-keep-searching is precisely the behavior that grows the KV history each round, so the 63% pattern is a compounding cost under quantized serving. The paper never studies agents, so that is my extrapolation, not its claim.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the transferable pattern with a number attached — place the surrogate loss at the readout, not the representation. Table 4 holds the base rotation and the KL term fixed and moves only where the error is measured (65.33 / 62.67 / 70.67), and I want that as the standard sanity ablation whenever I write a proxy objective or a verifier signal, because the intermediate-space proxy here was *worse* than the raw-reconstruction proxy, not merely weaker. The open question the paper leaves for anyone running agents on quantized caches: the error budget is not uniform across history length — generation benchmarks look nearly BF16-clean while 128K retrieval sits at ~41% against 80.89, and calibration is done over only the final 64 query positions of short traces. What would a calibration objective look like that weights errors by how far back in the history the cached token sits, and would it close the gap this position-agnostic rotation does not?

[Source (arXiv 2608.02691)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02691) · [code](https://github.com/daniel-eai/Output-Aware-INT2-KV-Cache-Quantization)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Muse Glimmer Model Card</strong> · Meta Superintelligence Lab, August 2026</summary>

*Model card for an Apache-2.0 open-weight release (the card gives only "Model Release Date: August
2026"; the Hugging Face repo was created 2026-08-09), shipped alongside a 7-page "Muse Glimmer
Evaluation Methodology" report and separate GGUF and ExecuTorch-PTE repos. Muse Glimmer-30B is a
52-layer dense causal transformer of ~29.6B total parameters including a ~1.8B ViT-G/14 perception
encoder, 131,072-token context, distilled from the larger Muse Spark and aimed at agentic work
running entirely on one consumer GPU or Mac. The card claims only that the model "performs strongly
for its size class" — the wins concentrate in general agentic benchmarks (75.5 MCP Atlas public,
74.6 DeepSearch QA against Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B) rather than a sweep — at 233.4 tok/s on an
RTX 5090.*

**From the report**

> Architecture: "Dense Causal Transformer with Perception Encoder", ~29.6B total params — 52 layers, hidden 6656, GQA 32 query / 2 KV (16:1), head dim 128, SwiGLU FFN intermediate 19,968, attention pattern [Local, Local, Local, Global] repeating with a 2048 sliding window, gated attention, RoPE θ=500,000 on local layers only; perception encoder is a ~1.8B ViT-G/14 (50 layers, width 1536, patch 14, max 4,096 visual tokens per image); vocab 202,048; context 131,072; knowledge cutoff January 4, 2026. — §Model Overview
>
> Training: the entire distillation statement is one clause — "a 30-billion-parameter causal language model with a dedicated perception encoder, distilled from Muse Spark." The only post-training detail given is safety-side: safety SFT on "curated examples demonstrating correct safety behavior, including agentic safety scenarios covering tool-use boundaries, prompt injection resistance, and permission handling"; safety RL "with safety-specific reward signals that penalize policy violations while rewarding helpful responses to legitimate requests"; and "appropriate information flows" principles "embedded directly into model weights through dedicated synthetic training data." No data scale, no distillation objective, no general (non-safety) RL. — §intro, §Train-Time Mitigations
>
> Quantization: ~4-bit weights shrink the LM to "under 20 GB", which "leaves enough headroom for the model's KV cache, the perception encoder for image understanding, and the speculative decoding drafter to run simultaneously within a 24 GB or 32 GB envelope." K-Quant-Dynamic = 0.2% degradation at a 32 GB VRAM target; K-Quant-17GB = 1.0% at 24 GB; full precision targets 64 GB. Footnote: "Degradation measured using an average on accuracy metrics across 15 common benchmarks." — §Optimized for Local Deployments
>
> Speculative decoding: a DFlash block-diffusion drafter "predicts entire blocks of 16 tokens in a single forward pass" (5 draft layers, sliding window 2048 on all layers, 32 query / 8 KV). Measured with the K-Quant-17GB model plus the quantized drafter at batch size 1 and greedy decoding: RTX 5090 74.9 → 233.4 tok/s (3.1×), Apple M4 Max 23.7 → 37.8 (1.5×), M5 Max 26.6 → 50.2 (1.8×) — the Apple numbers via ExecuTorch, the RTX number via llama.cpp. — §Faster Generation Through Speculative Decoding
>
> Agentic headline numbers (Muse Glimmer-30B High Reasoning / Gemma4-31B Thinking / Qwen3.6-27B Thinking): MCP Atlas (Public) 75.5 / 54.2 / 62.5; DeepSearch QA 74.6 / 61.7 / 71.1; SWE-Bench Pro 51.2 / 36.9 / 50.2; 𝜏3-Banking 23.5 / 15.1 / 16.7; Gaia2 43.3 / 36.4 / 40.0; WildClawBench 47.6 / 37.6 / 43.2; AIME 2026 94.7 / 89.2 / 94.1. — §Benchmarks
>
> The card prints its losses rather than hiding them: OSWorld-Verified 65.9 vs Qwen3.6-27B's 75.6; TerminalBench 2.1 51.7 vs 60.7; SWE-Bench Verified 76.0 vs 77.2; SkillsBench 44.3 vs 46.6; GDPval-AA v2 953 vs 1141; GPQA Diamond 83.5 vs Gemma4-31B's 85.7; HLE Text 22.0 vs 23.6. On safety, CI Memories violation rate 26.4 (Gemma4's 12.1 is better) and Siren AgentDojo attack success 28.4 (Gemma4 25.6 better) at utility 94.2. — §Benchmarks
>
> Eval harness for the benchmarks Meta ran itself: MCP-Atlas = 500 public tasks over "20+ MCP tool servers (such as GitHub, Twelve Data, Notion)", graded by an LLM judge (Gemini 2.5 Pro) against ground-truth claims, pass above a 0.75 score threshold, mean over 4 runs. DeepSearchQA = 900 hard research questions with "a single browsing tool with three functions: search, open and find", a multi-step reason→call→observe loop, judge gpt-oss-120b extracting the predicted answer set and computing F1 against targets, same search engine for all models, 4 runs. SWE-Bench Pro (731) / Verified (500): scaffold is "a bash tool and a file operation tool", mean over 4 runs. AIME 2026 = all 30 questions over 10 runs; GPQA Diamond = all 198, sourced from Artificial Analysis. All Muse Glimmer numbers at high reasoning strength, temperature 1.0 / top_p 0.95 / top_k 64. — Eval Methodology
>
> Stated caveats: "For models other than Muse Glimmer, we report the most favorable result between self-reported scores or our internal reproductions", and "our evaluation setup (e.g. agent tools and system prompts) may not be specifically tuned for proprietary third-party models. Therefore, the results may not reflect these models' best performance." The OmniDocBench v1.5 number uses an internal protocol that folds formulas into text groups and swaps the official MGAM matcher for "a simpler Hungarian algorithm". Model-side: "the model may still make errors in multi-step reasoning, particularly in novel scenarios not well represented in training data", "quantized inference may show minor quality differences in edge cases", and "the model is not explicitly optimized for video; video input is processed as individual frames". — Eval Methodology §Overall/§Multimodal; card §Considerations and Limitations

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the DeepSearchQA paragraph in the methodology report first — it is my loop shape exactly (one browsing tool exposing search/open/find, reason → call → observe → repeat), but graded as F1 over an extracted answer *set*. Set-F1 rewards recall, so a trajectory that states an answer, confirms it, keeps searching and picks up one more correct item scores strictly higher; that is a metric which structurally cannot see over-search, and I should check whether my own scoring has the same blind spot. Then §Optimized for Local Deployments for the exchange rate I have been missing: 0.2% average degradation at a 32 GB target versus 1.0% at 24 GB across 15 benchmarks at roughly 4 bits — and note the decomposition, because the entire envelope is bought with weight quantization while the KV cache is only *budgeted* headroom, never compressed, at a 131K context. Read the methodology bullets as a disclosure template and then discount the margins by them: baselines take "the most favorable result between self-reported scores or our internal reproductions", the agent tools and system prompts are admittedly untuned for third-party models, OmniDocBench swaps the official matcher for Hungarian matching, and the sampling config is not symmetric either — Qwen3.6-27B runs at top_k=20 (temperature 0.6 on GAIA2/WildClawBench) against top_k=64 for Muse Glimmer and Gemma4-31B. Any one of those moves a 2–3 point gap. Finally, read the benchmark table against the effort knob: every published number is the single "high" column, the card ships four positions (low/medium/high/xhigh), and no low/medium/xhigh numbers appear anywhere — a shipped cost/benefit dial with zero published evidence about its curve.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — the card advertises "Failure Recovery" as a trained-in capability ("when a tool call fails or returns an unexpected result, the model diagnoses the error and retries rather than halt") with no stated stop criterion, and the only exposed control over persistence is a global system-prompt dial, `Reasoning strength: low/medium/high/xhigh` — the ExecuTorch server even rejects the OpenAI `reasoning_effort` field with a 400, so that dial is the whole interface. That is the global patience knob my note argues cannot replace a state-conditioned stop/pivot policy, now shipped as a product surface; and DeepSearchQA's answer-set F1 means continuing to search after a confirmed hit costs nothing in the channel that evaluated it. **Wearable world model** — the clearest public accounting of the on-device budget my note assumes: LM under 20 GB at ~4 bits so KV cache, perception encoder and drafter co-fit in a 24 or 32 GB envelope, at a measured 0.2% (32 GB) versus 1.0% (24 GB) accuracy cost. The instructive gap is that all of the envelope comes from weight quantization and none from KV compression — at 131K context the KV half is exactly what a simulation-style rollout blows up, and the card gives no number for it. **AgentPlanet** — Gaia2 as they run it is my three-role factorization externalized into non-colluding parts: a hand-authored stateful "universe" of everyday apps in an isolated container, an event daemon perturbing the world mid-episode, and an in-container judge scoring realized tool actions and end state against oracle events. Reward-channel integrity holds there precisely because the world author is not the policy, which makes it the control condition a one-model-plays-all-three setup has to beat. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a gap rather than a link: the card gives the two endpoints (a January 4, 2026 knowledge cutoff, and the bare clause "distilled from Muse Spark") and nothing between them — no objective, no token- or logit-level detail, no statement of the tokenizer relationship between the two models, and no word on whether the larger model's later knowledge was transferred or clipped at the student's cutoff. The cleanest public instance of the question my note poses, published without the answer.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the reporting discipline on the benchmarks Meta ran itself — each entry states task count, judge identity and run count (MCP-Atlas 500 / Gemini 2.5 Pro / 0.75 threshold / 4 runs; DeepSearchQA 900 / gpt-oss-120b / 4 runs; Gaia2 800 / gpt-oss-120b / 3 runs; AIME 2026 30 questions / 10 runs) — which is directly portable to numbers whose single-rollout flip rate I already know is large. Worth noting the asymmetry too: the Artificial-Analysis-sourced rows (𝜏3-Banking, GDPval-AA v2, Terminal-Bench 2.1, GPQA, HLE) are pointers to a third-party methodology with no judge or run count of their own. The open question I would chase before assuming block-diffusion drafting transfers: the 233.4 tok/s / 3.1× figure was measured through llama.cpp against the GGUF artifacts, while the shipped CUDA ExecuTorch export clamps the DFlash block length to [2, 4] with n_draft ≤ 3 where Metal accepts [2, 16]. If the headline speedup needs a 16-token block, the advertised number and the artifact an NVIDIA user downloads are not the same configuration.

[Source (Hugging Face model card)](https://huggingface.co/meta-models/Muse-Glimmer-30B) · [Evaluation methodology (PDF)](https://research.meta.ai/static/muse-glimmer-methodology)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Mismatch Matters: On-Policy Distillation Beyond Token Agreement</strong> · HKU + USTC + CUHK, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.09836, submitted 10 Aug 2026; cs.AI, cross-listed cs.CL) identifying a failure
mode of on-policy distillation it calls "degenerate agreement": the student falls into repetitive
loops that make the teacher's conditional predictions collapse onto its own, driving measured
teacher–student KL to near zero while the response is globally broken. It reframes supervision
around mismatch rather than agreement — splitting it into student-excess tokens (sampled but
teacher-rejected) and student-deficit tokens (teacher-preferred but almost never sampled) — and
proposes TIDE, which applies bounded Hellinger shaping at gated excess positions and analytic
teacher top-K cross-entropy at deficit positions. Trained one epoch on DAPO-Math-17K with two
teacher–student pairs and evaluated on nine math benchmarks, TIDE lifts Avg@8 from 6.9% to 20.3%
under strong mismatch while cutting average response length from 22,395 to 7,294 tokens.*

**From the report**

> Degenerate agreement, diagnosed: over OPD training the fraction of rollouts containing repetitive loops rises from 16.8% to 48.4% on a fixed DAPO-Math-17K prompt set (one example repeats "I hope it is correct" 149 times); constructing controlled prefixes with increasing repetition counts lowers both student–teacher KL and teacher entropy, with sixteen repetitions dropping KL by 63×, and median teacher–student KL reaching 0.0004 nats at 100% top-1 agreement. — §2.2, Figs. 2/4
>
> The teacher does not correct the loop, it joins it: prompted with naturally occurring repetitive student prefixes under greedy decoding, the teacher continues the same student-induced repetitive pattern in 93% of cases. — §C.1
>
> Agreement is the wrong gate: supervising only the 60% best-matched positions raises Qwen3-1.7B-Base Avg@8 from 6.87 to just 7.18, whereas supervising the 20% most-mismatched positions more than doubles it to 14.58. — §2.3, §C.2
>
> Method: TIDE = L_exc + λ·L_def with independent routing. The excess branch replaces the unbounded log-ratio with bounded Hellinger shaping h(a)=2[exp(a/2)−1], proved to satisfy −2 < h(a) < 0 and h(a)=a+O(a²), and to correspond to the gradient of squared Hellinger distance; the deficit branch adds analytic cross-entropy over the teacher's renormalized top-K support so teacher-preferred tokens need never be sampled. Defaults K=16, ρ⁻=ρ⁺=0.2, λ=1.0, with a batch-quantile gate rather than an absolute threshold. — §3.1–3.3, Props. 1–2, Alg. 1
>
> Why deficit tokens need analytic injection: deficit positions are 1.32% of non-loop tokens with median student probability 2.04e-4 (an expected ~4,909 draws), and under the four-rollout budget 95.5% are sampled with less than 1% probability; a deficit-only intervention alone raises Avg@8 from 6.87 to 18.32. — §C.3, Table 7
>
> Headline, strong mismatch (Qwen3-8B → Qwen3-1.7B-Base): OPD ends at 6.9% Avg@8 — exactly the untrained student's 6.9% — while TIDE reaches 20.3% (+13.4), Pass@8 25.3% → 34.2%, average length 22,395 → 7,294 tokens, missing-\boxed rate 65.5% → 5.4%; TIDE also beats AOPD (+2.0), GRPO (+4.0), PowerOPD (+4.6) and FiRe-OPD (+5.4), and is best-or-tied Avg@8 on seven of nine benchmarks *under strong mismatch*. — Tables 1–3, §4.2
>
> Weak mismatch (JustRL-DeepSeek-1.5B → DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B) gains are small: Avg@8 45.7% → 46.7%, Pass@8 61.8% → 65.0%, and both students stay below their teachers (teacher Avg@8 49.2% weak, 36.6% strong versus TIDE's 20.3%). — Table 1, §4.2
>
> Recipe and harness: one epoch on DAPO-Math-17K in verl, prompt batch 64, 4 on-policy rollouts per prompt, max response 7,168 tokens, AdamW at constant 1e-6, one policy update per rollout batch (so the importance ratio is 1 and PPO clipping never engages), no auxiliary KL and no format reward. Eval is vLLM at temperature 0.7 / top-p 0.95 / n=8, cap 31,744 tokens, thinking mode disabled, a rule-based symbolic \boxed verifier with no LLM judge, unweighted macro-average over nine benchmarks, final checkpoint only with no benchmark-based selection. — §4.1, §D.2/D.4/D.5
>
> Stated limitation: "TIDE assumes a locally reliable teacher. Our evaluation is limited to two teacher-student pairs and mathematical reasoning; dialogue, code, and multilingual settings remain open. Moreover, our fixed-state, operator-level theory gives no global convergence guarantee, and adaptive keep-rate schedules warrant study." — §5 Conclusion (limitations paragraph)

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the §2.2 probe is the reusable part — freeze the repeating unit, synthesize prefixes at k = 1..16 repetitions, plot supervisor KL and entropy against k. I want to run that exact construction on search trajectories, where the repeating unit is a re-query cycle, to check whether any agreement- or consistency-based signal I trust also collapses toward zero on loops that are globally wrong. §2.3 with §C.2 is the number to quote in an argument about token selection: 60% best-matched positions moved Avg@8 6.87 → 7.18, while the 20% most-mismatched got 14.58 — a direct empirical case against overlap-gated selection. §3.2 with §C.3 is the mechanism I care about most, analytic cross-entropy over the teacher's renormalized top-16 at positions the student would need ~4,900 draws to sample; that is how you move a pattern the student cannot reach by sampling. And Table 3 is a cheap operational habit worth copying: report average length, no-answer rate and Distinct-4 next to accuracy, because in the strong-mismatch setting OPD leaves accuracy at the untrained student's 6.87 while length goes 13,465 → 22,395 tokens and Distinct-4 falls 0.099 → 0.062 (Table 6) — accuracy alone would not have flagged it. One reading caution: the abstract claims a 3.6× length reduction, but Table 3's own 22,395 / 7,294 is 3.07×, so quote the token counts, not the ratio.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Post-cutoff distillation** — the deficit branch is the exact transfer channel that note needs: instead of waiting for a teacher-preferred token to be sampled, inject analytic cross-entropy over the teacher's top-K at positions the student assigns ~2e-4 mass, which is precisely the profile of a fact the student could not have had. Two caveats for me: both pairs are within-family, so the cross-tokenizer alignment half of the problem gets no evidence here, and their gate is by mismatch magnitude, not by whether the token carries new knowledge. **Over-reflection in search agents** — the same structural failure with a different supervisor. Their student states a locally-consistent continuation and the teacher confirms it (KL down 63×; the teacher continues the loop in 93% of greedy probes), which is the distillation twin of confirm-the-answer-against-a-real-hit-then-keep-searching. It argues my stop/pivot policy should be conditioned on a mismatch statistic that stays informative *inside* a loop, since the confirmation signal itself is what the loop exploits. **AgentPlanet** — "student-induced teacher hacking" is Goodhart on a learned supervisor whose conditioning context the optimizer controls, structurally the same hazard as a world-author that can make its own tasks easy. Their two anti-Goodhart moves port cleanly: bound the correction (Hellinger, provably in (−2,0)) so a handful of extreme positions cannot dominate, and gate by batch quantile rather than an absolute threshold so the gate cannot be gamed by shifting the score scale. A stretch on domain — this is math next-token distillation with no environment or task authoring. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a stretch: TIDE is strictly on-policy with one policy update per rollout batch, so it says nothing about update-to-data ratio or off-policy reuse. The only contact is a different data-efficiency lever — the deficit branch extracts supervision from *unsampled* tokens via one extra top-K forward pass, i.e. more signal per rollout rather than more gradient steps per rollout.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the two shaping primitives, ported into reward design wherever a few extreme negative advantages dominate — h(a)=2[exp(a/2)−1], bounded in (−2,0) and reducing to a as a→0 (a faithful small-signal replacement for the log-ratio, unlike clip/tanh which need an arbitrary saturation scale), combined with a batch-quantile keep-rate gate instead of an absolute threshold. The open question their setup leaves: the deficit branch needs a full next-token distribution to define top-K, so what is the analogue when the supervisor is a verifier or a retrieved search result rather than a teacher LM — is there any way to name "the teacher-preferred continuation the student never samples" when supervision is outcome-level? Also unanswered: TIDE at 20.3% is still far under the 36.6% teacher, so how much of the strong-mismatch gap is the objective versus the 1.7B student's capacity.

[Source (arXiv 2608.09836)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09836) · [code](https://github.com/yzc-666/TIDE)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>OmnilingualGAIA2: Evaluating the Multilingual Gap in Frontier AI Agents</strong> · Meta Superintelligence Labs, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.08775, v1 9 Aug 2026 / v2 11 Aug 2026; CC BY-SA 4.0) presenting a
machine-translated multilingual expansion of the GAIA2 agentic benchmark — four of GAIA2's seven
capabilities (adaptability, ambiguity, execution, search) across ten target languages in five
writing systems, 160 scenarios per language-capability cell, with a localised human-calibrated
verifier that preserves GAIA2's executable grading path. Seven frontier and open-weight agents are
run under a fixed harness with pass@3 as the headline metric. The central claim is that a
cross-lingual gap of 8.8–18.4 pass@3 points is universal in direction across agents, is majority
model-driven (55.4%) rather than translation-driven, sits above a bounded translation-contamination
floor of 6.4% of scenario–language pairs, and does not close with model scale.*

**From the report**

> Core method: the pipeline translates only the language-dependent surface (task description, universe app-state text, oracle-event text spans) and freezes the executable structure (identifiers, timestamps). Universes are translated first in per-app batched calls, a reference term table is built from the translated surfaces to enforce cross-surface entity consistency, then tasks and oracle events are translated under that table, with a final sweep substituting residual source-language spans — "crucial to avoid constructing unsolvable tasks that mention non-existing entities". — §3.2, Fig. 1
>
> Translation recipe: BOUQuET selects Gemma-4-31B-Instruct as the top-ranking open-license translator for all ten targets, confirmed by pairwise LLM-as-judge over 200 app-stratified spans adjudicated by two judges (GPT-5.4, Gemini-3.1-Pro) with majority over three position-randomised trials = 400 pooled verdicts per (translator, language) cell. A translate-then-review second pass was dropped as a near-no-op: it "rewrites fewer than 9% of fields", all sixteen reviewer configs have Δ confidence intervals crossing zero (pooled Δ = +1.0 pp, CI [−1.0, +3.0]), while roughly doubling per-scenario latency. — §3.2, §B.1–B.2
>
> Verifier: the LLM-as-a-Judge component is localised on two axes — prompts stripped of English-only few-shot priming, and the judge model moved to GPT-OSS-120B (5.1B active params, about an order of magnitude fewer than the dense Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct reference judge). On English the four prompt × model configs are indistinguishable (full-corpus κ 0.714–0.732, κ ≥ 0.928 on the LLM-touched slice) and the shipped config agrees with GAIA2's reference judge at κ = 0.981; under translation, on the n=153 LLM-touched slice κ ranges 0.886 (tur) to 0.971 (eng, cmn), every Δκ inside a pre-registered ≤0.10 hazard threshold. — §3.3, §C
>
> Headline numbers (pooled pass@3): Claude-4.7-Opus 82.7 English → 73.6 target-language average (9.1 pp gap); GPT-5.4 68.4 → 58.5 (9.9); Gemini-3.1-Pro 62.2 → 53.4 (8.8); Qwen-3.6-35B-A3B 56.6 → 38.2 (18.4, the widest). The capability ordering search > execution > adaptability > ambiguity is stable across languages — "translation reshapes the level without reordering difficulty". — Table 1, §5.1
>
> Gap attribution: failures are stratified by within-scenario determinism *before* reweighting, because deterministic collapses are 59% translation-driven while stochastic partial regressions — which carry more of the gap — are 75% model-driven. Reweighted, 55.4% (CI 50.3–60.7) of the cross-lingual gap is genuine model failure, 34.5% (29.5–39.1) a translation defect, 10.1% (7.1–13.5) a verifier artefact; "restricting attention to complete collapses inverts this to a translation-dominated ≈55%/33% split", which is the selection bias the stratification removes. Five annotators confirmed the automatic fault side on 128/140 items = 91.4% (85.6–95.0). — §6.2, Tables 3–4
>
> Contamination floor: all 6,056 English × target scenario–language pairs are cross-tabulated by outcome; any pair whose target passes on ≥1 run is translation-clean by construction (74.5% of pairs), leaving only the 25.5% "target-never-solves" region to audit.
