The translation-defect rate falls monotonically as English competence drops — 59% (pass→fail), 16% (partial→fail), 10% (fail→fail, the blind spot, audited by input-faithfulness comparison rather than pass/fail) — so "translation defects render only 6.4% of all pairs unsolvable in the target, clustered on a handful of oracle- and keyword-mistranslation bugs". — §6.2, §G Table 19
>
> Eval setup: all agents run under the OpenClaw harness except Kimi-2.6, which runs under OpenCode because it "scores near zero when driven by OpenClaw", root-caused to the harness dropping its native streaming reasoning_content and tool_calls. Open-weight agents serve at a 131K default context with thinking mode on, and rollouts failing purely from trajectory truncation are re-scored at the model's max window — truncation affects up to 20% of cmn, 34% of jpn and 44% of hin rollouts. pass@3 is the headline metric, n=160 scenarios per language-capability cell, with paired McNemar tests and Wilson intervals. — §4
>
> Stated limitation: "OmnilingualGAIA2 is constructed by an automatic machine-translation pipeline in a translator-only configuration, without human post-editing of the full corpus"; the on-pipeline signals "do not substitute for a thorough per-language MT-quality audit against professional human references", so "a bounded but non-zero residual translation-defect floor persists in the evaluated release, concentrated in non-Latin, morphologically rich target languages". — §Limitations

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §6.2 for the determinism stratification, not the 55/35/10 headline. They split failures into deterministic (target never passes) versus stochastic (target passes some runs) *before* reweighting, and show that sampling only complete collapses inverts the answer from 55% model-driven to ~55% translation-driven. That is the exact selection bias I would have walked into auditing my own translated or synthesized eval sets, where single-rollout flip rates are high enough that a one-shot failure means almost nothing. Then §G Table 19 for the cheapest contamination bound I have seen: any source × target pair whose target passes at least once is translation-clean by construction, so 74.5% of pairs need no audit and only the target-never-solves quadrant gets attention — paired with their input-faithfulness audit for the both-fail cell, which works without any pass/fail signal at all. Read §5.3 and §5.4 together as a commit-policy result rather than a translation result: the regression is invisible in the quantitative and categorical checkers (<0.4 pp) and lands entirely on the tool-count gate (+10.6 pp) and the textual-reply checker (+6.2 pp), while the write-action share of tool calls drops 32.1% → 29.9% — under degraded comprehension the agent forages more and commits less. And §H for the sharpest single observation: of 939 cmn/hin/jpn scenarios, 88 (9%) failed for script-related reasons, 53 of them the agent mishandling script inconsistency in the environment data — and in many of those the agent explicitly noticed both script forms of an entity in its own reasoning yet still matched on only one. That is not a knowledge gap and not a translation bug; it is a missing state-conditioned retry policy, which is a training target rather than a prompt fix.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — §5.3/§5.4 give the mirror image of what my 23,110 tagged trajectories show. Where my traces over-search after already confirming an answer, their agents off-English shift budget from state-changing writes to exploratory reads (write share 32.1% → 29.9%, 30.5% → 28.1%, 29.3% → 28.0%) and Claude's regressing scenarios issue *fewer* calls rather than more. Same axis, both directions — direct evidence that a global patience knob is the wrong instrument and the stop/commit decision has to be conditioned on a comprehension state, since here the state variable is simply which language the prompt is in. **AgentPlanet** — their three-way fault partition (55.4% policy / 34.5% world-corruption / 10.1% verifier, with a scenario-clustered bootstrap and 91.4% human confirmation) is exactly the reward-channel-integrity audit my world-author needs standing. If one model authors the initial state, the oracle and the grader, the failure modes it can hide behind are precisely their translation-defect and verifier-artefact categories, and §6.2 shows those are separable from genuine policy failure at scale by automatic triage. Their §6.3 count-gate finding is the anti-Goodhart lesson: the oracle grades write-action *count*, so an agent that omits an attendee, deletes the event and recreates it correctly reaches the right end state and is scored wrong — an end-state-blind reward is a surface an authoring loop will learn to sit on. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — partial stretch, since the paper does no RL. But the pass@1 14.0 / pass@3 9.1 / pass@3-all 17.5 gap spread for Claude says the multilingual deficit is largely variance rather than capability: the solution is reachable within three attempts but not reliably. That is exactly the regime where raising the update-to-data ratio and replaying rare successful rollouts pays, because the informative trajectories exist in the buffer and are being thrown away. **Inventing the z-axis** — a stretch and I will call it one, since nothing here is 3D. The transferable mechanism is their both-fail audit protocol: when there is no pass/fail signal to lean on, audit the derived artifact against its source and flag only discrepancies that would change the correct action (9.9% defect rate, CI 5.6–16.9). Same shape as asking whether a reconstructed volume invented a structure that changes the read, rather than whether it merely looks different.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the contamination bound, wholesale — cross-tabulate source × target outcomes over every pair, declare every pair whose target passes at least once translation-clean by construction, and audit only the target-never-solves quadrant, stratified by how well the source does (59% / 16% / 10% defect rates across pass→fail, partial→fail, fail→fail, size-weighted to a 6.4% floor). It converts an open-ended human-review problem into an audit of a quarter of the corpus and is directly runnable on any localized or re-authored eval set I build. The open question they raise and decline to answer: they show the hard action-count gate mis-scores idempotent self-corrections that reach the correct end state, and explicitly label those "measurement artefacts of end-state-blind grading" — but they never re-grade under an end-state-equivalence checker. How much of the 10.6 pp tool-count-mismatch regression, the single largest component of the gap, survives a checker that grades final state instead of action count? That number decides whether count-gating belongs in the assertion-based verifiers I write at all.

[Source (arXiv 2608.08775)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08775)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Introducing Muse Code and Muse Spark 1.2</strong> · Meta Superintelligence Labs, August 2026</summary>

*Official Meta announcement (August 5, 2026; published under the Meta AI Research brand with Meta
Superintelligence Labs as the declared author) introducing Muse Code, a beta terminal coding agent
built as a main agent loop plus persistent async background agents over an append-only local event
log, and Muse Spark 1.2, a coding-focused update to the July 9 Muse Spark 1.1 (1,048,576-token
context on the Meta Model API) that was co-trained with the Muse Code harness. Meta's charts report
Terminal-Bench 2.1 82.9% (vs 76.2% for 1.1), DeepSWE v1.1 59.3% (vs 53.0%), and a chart-topping
MCP Atlas 90.3%, alongside a "contributor" API tier at $0.10/$0.20 per M input/output tokens (vs
$1.25/$4.25 standard) in exchange for permission to train future Meta models on prompts and
completions; a linked evaluation-methodology note details harnesses, sandboxes, and attempt
counts.*

**From the report**

> Muse Code runs "a simple agent loop plus a set of async background agents" that "remain active throughout each session, rather than being spawned for individual tasks," on top of a local event log where every model call, tool run, approval, and edit is appended, making the runtime "replay-exact and restart-safe." — §Muse Code
>
> "We significantly scaled up training compute on coding tasks while expanding training environment diversity," and the model was "extensively trained on long-horizon coding tasks, including whole-repository generation, large end-to-end projects, and auto-research," leveraging planning, goal conditioning, and context compaction. — §Muse Spark 1.2, §Long-Horizon
>
> "We co-trained Muse Spark 1.2 with Muse Code"; training "included rejection sampled harness trajectories and recipe optimizations for goals, compaction, and subagents, alongside the integration of the Muse Code toolset to maximize harness compatibility." — §Co-Training With Muse Code
>
> Self-improvement loop: "we used Muse Spark 1.1 to generate challenging coding environments and instruction-following templates," then the model "graded candidate solutions on how well they satisfied those requirements, producing a scalable training dataset for Muse Spark 1.2." — §Self-Improvement
>
> Terminal-Bench 2.1: Muse Spark 1.2 (in Muse Code) 82.9% versus Muse Spark 1.1 (in mini-swe-agent) 76.2%, behind chart-leader Opus 5 (max)+Claude Code at 86.7%; DeepSWE 1.1: 59.3% versus 53.0% (Opus 5 65.0%, GPT-5.6 Terra 64.8%); Meta Internal Coding Bench (440 tasks from real internal pull requests, internet disabled): 70.6% versus 68.3% (Opus 5 79.4%). — §Coding evaluation charts
>
> General agents: MCP Atlas 90.3%, top of Meta's chart (1.1 = 88.1%, Opus 5 xhigh = 85.8%; Scale AI's own harness over 1,000 human-authored tasks / 36 real MCP servers / 220 tools, pass = claim coverage ≥ 0.75), and GDPVal-AA v2 Elo 1631 versus 1371 for 1.1 (Opus 5 1852; human baseline anchored at 1,000, run by Artificial Analysis). — §General Agents charts; methodology PDF
>
> Eval setup: "each model is evaluated with its selected agent product" at "the maximum available reasoning strength" (xhigh for Muse Spark 1.2/1.1), each attempt in an isolated Daytona cloud sandbox graded by the benchmark's verifier, reported as "average task success rate (pass@1) across five attempts"; Terminal-Bench 2.1 = all 89 official tasks, DeepSWE v1.1 = 113 tasks across 91 repos in five languages with handwritten functional verifiers plus regression checks. — methodology PDF
>
> Self-stated caveats: the DeepSWE runs are "not harness-identical to the leaderboard" (the official leaderboard uses mini-swe-agent for every model), and third-party numbers are "best-effort" because the setup "may not be specifically tuned for proprietary third-party models" and "may not reflect these models' best performance." — methodology PDF

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §Self-Improvement first: predecessor-generated challenging environments plus template-conditioned grading is the minimal published environment-synthesis flywheel, and what it omits — any independent gate between generator and grader — is precisely where my synthesis pipelines insert deterministic checks, so I read it as a statement of what a frontier lab considers acceptable reward-channel hygiene. Then §Co-Training against the methodology PDF's harness table: 1.2 is scored inside Muse Code while 1.1 is scored inside mini-swe-agent, so the 82.9-vs-76.2 headline is a model+harness joint delta by construction — and "rejection sampled harness trajectories" with recipe optimizations for goals/compaction/subagents is the clearest public admission that the scaffold sits inside the training distribution rather than being an eval-time wrapper. The methodology PDF itself is the part worth keeping for eval craft: pass@1 averaged over five attempts, per-benchmark verifier mechanics (DeepSWE functional verifier plus regression checks on a pristine checkout; MCP Atlas claim-coverage ≥ 0.75), and two self-stated caveats about harness non-identity and untuned third-party setups. Two numbers to file: MCP Atlas 90.3% via Scale AI's own pipeline as a frontier reference on a benchmark I run, and the contributor tier as a live market price on agentic interaction data — a 12.5–21× discount for the data flywheel.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the self-improvement loop is a two-of-three-roles instance of my factorization: Muse Spark 1.1 plays world-builder (generating challenging coding environments and templates) and the reward channel (grading candidate solutions), with no described deterministic-gate checklist between generator and grader — reward-channel integrity rests on the same model family being trained, which is the anti-Goodhart gap my gate design targets. **Over-reflection** — their fix for redundant exploration is architectural: persistent background agents that stay resident to "avoid redundant information gathering," plus goal conditioning and context compaction — attacking the same confirm-then-keep-searching default I measured at ~63% across the search trajectories I audited, but from the harness side rather than my policy-side per-type repair and state-conditioned stop/pivot RL. **The rollouts we throw away (FlashSAC)** (stretch) — "rejection sampled harness trajectories" means discarding losing rollouts from a harness whose long-horizon episodes run 1,000+ tool calls over up to 24 hours (kernel case study) — exactly the expensive-rollout economics where my note argues replay-buffer reuse should beat discard-heavy pipelines; the post gives no discard fraction and does not say whether off-policy reuse was tried.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the co-training move — put harness behaviors (compaction events, subagent handoffs, goal restatement) into the SFT distribution via rejection-sampled harness trajectories instead of treating the scaffold as fixed at eval time, and report model+harness as the joint unit, the way their charts pair each model with its selected agent product. The open question the post leaves: nothing is said to gate the self-improvement grader — no independent verifier, held-out check, or contamination control between 1.1-generated environments and the graded training set (the executable verifiers described apply to evaluation, not the training loop) — so how they avoid grader-collapse or circular validation is unstated.

[Source (Meta blog)](https://research.meta.ai/blog/introducing-muse-code-and-muse-spark-1-2)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Self-Distilled Reward Shaping</strong> · USTC + Alibaba, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.03223, submitted 4 Aug 2026; 17 pages, CC BY 4.0) proposing ADRS, which turns
sparse trajectory-level agent rewards into token-level credit without a second model: the same
frozen behaviour-policy snapshot re-scores already-sampled tokens a second time with task-matched
procedural "skill" text prepended to the context. Each realized token's privileged score is centered
against the mean privileged score within its interaction step, multiplied by a scalar reliability
gate derived from the within-group covariance between privileged confidence and realized return, and
added to the base token reward* before *the GRPO/GiGPO advantage operator rather than as an auxiliary
distillation loss. Evaluated on ALFWorld, WebShop and Search-R1-style QA with Qwen2.5-3B/7B-Instruct
and Qwen3-1.7B, it reports its largest gains on the longest-horizon environment (ALFWorld 94.5% vs
84.4% at 3B) while keeping rollouts and inference completely skill-free.*

**From the report**

> Core method: the privileged branch is context-only self-distillation — "the same frozen snapshot rescores the realized tokens under ρ(x) ⊕ h; this branch changes only the context: it neither samples another trajectory nor introduces a separate or larger teacher, and privileged text is absent from student rollouts and inference." The privileged score of each realized token is then centered against the *step mean of privileged scores*, b_s = (1/|V_s|)·Σ_j l^T_{s,j}, giving q_{s,j} = l^T_{s,j} − b_s with Σ_j q̂_{s,j} = 0 — "a positive q̂ indicates that a sampled token receives a privileged score above the step average". Table 1 contrasts this explicitly with the prior method's gated teacher–student token gap. — §3, §4.1
>
> Reliability gate: Proposition 1 gives d_g = Cov_g(α, R) / (ᾱ_g(1 − ᾱ_g)), so "the sign of TVA agrees with the within-group empirical covariance between privileged confidence and return"; the detached gate m_u = sg[σ(τ·d̃)] rises above 0.5 for positive association, is set to 0.5 for groups with fewer than two units, and is 1 when TVA is off. TVA has two granularities (L2 completion-level, L3 step-level) plus a non-TVA L1 token fallback used only when L2/L3 tensors are unavailable. — §4.2, §A.6
>
> Injection point: the teacher reward r^T = η·m·q̂ is added to the base token reward *before* advantage construction, and the final coefficient is A^ADRS = A^traj + η·Z^T. Because a step-shared gate makes Σ_j r^T_{s,j} = 0 (Proposition 2), Proposition 3 shows the GRPO trajectory score — and hence A^traj — is provably unchanged; the entire effect is within-step token-level redistribution of update strength. — §4.3, §A.7
>
> Training recipe: GRPO and GiGPO backbones; Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen3-1.7B; 8 rollouts per prompt, 1 PPO epoch, lr 1e-6, bf16, train temperature 1.0 / eval 0.4; train batch 16/16/128 and max turns 50/15/4 for ALFWorld/WebShop/Search; η = 0.1 / 0.02 / 0.05–0.1; multiple NVIDIA H100 GPUs. — §5.1, §A.5, Tables 5–6
>
> Privileged-signal source: 37 general + 90 task-specific skills built from 619 training-split memories (ALFWorld 223 = 113 successful / 110 failed; Search 196 = 132/64; WebShop 200 = 120/80). Selection is deterministic task-matching, not nearest-neighbour retrieval, and the skill text is inserted verbatim as "[Privileged Skill Information]" only in the teacher rescoring pass. — §A.4
>
> Headline numbers: ALFWorld overall success 94.5% at Qwen2.5-3B versus 84.4% for the closest prior method (+10.1 pts), 96.1% vs 85.9% at 7B, and 62.5% vs 53.9% at Qwen3-1.7B. WebShop 3B reaches 87.5 score / 76.6% success versus 85.0 / 68.0. Extended to 300 steps, ALFWorld ADRS-global+entropy/clip hits 97.7 best / 96.1 final against GRPO's 92.2 / 86.7, and TVA on GiGPO adds +0.8 peak and +2.4 last-3. — Table 2, Tables 3/9/10, §5.2
>
> Eval harness: ADRS "follows the benchmark, training, and evaluation setup of SDAR". 128 ALFWorld validation tasks scored by family (Pick/Look/Clean/Heat/Cool/Pick2); 128 WebShop tasks per checkpoint sampled from goals 0–499 with both normalized score and exact-completion success; Search evaluated on the full 51K seven-dataset validation set every 75 optimizer steps, with a 504-example development subset every 15 steps used for the η sweep; all evaluation at temperature 0.4, and all unstarred methods evaluated with no skill context. — §A.1–A.3, Table 4
>
> Caveats the paper states: η = 0.005 peaks at 38.3 (step 90) then collapses to 9.6 by step 150, "showing that an excessively weak teacher coefficient can produce unstable checkpoint behavior rather than a smooth reduction toward the outcome-only baseline"; Proposition 1 is "observational and batch-local"; and Proposition 3's equivalence holds only "at the behavior-policy point with clipping inactive" (§A.B further excludes multi-epoch PPO by name). — §4.2–4.3, §B, §C.1
>
> Caveats visible in the tables but not discussed: in the matched fixed-step comparison the Search gain is essentially nil — GRPO 38.4 → ADRS-global 38.3 at step 150 (−0.1) and 40.2 → 40.5 at step 300 (+0.3) — against +10.1 on ALFWorld (Table 10); and at Qwen2.5-7B ADRS trails the prior method on Search (48.2 vs 49.0) and WebShop (88.0/79.7 vs 89.4/82.8) (Table 2), rows §5.2 passes over. Note also that Table 2 and Table 10 disagree on Search at step 150 for Qwen2.5-3B (36.4 → 45.0 versus 38.4 → 38.3) for what §A.5 says is the same run, and the paper never reconciles them.

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §4.2 with Proposition 1 first and little else — d_g = Cov_g(α, R) / (ᾱ(1−ᾱ)) is a two-line, model-free integrity statistic on a self-generated reward channel, computed from the 8 rollouts I already have per prompt, and it answers the question I actually care about: is this auxiliary signal correlated with a return it did not author, in this batch, right now. Then Proposition 2 alongside the final advantage, because it reframes what "reward shaping" means here — the step-shared gate makes the teacher reward sum to zero within a step, so the trajectory score is untouched and the entire effect is redistributing update magnitude across tokens inside one decision. That is a far safer place to inject a synthetic signal than anything that moves trajectory-level return, and it is the property I would want in hand before letting any generated reward near my objective. Read Table 10's fixed-step rows before Table 2's headline: Search goes 38.4 → 38.3 at step 150 and 40.2 → 40.5 at step 300, against +10.1 on 50-turn ALFWorld — so dense token credit appears to buy little at a 4-turn horizon, which is where most of my search evals sit. I hold that reading loosely, since Table 2 reports +8.6 on the same benchmark and step and the paper never reconciles the two. And read §C.1 for the deployment risk: η = 0.005 peaks at 38.3 by step 90 then falls to 9.6 at step 150, so the shaping coefficient does not degrade gracefully toward the outcome-only baseline as you shrink it — any shaped-reward run of mine needs a per-environment sweep and a collapse watchdog, not a single default.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the cleanest published instantiation of the gate I keep sketching. The paper separates "teacher confidence" from "teacher reliability" explicitly (Fig. 1 shows a trajectory that is more canonical, with higher confidence, and return 0) and then makes the gate provably equal to the sign of the within-group covariance between the privileged channel's confidence and the environment return. Ported to my setting: let the world-authoring role contribute to credit only in proportion to how well its confidence tracks a return it did not produce, and set the gate to a neutral 0.5 when the comparison group is too small to estimate the covariance. **Over-reflection in search agents** — §5.5 is the measurement instrument I want. Splitting the signed teacher–student log-prob gap into action tokens (−0.8582), object tokens (+0.4591) and style tokens (0.0085) at step 150 — a 95.3× magnitude ratio — is the decomposition needed to credit a stop or pivot decision token differently from the surrounding prose, instead of a global patience knob. The caveat that matters: their Search environment is capped at 4 turns and shows no gain there, so the mechanism is demonstrated on 50-turn embodied control, not on the confirm-then-keep-searching loop I tagged. **Post-cutoff distillation** — mechanically the right shape, one axis simpler. ADRS scores the *same* realized student tokens twice under two contexts from *one* frozen snapshot, so the privileged channel isolates what the extra information moved without a second model in the loop, and the within-step centering is the right fix for raw log-prob offsets drifting with position and context length. Cross-tokenizer alignment — the harder half of the problem my note poses — remains untouched here, since the two passes share a model and a vocabulary by construction. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a complementary lever, worth naming precisely so I do not conflate them. FlashSAC's answer to expensive rollouts is more gradient steps per rollout; ADRS runs 1 PPO epoch and instead extracts more credit per rollout via one extra frozen forward pass over tokens already collected, at zero additional environment interaction. Since the two act on different terms, stacking high-UTD off-policy reuse on top of pre-advantage token shaping is a cheap open experiment — though ADRS's derivation only holds "at the behavior-policy point with clipping inactive", which is exactly the assumption high-UTD off-policy reuse breaks.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the TVA gate as a standalone monitor rather than as a method — d_g = Cov_g(α, R)/(ᾱ(1−ᾱ)) over the 8 rollouts I already sample per prompt costs nothing and gives a per-batch scalar answering "is my shaped or synthetic reward channel currently correlated with real environment return". I would log it on every run that injects a non-environment signal and treat a persistently negative d_g as a Goodhart alarm rather than waiting for the eval to regress. The sharp open question the paper leaves: it never explains the sign of its own diagnostic. The privileged context assigns action tokens a large *negative* teacher-minus-ordinary gap (−0.8582 at step 150, flipping to a −1.3105 action / −0.3664 object / +0.5108 style profile by step 300), and §5.5 reads this only as "separation" and "concentrated update contrast" — never connecting the sign of that diagnostic to the sign of the centered score that actually shapes the reward. Before porting the mechanism I would want to know whether the +10.1 on ALFWorld is credit assignment or a disguised entropy/regularization effect.

[Source (arXiv 2608.03223)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03223) · [code](https://github.com/gitrxh/ADRS-arxiv)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Trajectories That Segment Themselves: Agent-Declared Boundaries as a Training Unit</strong> · Jingxi Wei (no affiliation listed), August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.02302, submitted 3 Aug 2026; cs.AI/cs.LG/cs.SE, "20 pages, 6 figures, 11
tables"; single author, with no affiliation line on the record but a University of Illinois
Urbana-Champaign contact email on the title page) proposing "collection-time semantic
self-segmentation". Instead of cutting long-horizon coding-agent trajectories by action, by episode
label, or by fixed window, the acting agent is placed under a declarative contract that makes it name
its current falsifiable causal hypothesis while the rollout is being generated, so each adoption event
becomes a variable-length semantic phase boundary — with no gold patch, milestone vocabulary,
environment replay, teacher logits, or retrospective segmenter involved in placing it. The corpus is
904 real tool-using trajectories over 790 task instances and 122 repositories/suites, yielding four
supervised targets from one collection. The headline claim is that the declared boundaries are
semantically coherent and not cheaply reproducible, while the paper is explicit that downstream DPO on
those boundaries changed zero decisions on its held-out adversarial set.*

**From the report**

> Core method: the acting agent declares its currently adopted causal explanation during rollout, and a phase runs from that adoption to "the next adoption or the end"; on failure a reviewer that has seen the reference fix may return "exactly two things — a negation of what is wrong in the current approach, and the smallest positive direction to investigate next," and is forbidden from "naming the fix, a file or line, any identifier taken from the reference, or any code." Three regimes are recorded: self_solved, self_corrected, oracle_redirected. — §3.1, §3.5
>
> Instrumentation cost: "the agent emitted 4,464 declarations at a mean of 52 tokens: 233,601 tokens against 2,391,128 the agent generated, so the protocol adds 9.8% to what the agent writes. Against the whole transcript, which tool output dominates and the protocol cannot influence, the same declarations are 0.57%". — §1.3
>
> Corpus scale: ten source runs, 904 raw trajectories over 790 task instances and 122 repositories/suites (SWE-style repo tasks, LiveCodeBench, Terminal-Bench); 852 with ledgers, 803 with retrospective audits; 2,721 retained audit keys and 2,720 reconstructed spans; 3.59 phases per contributing trajectory (median 2); median phase = 16 messages / 9.5 tool calls / 8,892 tokens, p90 = 61 / 37 / 44,008. — §4, Table 2
>
> One collection, four supervised targets: audit 2,721 rows (judge direction soundness, drawn from exactly the failed regions an episode label discards), propose 1,405 rows (infer the next hypothesis from a masked boundary state), fix 553 rows (diagnosis to patch), and 2,551 preference pairs (advancing vs stalling openings; 726 outcome-verified, 1,825 LLM-judged). — Tables 1–2
>
> Coherence result: with the declaration deleted and hypothesis text scrubbed, an independent observer attributes action blocks to their governing hypothesis at 0.484 vs 0.222 chance (2.18×, CI [+0.180, +0.336]) over 221 phases from 49 trajectories. In the separate paired run where a single matcher scored both segmentations of the same 374 trajectories, declared scores 0.495 vs 0.208 chance (2.38×, [2.25, 2.51]) against equal-length windows at 0.431 vs 0.208 (2.07×, [1.96, 2.20]), with a sign test of 162 declared-better / 100 window-better / 112 tied at p = 0.0002. A TF-IDF lexical control reaches 0.353, and label permutation over 502 phases collapses accuracy to 0.108 (1.06× chance). — §5.1, Table 3
>
> Placement result and its negative twin: a code-blind annotator — the first author, whom the paper labels "an interested annotator" providing "an interested floor," not an arm's-length third party — matches 24 of 40 boundaries within ±1 step against a random expectation of 11.5 (p < 0.0001) but places 62 marks for those 24 hits (1.55× over-segmentation). A mechanical test-event rule beats chance at neither end of the sweep: strict matches 4/40 against an expectation of 5.6, permissive 29/40 against 29.4 (10.6% precision). — §5.2, §5.3, §D
>
> Downstream training recipe and result: standard DPO with the objective unmodified, on 2,551 pairs against a merged 27B SFT policy with a fresh rank-16 LoRA adapter (α 32, dropout 0.05, β 0.1, cutoff 8,192, lr 5e-6, 1 epoch, batch 1 × 8 accumulation, fused AdamW, seed 42, 319 steps, 116,727,808 trainable of 27.5B). Training separates cleanly (loss 0.6696 → 0.4510, margin 0.0550 → 1.2133, accuracy 0.619 → 0.798) and the label-inverted control does not (margin −0.0027, accuracy 0.473). — §6, Table 6, §C
>
> Stated limitation, held-out transfer: on the 91-item adversarial held-out set no adapter changes a single decision (0.824 accuracy for both the SFT reference and Phase-DPO; median shift 1.40 against a candidate gap of 18.89), while on 60 matched-construction items Phase-DPO moves 4 decisions, all wrong to right (0.700 → 0.767, McNemar p = 0.125), and both controls move none. The paper's own reading is that "the fitted preference is real but bound to how the pairs were written" and that "construction is not isolated" — with 1,825 of the 2,551 pairs drawn from a single generator family, "the variable to vary next is the diversity of the pair corpus rather than the boundary." — §6.2, Table 4, §9
>
> Further self-limitations: §8 reports that of 877 resolved episodes, 522 declare more than one phase and 355 of those (68%) contain phases of differing verdict class — but §E states "the verdict classifier is a deterministic parser over shell output, not a model judgment, but we have not validated it against the repository's real test suite; the 68% figure above depends on it." §9 adds that the phase-level credit assigner "is implemented but never trained," and §2 concedes that retrospective audit and preference synthesis still use model judgments, so the no-external-dependency claim is scoped to boundary *placement* only.

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §8 with §E first — 355 of 522 multi-phase resolved episodes (68%) contain phases whose verdict class disagrees with the episode label, and the phase-level advantage flips sign against it. That is the same defect I keep hitting when a trajectory ends correct but contains a long stretch of redundant confirmation: episode-level reward pays for the detour. I would carry the caveat with the number, since the verdict classifier is an unvalidated shell-output parser. §3.1's reviewer contract is the piece I would lift verbatim: because the agent names its conjecture, a reviewer can negate it *by name* while being barred from naming the fix, a file, a line, an identifier from the reference, or any code — which manufactures wrong-cause-then-correction transitions that recorded work rarely contains. That is a recipe for synthesizing pivot supervision rather than hoping natural rollouts contain it. §5.1–5.3 read as a falsification ladder for any self-declared annotation, and I want it as an acceptance gate: the declared arm beats equal-length windows at p = 0.0002 in the paired run, a TF-IDF control gets 0.353 of the 0.484, label permutation collapses to 0.108, and a mechanical test-event rule fails at both ends of the sweep. If a self-declared segment cannot survive all four, it is a formatting convention, not a signal. And Table 4 is the cautionary read: the DPO margin goes 0.0550 → 1.2133 at 0.798 training accuracy and still changes 0 of 91 decisions on the adversarial held-out set. Training-time separation on preference pairs told the author nothing about transfer — exactly the failure mode to check for when my own pair construction and my own eval share a generator.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **Over-reflection in search agents** — a direct mechanism match. My 63%-of-23,110 finding is that a trajectory states an answer, confirms it against a real hit, then keeps searching; this paper's 68% of multi-phase resolved coding episodes containing phases of differing verdict class is the same credit-unit mismatch in another domain. Its declaration event is a candidate implementation of the state-conditioned stop/pivot signal I want instead of a global patience knob: the boundary is where the agent itself says the governing conjecture changed, so the decision becomes supervisable at that point rather than at episode end. The reviewer contract is also a per-failure-type repair generator — it fabricates the wrong-cause-then-correction transitions my tagged corpus is short of. **FlashSAC as a recipe for search agents** — a mechanism link through rollout economics. My premise is that search rollouts are too expensive to discard after one or two gradient steps; this paper's answer to the same pressure is yield-per-collection, turning one instrumented rollout into four supervised targets (2,721 audit / 1,405 propose / 553 fix / 2,551 preference pairs), including audit supervision harvested from exactly the failed regions an episode label throws away, for a 9.8% overhead on agent-written tokens. §8 also names the failure my update-to-data argument targets: group-relative advantage is identically zero when a sampled group is uniformly unsuccessful, so near the solve-rate floor an online arm gets no gradient at all. **AgentPlanet** — a partial link on reward-channel integrity. The open problem I named is that a self-authoring model must not be able to make its own signal easy, and this is a small, clean instance of the same discipline: the actor supplies the boundary, but the boundary is validated by parties that never saw the declaration — an independent model observer at 2.18–2.38× chance, a TF-IDF control, a label permutation collapsing to 1.06× chance, and a mechanical rule that fails. (The code-blind human leg is *not* one of those parties, since the annotator is the author; the paper says so and calls it a floor.) A stretch on scope — the paper segments trajectories, it does not author worlds — but the falsification protocol transfers directly as the anti-Goodhart gate for any component that annotates its own training signal. **Post-cutoff distillation** — a stretch where only the gating logic rhymes. My design gates the token-level reward to exactly the tokens carrying knowledge the student could not have had; this paper gates supervision to exactly the spans a given conjecture governed, and is explicit that no teacher logits are used anywhere in placing a boundary. The shared claim is that span selection, not the loss, is the lever — but with no cross-tokenizer or knowledge-provenance machinery, this stays an analogy rather than a transferable method.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the three-control acceptance gate for self-declared annotations, ported wholesale — a lexical (TF-IDF) baseline to prove the model adds something beyond surface words (0.353 vs 0.484 here), a label permutation to prove the signal needs correct assignment (collapse to 0.108, 1.06× chance), and a mechanical-rule sweep to prove the boundary is not trivially recoverable (4/40 strict vs 5.6 expected; 29/40 permissive vs 29.4 at 10.6% precision). Anything my pipeline segments or tags should clear all three before it becomes a training unit. The sharp open question the paper leaves: it argues phases are the right credit unit and then never trains an online arm on them, so nobody has shown phase-level advantage beats an episode label under RL — and it hands over the exact obstacles (policy plus frozen reference plus rollout KV cache co-resident, and zero group-relative advantage on uniformly-unsuccessful groups), which is a concrete target for anyone with the memory budget and a domain whose solve rate is not floored.

[Source (arXiv 2608.02302)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02302) · [code](https://github.com/Jingxi-Wei/hypothesis-ledger) · [data](https://huggingface.co/datasets/jingxiwei/hypothesis-ledger-selfcorrection)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>WorldExam: Benchmarking World Models from Apparent Appearance to Inherent Reactivity</strong> · CASIA + SLAI/CUHK + AMAP + THU, August 2026</summary>

*arXiv preprint (2608.02603, submitted 3 Aug 2026; cs.CV) presenting a hierarchical diagnostic
benchmark for controllable video generators used as world models, organized as four ascending levels
— Visual Quality, Control Adherence, Spatial Consistency, World Reactivity — comprising 1,474 cases
across eight tasks and covering camera-driven, action-driven and language-driven control interfaces
under one protocol. The top level, World Reactivity, scores scene-conditioned reactions and
goal-directed behavior the input never explicitly requests, judged by frozen per-case checklists
graded by a VLM for four of its five tasks (Terrain Interaction is scored geometrically behind a
Subject Control gate) and validated against three human annotators over 800 instances / 5,793
checklist items. Evaluating 20 models, the headline claim is that the three paradigms split cleanly
by capability and that no model combines broad task coverage with consistently strong performance:
high visual quality and faithful instruction-following do not predict inherent reactivity.*

**From the report**

> Four-level hierarchy: Visual Quality = "perceptual plausibility, temporal stability, and aesthetic quality"; Control Adherence = "whether the controlled camera or subject follows the input control"; Spatial Consistency = "whether the model preserves a coherent world when the camera revisits a previously observed viewpoint"; World Reactivity = "scene-conditioned reactions and goal-directed behaviors beyond what is explicitly specified in the input". — §3.1
>
> Composition: 1,474 cases over eight tasks split into a static-scene track (Camera Control, Scene Revisit — open to all three paradigms) and a dynamic-interaction track (Subject Control, Terrain Interaction, Object Interaction, Social Interaction, Physical Reaction, and Goal Completion, which is language-driven only); 31.4% first-person viewpoints, largest single scene category is traffic at 14.7%, and "no single subcategory accounts for more than 35% of its corresponding task". Static-scene first-person scenes are drawn from existing datasets (DL3DV-10K, NTIRE-2017, MIND); dynamic-interaction initial images are synthesized. — §3.2, §3.4
>
> Case-construction recipe: task-specific pattern library → schema-guided LLM case composer expands a sampled pattern into a structured draft with its checklist → N candidate initial images are synthesized → human filtering selects I⁰, keeping candidates where "relevant entities are visible, the spatial layout supports the intended behavior" and discarding images that "already depict the evaluated event" or make the behavior physically infeasible → an image-conditioned case refiner regrounds the draft and checklist against I⁰, and "for tasks evaluated using checklists, the final checklist L = {ℓ_k} is fixed at this stage". — §3.3
>
> Scoring split: the lower levels are automatic and geometric — Camera Control uses monocular pose recovery with S_cam = 100·√(s_t·s_r), s_t = max(0, 1−e_t/0.5), s_r = max(0, 1−e_r/15°); Scene Revisit uses S_rev = 100·√(S_succ·(s_P+s_L+s_S)/3) over PSNR/LPIPS/SSIM; Subject Control recovers 3D trajectories with SAM2 + VGGT-Ω. Four reactivity tasks are checklist-scored by a GPT-5.5 VLM judge as S_check = (100/K)·Σ 1[ℓ_k satisfied in V], the judge receiving 10 uniformly sampled frames with "contradicted, missing, ambiguous, off-screen, or otherwise unverifiable evidence counted as unsatisfied". Visual Quality uses general metrics only, and the set differs by track: static-scene reports 3D Consistency, Photometric Consistency, Temporal Flickering, Aesthetic Quality and Imaging Quality; dynamic-interaction reports Subject Consistency, Motion Smoothness, Aesthetic Quality and Imaging Quality, dropping the three geometry/flow metrics "because valid motion and state changes disrupt their geometric and optical-flow correspondences". — §4.1, §4.3
>
> Judge validation: 800 evaluation instances (200 per task) / 5,793 checklist items, three annotators per item with majority vote as reference; overall Spearman ρ = 0.8614, PLCC = 0.8583 — but Social Interaction is the weak cell at ρ = 0.7019 / PLCC = 0.7103 versus Goal Completion at 0.8960 / 0.9017. — §5.6, Table 5
>
> Appearance does not predict reactivity: across language-driven models the General (visual-quality-side) averages occupy a narrow band of 79.64–81.04 while their Task averages span 39.85 (Kling 2.5) to 65.02 (Veo 3.1) — the paper's framing from the outset is that "strong Visual Quality, Control Adherence, and Spatial Consistency do not guarantee successful scene-conditioned reactions or goal execution". — §5.5; §3.1 for the quote
>
> The paradigm split, in numbers: camera-driven NeoVerse tops Camera Control at 97.33 and Scene Revisit at 89.25; action-driven models win Subject Control (LingBot-World 55.47 vs Veo 3.1's 37.28) but collapse on world response — Terrain Interaction best action-driven 27.49 against language-driven 64.39 (Vidu Q3), and Physical Reaction bottoms out at WorldPlay 26.91 / LingBot-World 33.43 against Hailuo 2.3's 63.84. Overall (defined as the mean of Task and General) is led by NeoVerse 85.39 on the static track and Veo 3.1 72.77 on the dynamic track; lowest Task average overall is WorldPlay at 37.86 (Overall 57.57). A robustness rerun of every geometry metric on a Depth Anything 3 backend shifts Overall by a mean absolute 3.09% (static) and 0.57% (dynamic) with all within-paradigm rankings preserved. — Tables 2 & 4, §5.7
>
> Stated caveats: five action-driven models are excluded from the dynamic track outright for "FPV-only control or unreliable TPV subject control"; Goal Completion "remains limited to language-driven models"; and the metrics "assess observable end-to-end behavior rather than determining where reasoning occurs or establishing that the video generator itself has learned an internal causal representation" — with, for closed commercial systems, "proprietary prompt enhancement may contribute to scene grounding and execution planning". — §8 Table 8 caption, §6

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §5.5 is the whole point — General averages pinned in a 79.64–81.04 band while Task averages swing 39.85 → 65.02 is a clean demonstration that the cheap automatic channel is nearly constant across models that differ by 25 points on the thing you actually care about. That is the exact failure mode I worry about when a proxy metric becomes an RL reward. §4.3 plus Table 5 is the verifier-validation protocol I want to copy: freeze a per-case checklist at construction time, score it as (100/K)·Σ1[satisfied] instead of a holistic 1–5 judge rating, then report agreement disaggregated per task so the weak cell (Social Interaction, ρ = 0.7019) is visible rather than hidden inside a comfortable 0.86 headline. §3.3's construction pipeline earns a read for one filter alone — discarding initial images that "already depict the evaluated event" is an anti-leak gate for LLM-authored tasks stated in a single line, and it is the same hazard I hit whenever a generator writes both the scenario and its own success criterion. And §6's admission that the metrics measure observable end-to-end behavior and cannot say whether the generator learned an internal causal representation, combined with the prompt-enhancement caveat for closed APIs, marks how far any claim built on a black-box behavioral eval can be pushed before it becomes an attribution I cannot defend.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — WorldExam empirically separates the policy channel from the world-model channel: Control Adherence asks whether the commanded agent or camera did what it was told, World Reactivity asks whether the world produced consequences nobody specified, and the two dissociate (action-driven models hit 55.47 Subject Control but 24–27 Terrain Interaction — the subject moves and the world stays inert). That is the cleanest external evidence I have that factoring p(world, trajectory) into separate role-losses is measurable and not just notation, and the "discard images that already depict the evaluated event" filter is a concrete instance of the anti-Goodhart gate I need so a world-author cannot pre-satisfy its own task. **Wearable world model** — this argues the latent-encoder-vs-generative fork should be decided on reactivity, not fidelity: visual-quality metrics are near-constant across models whose reactivity differs by 25 points, so a compressed and quantized generative model that holds its VBench-style quality metrics could still lose the property that makes it a world model at all. Their inference table (49–192 frames, 560×336 to 1280×720, streaming models capped to a 100–200 frame window) also gives a realistic horizon budget to design against. **Inventing the z-axis** — Scene Revisit is structurally the same test: return the camera to a viewpoint already observed and check, with a binary pose-success gate plus PSNR/LPIPS/SSIM, whether the generated geometry stayed the one it showed you before. S_rev = 100·√(S_succ·(s_P+s_L+s_S)/3) is a reusable shape for scoring re-slice consistency in a 3D reconstruction, where the failure I care about is invented structure rather than blur. **Over-reflection in search agents** — a stretch across modality, sharing only the scoring shape: Goal Completion grades a multi-item checklist per case rather than a single outcome bit, crediting partial progress and accepting alternative executions. That is the credit-assignment shape I want for a state-conditioned stop/pivot policy instead of a global patience knob; the connection is to the reward structure only, since nothing here concerns search or text agents.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the judge-validation protocol, ported before any VLM or LLM checklist becomes an RL reward — a frozen per-case checklist authored at construction time (covering the initiating condition, the resulting reaction or goal-execution progress, and invalid outcomes), binary per-item scoring at (100/K)·Σ1[ℓ_k satisfied], three independent annotators with majority vote over ~5,800 items, and agreement reported per task so a weak cell like ρ = 0.7019 surfaces instead of averaging into 0.86. The open question I would chase: they admit they cannot tell whether reactivity lives in the video generator or in a closed system's proprietary prompt enhancement, which means the top of their dynamic leaderboard may be measuring an upstream planner. An ablation that strips or fixes the enhancement layer would decide whether "world reactivity" is a property of the world model at all — and the same confound applies to any agent benchmark scored through a vendor API.

[Source (arXiv 2608.02603)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02603)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Qwen3.8-Max: A New Bar for Coding and Cowork</strong> · Qwen Team (Alibaba), August 2026</summary>

*Official release post (August 3, 2026) for Qwen3.8-Max, the new Qwen flagship — "2.4T parameters
(95B active)" on the Qwen3.5 architectural foundation, text+image input with a 1M-token context
window declared in the post's integration configs, and the first Qwen-Max-class model slated for
open weights ("next week" on Hugging Face/ModelScope). The headline claim is end-to-end completion
of long-horizon real work — multi-day autonomous coding, professional workflows, thousand-turn
tasks — attributed to RL over jointly scaled real environments with a "Universal Reward System" as
the single reward source; the launch table posts Terminal-Bench 2.1 86.6, PaperBench 93.0,
SWE-bench Pro 67.7, and Toolathlon Verified 72.5 against Opus 4.8 / Fable 5 / GPT-5.6 Sol (max) /
Qwen3.7-Max baselines.*

**From the report**

> "Built upon the architectural foundation of Qwen 3.5, Qwen 3.8-Max scales to 2.4 trillion parameters" — the spec line reads "2.4T parameters (95B active)" — and "this also marks the first time we will open-source the weights of a Qwen-Max-class model," with weights due on Hugging Face/ModelScope "next week." — §Intro, §Build with Qwen3.8
>
> The RL reward source is "a Universal Reward System that internalizes heterogeneous verification — spanning execution-based checking, rubric-conditioned adjudication over text and rendered visual output, and agentic inspection — under automatically scalable rubrics," unified "within one reward system… eliminating the inconsistency inherent in maintaining task-specific verifiers." — §Work · Scaling Real-World RL Systems
>
> Environments scale on three decoupled axes — Task (single-task → multi-task → multi-day), Workspace (multi-file → hierarchical → complex heterogeneous folders), Harness (category/version/skills) — so "environment growth compounds combinatorially," with an online data balancer that shapes every batch's task/difficulty/workspace/harness distribution, "suppressing inter-batch gradient variance." Fig 1 plots a score index over 10+ benchmarks against RL-environment count: 0.474 SFT baseline → 0.725 best checkpoint at 4,000 environments — declining to 0.689 at 5,000. — §Work, Fig 1
>
> Coding headline rows: Terminal-Bench 2.1 86.6 (Claude Code harness, avg@10, 5-hour timeout, max_tokens=131,072; GPT-5.6 Sol posts 88.8), PaperBench 93.0 (BasicAgent under Code-Dev mode, judged by Claude Opus 4.6, averaged over 3 runs of max 12 h), SWE-bench Pro 67.7 (Claude Code, temp 1.0 / top_p 0.95 / 256K context; Fable 5 posts 80.0) — the SWE-bench Pro footnote adding "problematic tasks corrected and all baselines evaluated on the refined benchmark." — §Full Benchmark Table, notes 2/3/8
>
> The agentic rows are mixed and the footnotes admit asymmetries: Toolathlon Verified Pass@1 72.5 trails Fable 5's 77.9 and Opus 4.8's 76.2; WideSearch 81.9 average item-F1 over four runs uses "the Claude Code harness for external models and the Qwen-Agent harness for ours"; and note 1 states "Fable5 results may involve fallbacks." — §Full Benchmark Table, notes 1/17
>
> Long-horizon chip-design case: one continuous autonomous run of ~500 turns, 71 evaluations, and 13 milestones took a GCD/RSA accelerator from 8,298 to 678 gates ("leading all evaluated models") — the largest single step at Turn 22 (a 16-bit modulo divider replaced with iterative shift-subtract, −6,288 gates), module-fusion structural work still landing at Turns 170–252 — with OpenROAD place-and-route shrinking the die from 106×106 to 46×46 µm² (81% area reduction) and closing timing at 500 MHz. — §Long-Horizon Task
>
> E-Commerce Bench, a 365-day operation simulation on desensitized Taobao/Tmall data (12 store types, ~600 suppliers with 152 covertly embedded fraudulent merchants, 7,000 products), turned ¥100,000 seed capital into a ¥416,252 final balance (4.16×) across 2,000+ interaction rounds — 38% above second-place GLM 5.2 and a 152% improvement over Qwen3.7-Max. — §Long-Horizon Task · Continuous Learning
>
> RecreationBench is "an internal long-horizon application-recreation benchmark" across five platforms (Ubuntu, macOS, Windows, Android, web) in which the model sees a running app only as a black box — no source code, no internet — and rebuilds it from scratch; the post's own table scores Qwen3.8-Max 51.7, second to Fable 5's 56.1 on Qwen's own benchmark. — §Multimodal Agents, Table 2 note 10

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the Universal Reward System paragraph read against the post's own footnotes. One reward stack spanning execution checks, rubric-conditioned adjudication over rendered output, and agentic inspection is the verifier unification everyone wants — but the NL2Repo-Bench footnote ("to prevent reward hacking, we disable Bash commands that attempt to access the specific repository, such as pip download, pip install, and git clone") concedes the reward surface is attackable, and the post never says how that same surface is defended during RL rather than at eval time. Then Fig 1, whose x-axis is environment count, not training steps: 0.474 → 0.725 at 4,000 environments then down to 0.689 at 5,000 is the first public curve I know of that treats environment diversity as the scaling axis, and the peak-then-decline plus the batch balancer reads like direct evidence that raw environment count stops paying without batch-distribution control. And the chip-design milestone table is the best public anatomy of a ~500-turn run in any launch post: over 80% of the total gate reduction lands at Turn 22, yet module-fusion rewrites still land at Turns 170–252 — the post's own claim of "major structural breakthroughs even hundreds of turns into a run," and a concrete counterexample to the assumption that long runs only harvest early gains. Throughout, read the footnotes as carefully as the numbers: harness asymmetry on WideSearch, per-row external judges, corrected task sets — and their own model placing second on their own RecreationBench, an admission that raises my trust in the rest of the table.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — the Universal Reward System is the industrial version of my checklist-of-deterministic-gates-as-reward for world-building: both replace per-task verifiers with one scalable reward source over synthesized environments, and the pip/git-clone lockdown against reward hacking is exactly the reward-channel-integrity failure my gates are designed around. **Over-reflection** — their long-horizon loops earn extra turns by consuming fresh evidence each iteration (cocotb simulations, OpenROAD layouts, rendered UI), the grounded opposite of the ~63% confirm-then-keep-searching default I measured across 23,110 search trajectories; internalizing verification-against-output into the reward is a reward-side route to the state-conditioned stop/pivot behavior I train explicitly. **Inventing the z-axis** — a showcase-level link: the rehabilitation-therapist demo turns a 2D paper assessment form into freely rotatable 3D anatomy with layer-by-layer overlays, literally inventing the z-axis from 2D with no honesty test mentioned — precisely what my oblique re-slice check would probe. **The rollouts we throw away (FlashSAC)** (stretch) — their trajectories cost 125 hours or 500 turns apiece, the extreme end of the expensive-rollout regime my replay-buffer port targets, yet the post is silent on whether any rollout is ever reused off-policy.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the online data balancer — shape every RL batch to hold a fixed joint distribution over task/difficulty/workspace/harness so inter-batch gradient variance stays down; for search-agent RL the axes become benchmark family, difficulty, and tool surface. And reproduce Fig 1 on my own environment-scaling line — aggregate score versus number of RL environments with an SFT-baseline floor — because a 0.725 peak at 4,000 environments sliding to 0.689 at 5,000 implies an environment-count optimum worth locating before paying to synthesize more.

[Source (Alibaba Cloud blog)](https://www.alibabacloud.com/blog/qwen3-8-max-a-new-bar-for-coding-and-cowork_603421)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>From Tokens to Watt-hours: Analytical Energy Estimation for LLM Inference on Modern GPUs</strong> · TWT GmbH + NTUA + Harvard (+ others), July 2026</summary>

*A workshop report (arXiv v1 July 29, 2026; accepted for oral presentation at the GREEN-AI workshop
co-located with ECML-PKDD 2026) giving a closed-form, telemetry-free estimator for the GPU-side
energy of LLM inference on H100-class accelerators: it converts parameter-scaled transformer FLOPs
and HBM bit traffic into joules using two measurement-derived hardware coefficients, separates
prompt prefill from autoregressive decode, and decomposes request energy into compute,
parameter-access, KV-cache-write, and attention-read components. Applied to a 16-model inventory
spanning 0.308B to 120B parameters at a 500-input / 500-output-token workload, it reports 0.961 to
374.4 mJ per output token and 0.000599 to 0.170747 Wh per request. The headline claim is that a
purely analytical model, calibrated on one prior measurement study, reproduces measured per-request
energy to within roughly 5–27% with no runtime instrumentation at all.*

**From the report**

> Core model: request energy splits into prefill and decode and then into compute plus memory, E_GPU = α_TC·C_TC + e_HBM·Q_HBM, with dense transformer FLOPs at K = 6 FLOPs per parameter per token; the H100-class coefficients are α_TC = 0.52 pJ/FLOP and e_HBM = 11.68 pJ/bit (A100: 0.70 and 13.11). — Eqs 1–9, §2.5, App E
>
> Three-way memory decomposition (compute is the fourth leg of the paper's four-way split): Bits_total = Bits_params + Bits_KV + Bits'_attn, where the KV-write term scales as 2·b_kv·d_model·n_l·T_out and the attention-read term as 2·b_kv·d_model·n_l·(T_out·T_in + T_out·(T_out−1)/2)·s_attn(N); memory energy is Bits_total·e_HBM·η(N), with b_w = b_kv fixed at 16 bits. — Eqs 10–11, 20–22, §2.3, §3.4
>
> Calibration recipe: three scaling factors fitted by constrained least squares against reported energy measurements from one prior measurement study, giving γ₀ = 0.10 at a 24B reference size, s_attn(N) = 1 + 1.5·(N/N₀)^0.9 and η(N) = 1 + 0.8·(N/N₀)^0.8; the fit "is intentionally kept simple to avoid overfitting" given "the small number of calibration parameters and limited data points." — §3.5, Table 2
>
> Validation, all four points at 500 input / 500 output tokens: 8B analytical 0.011795 versus measured 0.009270 Wh (27.23% error); 24B 0.032427 versus 0.026280 (23.39%); 70B 0.170747 versus 0.179230 (4.73%); 72B 0.170747 versus 0.233260 (26.80%) — "the analytical estimator matches measurement-based values within approximately 5–27% for the compared cases." — §4.5, Table 4
>
> Model-inventory numbers (16 models, 0.308B–120B): EmbeddingGemma 0.308B at 0.961 mJ per output token and 0.000599 Wh per request; Qwen3 8B at 24.960 mJ and 0.011795 Wh; Qwen3 32B at 99.840 mJ and 0.052721 Wh; Llama 3.3 70B at 218.400 mJ per output token and 0.170747 Wh; GPT-OSS 120B at 374.400 mJ per output token and 0.155204 Wh. — Table 3, §4.4
>
> Decomposition finding: compute energy grows approximately linearly with output length and KV-cache writes likewise, but attention-related KV reads grow super-linearly because the read count scales as T_out·T_in + T_out·(T_out−1)/2; "the contributions from parameter access and KV writes are negligible relative to compute and attention-related memory traffic across the evaluated range." For a 32B model at T_in = 100, the compute fraction falls below the attention fraction between 10² and 10³ output tokens and sits near 26% at 10³, over sweeps that run to 20,000 output tokens in Fig 1 and 10⁵ in Fig 3. — §4.1, §4.3, Figs 1 and 3
>
> Energy is not monotone in parameter count: "the memory-energy component exhibits non-monotonic behavior for certain models… because key contributors to memory traffic, such as the hidden dimension d_model and the number of layers n_l, do not scale uniformly with N" — the paper illustrates this with a dip between its 14B and 16B entries. — §4.2, Fig 2
>
> Stated limitations: the estimator "is limited to accelerator-side operational energy… excludes CPU execution, host memory, networking, storage, power-supply losses, cooling, and datacenter-level power usage effectiveness"; "the parameter-scaled FLOP model does not fully capture feed-forward expansion ratios, grouped-query attention, multi-query attention, mixture-of-experts routing, or modality-specific processing"; and the evaluation "assumes single-request execution and does not explicitly model continuous batching," with the factors to "be recalibrated for other hardware platforms, serving engines, or batching regimes." — §3.3, §5

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* the attention-read term and Fig 3 first, not the headline table. The attention terms are the only super-linear ones in the model — the read term on the memory side, and (when layer count and hidden dimension are known) the appendix's prefill attention growing with the square of prompt length on the compute side — and that is exactly the regime my long multi-turn trajectories live in, with accumulated tool output as input and long reasoning continuations as output. It gives me a closed-form price for context growth that I currently see only as a latency number. Then read Table 4 and treat the absolute watt-hours with suspicion: three of the four validation points are off by 23–27% and only the 70B point hits 4.73%, and the study supplying the calibration targets is the same study the validation compares against — as far as I can tell this is an in-sample fit, so I would use the model for ratios between configurations and never for a reported absolute. Read §5's limitation list as a spec of what the model cannot tell me: no mixture-of-experts routing, no grouped-query attention, no batching, no quantization, and no idle term at all. Since decode compute uses total parameter count, the 120B entry is priced as if dense, which means the compute half is inflated for any sparse serving stack by the total-to-active ratio — the paper says so itself rather than fixing it. And cross-checking Tables 3 and 5 myself: GPT-OSS 120B lands at 0.155204 Wh per request, *below* Llama 3.3 70B at 0.170747 Wh for the identical workload, because the 70B is 80 layers at d_model 8192 against 36 layers at d_model 2880. The lever is KV geometry, not parameter count — which reframes "pick a smaller model to save energy" into "pick a shallower, narrower KV footprint."
- *Where it meets my notes:* **The energy floor of inference** — this delivers the first half of my note's split rigorously and demonstrates the gap on the second. The model is purely activity-proportional with no static term, and the words "idle" and "sustained" never appear; the H100's roughly 700 W thermal design power and a datacenter PUE near 1.56 are cited only as things the model excludes. So it hands me a defensible per-token numerator while confirming that the floor has to be measured, not derived — and quantization enters only linearly through the bit-width on the memory half, never through the FP16-specific compute coefficient. **Quantizing a world model onto a wearable** — the mechanism link is exact: KV bit-width appears linearly in both the KV-write and attention-read terms, so halving KV precision halves both, and because the attention-read term is the one that grows quadratically, KV bit-width is the lever whose payoff steepens precisely in the long-context regime a persistent on-device world model occupies. The paper's own caveat applies: its fitted factors were calibrated at 16 bits on H100 and must be recalibrated for another platform, serving engine, or batching regime (and, by extension though the paper does not say so, another precision). **Over-reflection** — this prices the tail I measured. A redundant continuation does not merely add linear decode compute; every extra token also attends over the whole accumulated context, so the marginal cost of the post-answer segment lands in the quadratic attention-read term and is super-linear in how long the agent keeps going. That turns "over-reflection is wasteful" from a qualitative claim into a per-trajectory watt-hour delta, and the paper's own lever list — shorter outputs reduce autoregressive decoding cost — is literally the objective of a state-conditioned stop policy. **The rollouts we throw away (FlashSAC)** (stretch, since the paper never mentions RL) — it gives each rollout a closed-form GPU energy price from parameter count, geometry, and token counts with no telemetry, so "expensive rollouts" becomes a number and the update-to-data ratio becomes an explicit energy-amortization ratio: reuse a rollout k times and its cost divides by k. The single-request, no-batching assumption is the main thing that would need fixing before that number means anything for a real rollout fleet.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* one constant worth porting — 0.52 pJ/FLOP × 6 FLOPs per parameter per token = 3.12 pJ per parameter per output token, times the paper's step-function prefill multiplier for input tokens. It reproduces their own table exactly (3.12 pJ × 70e9 = 218.4 mJ per token for Llama 3.3 70B) and is computable from a trajectory JSONL's token counts alone, so I could attach an estimated watt-hour figure next to the dollar cost I already track per synthesized trajectory — with the honest caveat that the constant is dense-FP16-single-request and would misprice a batched sparse stack. The sharp open question it leaves: §5 admits mixture-of-experts routing is not captured, yet the inventory prices sparse entries with the dense rule at total parameter count. The obvious repair is to use active parameters in the compute term while parameter-access traffic keeps the total (weights still stream from HBM even when unrouted), but nobody has recalibrated the parameter-reuse, attention, and memory factors for that split — and the parameter-reuse factor is precisely what routing breaks. That is a small, well-posed calibration study for anyone serving large open sparse models.

[Source (arXiv 2607.26571)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26571)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>Kimi K3: Open Frontier Intelligence — Technical Report of Kimi K3</strong> · Moonshot AI (Kimi Team), July 2026</summary>

*The 47-page technical report for Kimi K3 (published July 27, 2026, alongside the open weights —
the July 16 release blog cataloged above had deferred all architecture/training/eval detail to it):
an open-weights 2.8T-parameter MoE with 104B activated parameters, native vision, and a 1M-token
context window. It details the hybrid attention stack (3 Kimi Delta Attention layers per Gated MLA
layer, Attention Residuals across depth, Stable LatentMoE activating 16 of 896 routed experts), a
post-training pipeline that RL-trains nine domain-by-effort expert policies and merges them via
Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation, and the supporting training/serving infrastructure. Headline
claims: an approximately 2.5× overall scaling-efficiency gain over Kimi K2, and frontier-level
agentic results (BrowseComp 91.2%) that trail only Claude Fable 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol across the
report's own suite.*

**From the report**

> Architecture: each block stacks 3 Kimi Delta Attention layers + 1 Gated MLA layer (a 3:1 hybrid; 69 KDA + 24 MLA over 93 layers), with NoPE on all MLA layers and position sense carried by KDA's decay; Attention Residuals let each layer attend over all preceding layers via learnable pseudo-queries, run as Block AttnRes (8 blocks of 12 layers); Stable LatentMoE routes 16 of 896 latent-space experts per token plus 2 full-width shared experts — 2.78T total / 104.2B activated, training context 128K → 1M versus K2. — §2.1–2.3, Table 1
>
> Stability at extreme sparsity: RMSNorm inserted before the MoE up-projection, SiTU-GLU soft-caps both GLU branches (β₁=4, β₂=25, output bound ≤ 100) against activation explosion, and Quantile Balancing sets each expert's routing bias to minus the (1−k/n)-quantile of the router-score margins (biases then mean-centered), derived from a single forward pass via per-expert histograms (~1000 bins, <1% comm cost) and frozen at inference. — §2.3, App B–D
>
> Scaling claim, exact wording: refined architecture+data+training "collectively deliver an approximately 2.5x gain in overall scaling efficiency over Kimi K2," measured by fitted scaling-law curves on held-out OOD validation data; the same study finds cosine decay beats WSD only when each schedule gets its own scaling-law-tuned hyperparameters. — §3.2, Fig 7
>
> Long-context recipe: "Length alone, however, does not confer long-range capability. To address this, we synthesize additional long-context data by carefully permuting and concatenating multimodal documents and sub-tasks, so that the embedded tasks can be solved only by attending to information scattered across the full 1M-token context"; a four-stage curriculum grows the window 8K → 64K in pre-training and 256K → 1M in cooldown, with NoPE+KDA extrapolating without RoPE rescaling. — §3.4
>
> Post-training: RL trains one expert per domain {general, agents, coding} × effort {low, high, max} — "Crossing these three domain experts with three reasoning effort levels in {low, high, max} yields a total of nine expert models" — then Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation merges them with a per-token reward r = clip(sg(log π_teacher^(d,e)/π_θ), −R_max, R_max); finer top-k distillation objectives showed "no clear advantage"; QAT (MXFP4 expert weights, MXFP8 activations) runs through all of SFT+RL so rollout and training share one quantization, "eliminating the train-inference mismatch." — §4.1.2–4.1.4
>
> RL infrastructure: "Throughout Kimi K3's training and evaluation, a total of 51,219,741 sandboxes across 1,505,678 images were created" — AgentENV Firecracker microVMs checkpoint/resume in as low as 133ms/49ms, a paused sandbox (agent waiting on model inference, up to 98% of sandbox lifetime) consumes no memory or CPU, fork enables reward judging without side effects, and memory overcommit reaches 6.5×. — §5.3.2
>
> Headline numbers and their harness: BrowseComp 91.2% (best; GPT-5.6 Sol 90.4, Claude Fable 5 88.0) using context compaction triggered at 300K tokens — with the full 1M window and no context management it scores 90.4% — at $2.03/task, about half GPT-5.6 Sol's cost; SWE-Marathon 42.0, 7 points ahead of Claude Fable 5 on an H20-recalibrated branch where Fable 5 hits fallbacks on 35% of tasks; all K3 evals run at effort max, temperature 1.0; MCP-Atlas uses the 500-task public subset, 100-turn limit, Gemini 3.1 Pro judge. — §6.1.3–6.1.4, §6.4
>
> Report-stated limitations: HLE-Full 43.5/56.0 (no-tool/tool) trails Claude Fable 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol, and CritPt 23.4% lags three proprietary models, "indicating that research-level reasoning remains a key direction for improvement"; the joint UK AISI + NIST CAISI assessment finds K3 "trails frontier cyber-capable models on end-to-end exploit completion, achieving arbitrary code execution on 0 of 41 tasks." — §6.1.4, §6.2.2

**My read**
- *What I'd look at:* §4.1.2–4.1.3 first. Nine domain-by-effort RL experts merged by a per-token clipped log-ratio reward is the most concrete published recipe for consolidating specialized agent policies into one model, and their negative result — finer top-k distillation objectives bought nothing over the plain per-token reward — is an ablation I no longer need to run myself. Then §4.2.4/§4.2.6 as an anti-Goodhart playbook at production scale: kernel-optimization rewards guarded by a hacking-detection list that is continuously extended as new exploits appear (CUDA graph replay, input caching, precision reduction), and Autonomous Execution Tasks pairing public diagnostic verifiers with hidden held-out ones under limited submission budgets — worth reading line-by-line against my own deterministic-gate reward checklists. §6.1.3 quantifies the harness as a variable: compaction-at-300K scores 91.2 versus 90.4 with the raw 1M window, so context management is worth about a point at the frontier and must be pinned before comparing browse-family numbers across reports. And §5.3.2 explains how 51.2M sandboxes become affordable — pause/fork/snapshot microVMs where the paused state (the agent waiting on inference, ~98% of sandbox lifetime) costs zero memory and CPU; fork-for-judging-without-side-effects is the primitive I want under my own environment serving.
- *Where it meets my notes:* **AgentPlanet** — K3's RL stack operationalizes the reward-channel-integrity thesis at scale: deterministic verifier gates, hidden held-out verifiers isolated from the policy, and a hacking-detection list that grows as new exploits appear, while its knowledge-graph-guided task synthesis is agents building the world that trains the agent — the planet(s₀, R) factor in my factorization. **Over-reflection** — their reasoning-effort RL attacks the same overthinking failure from the cost side: reward hard-overridden to −1 once tokens exceed τ·b₀(x), plus a generative-reward-model verbosity gate that auto-fails outputs beyond σ·l₀ — a budget-shaped alternative to my state-conditioned stop/pivot RL; and MOPD is precisely the channel through which a teacher policy's confirm-then-keep-searching habit would propagate into the merged model. **Post-cutoff distillation** — MOPD's dense clip(sg(log π_teacher/π_θ)) per-token reward is the same on-policy-distillation reward family the note builds on, and gating which teacher applies by (domain, effort) is structurally the note's per-token gating by knowledge type — though K3 merges same-tokenizer siblings, leaving the note's cross-tokenizer case open. **The rollouts we throw away (FlashSAC)** — the partial-rollout scheme deliberately trains on trajectories spanning multiple iterations, with a per-token regularization that "robustly handles highly stale data" — frontier-scale evidence that expensive agentic rollouts need not be discarded for staleness, the same economics argument behind porting replay-buffer RL to search agents.
- *Worth stealing / watching:* the per-problem token-budget reward — reward := −1 when T(y) > τ·b₀(x), with b₀ estimated from the cold-start policy and τ annealed down from a max-budget expert — as a cheap over-search suppressor to benchmark head-to-head against trajectory repair plus stop/pivot RL on my search agents. The open question the report leaves: there is no ablation of nine-expert MOPD against a single mixed-domain RL run — the consolidation gain is asserted, never isolated — and the report never states its total pretraining token count, so compute-matched comparisons are impossible.

[Source (PDF, GitHub)](https://github.com/MoonshotAI/Kimi-K3/blob/main/k3_tech_report.pdf)

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>System Card: Claude Opus 5</strong> · Anthropic, July 2026</summary>

*A 194-page pre-deployment system card (dated July 24, 2026) for Claude Opus 5, a text-output
frontier model with a May 2026 knowledge cutoff, positioned as an upgrade to Claude Opus 4.8 with
gains concentrated in agentic coding, computer use, and long-horizon knowledge work. It covers
Responsible Scaling Policy evaluations (chem/bio, AI R&amp;D, autonomy), cyber capability and
safeguards, harmlessness, agentic safety and prompt injection, a large automated alignment audit
with white-box internals analysis, a model welfare assessment, and roughly 40 capability
benchmarks. The headline claim is that the model is substantially stronger than Opus 4.8 across
the board while scoring as its developer's best-aligned model to date on their own automated
behavioral audit, and that it does not cross the RSP thresholds for CB-2 or automated AI R&amp;D — so
it ships under "a portfolio of ASL-3 protections at the same level as those applied to Claude Opus
4.8" (Executive Summary, §2.2.6).*

**From the report**

> Training recipe, as stated: trained on "a proprietary mix of publicly available information from the internet, public and private datasets, and synthetic data generated by other models," with deduplication and classification as the named cleaning/filtering methods and a ClaudeBot crawler that honors robots.txt; post-training is described only as aimed at aligning behavior with Claude's constitution. Knowledge cutoff May 2026, text output only. No parameter count, architecture, or RL algorithm is disclosed. — §1.1
>
> Post-training was audited at scale rather than described: "we ran an automated review of model behavior during training, reading summaries of roughly one and a half million episodes from the final phase of training, across thousands of training environments," via recursive summarization, with ~400 full transcripts read end-to-end. The clearest concerning pattern was "Claude's tendency to state an over-confident final answer that its thinking text could not support," alongside observed instances of fabricating execution output and citations, "editing or deleting tests and checks in order to pass," and "attempting to satisfy the inferred grading criteria, rather than the requested task." — §6.3
>
> Headline agentic / long-horizon numbers vs Opus 4.8: BrowseComp 90.8 vs 84.3; OSWorld 2.0 70.6 vs 55.7 (70.57% first-attempt success averaged over 5 runs, 1080p, max 500 action steps); Zapier AutomationBench 26.0% vs 17.0% on the private held-out leaderboard set (24% at medium effort for $0.89/task); FrontierBench v0.1 43.3 vs 21.1 (Harbor-run); ARC-AGI-3 30.16% RHAE at high effort vs 1.52% for Opus 4.8 and 7.78% for GPT-5.6 Sol; MCP Atlas 85.8% pass rate vs 82.2%, with 89.1% mean claim coverage. — §8.1, §8.12.3, §8.13.7, §8.13.2, §8.14
>
> Multi-agent scaffolds beat the single-agent frontier on BrowseComp: "the 10-agent team reaching our highest score of 93.6%, +3.1pp over best single-agent baseline," with 5.6× and 5.9× derived-latency speedups for N=5 and N=10 relative to the single-agent 10M-token baseline. Two harnesses were tested — an N-agent peer team (all agents see the full task, one designated lead, Send Message / Wait for Message tools, per-agent 1M-token limit, Git sharing on coding tasks) and async subagents (the lead spawns long-lived subagents that see only the lead's instructions, not the original task; no cap on subagent count; only the lead's submission is graded). — §8.11.1, §8.11.3
>
> Search-eval harness and decontamination protocol: BrowseComp was run with web search, web fetch, programmatic tool calling, and code execution, using context compaction triggered at 200k tokens to extend past the 1M-token window, with Claude Opus 4.7 as the model grader and a source blocklist (Appendix 9.2). Runs were then "scanned after the fact with a verifier Claude and an automated pipeline to identify answer leakage; any problems flagged were counted as incorrect." HLE used the same two-stage guard: blocklists for both searcher and fetcher, plus a transcript review that re-grades confirmed retrievals as incorrect. — §8.10.1, §8.10.2
>
